# Changelog

All notable changes to the V.E.L.O.C.I.T.Y.-MCP server are documented in this file.

The format is based on [Keep a Changelog](https://keepachangelog.com/en/1.1.0/),
and this project adheres to [Semantic Versioning](https://semver.org/spec/v2.0.0.html).

---

## [Unreleased]

### Added

- **NDA protocol reference** (`docs/NDA_PROTOCOL.md`): the binary format written down for reviewers — NMCP frame layout (magic + SHA-256 root + payload, 36-byte header), method codes, the TLV tag table and parser limits, a byte-by-byte worked example (106 B request vs 113 B JSON-RPC), the three carriers (HTTP/stdio/shmem), the Merkle toggle with its measured cost, the `tools/call` dispatch decision tree, and the measured per-tool matrix. Every figure traces to code or to a run — 2026-10-02, re-measured against the fixed guests on 2026-10-09 (§9.3).
- **`GET /v1/nda/capabilities`**: per-WASM-tool report of whether a binary (TLV) executor is expected — `{protocol, binary_executors, json_fallback, tools: [{name, language, binaryExecutor}]}`. The report now has exactly one source of falsity left, and the probe below is what catches it. Measured on a cold server 2026-10-09: 22 WASM tools → 22 binary, 0 fallback; see the six-guest fix under *Fixed*. The dashboard fetches it before loading tools, tags fallback tools `·json`, and skips the NDA leg of side-by-side mode for them, because timing the same JSON path twice measures nothing. `docs/API.md` now carries the payload contract a client author needs — the measured response shape (a fresh `--features http` process: 22 entries, 22 binary / 0 fallback), the mixed key case (snake_case top level, camelCase `binaryExecutor` per tool), the WASM-only filter (the *same* process answers 40 tools on `tools/list` and 22 entries here), registration order rather than sorted order, that the two counts are recomputed per request so they always agree with the array, and that a `false` records only that the fall-back classifier fired — the reason lives in the server log, not the payload.
- **Live dashboard** (`dashboard/`): zero-dependency browser console (single HTML file, no build step) that visually demonstrates the HTTP server — `/health`, polled `/v1/performance` metric cards + instantaneous req/s chart, all 40 tools with schema-derived argument prefill, measured `tools/call` round-trips, a 50-call ping burst with avg/p50/p95, and an SSE event feed. A **JSON-RPC vs NDA-binary transport toggle** (default: side-by-side) runs every call through both `/v1/mcp` and `/v1/mcp/nda` with a JS TLV codec ported from `client/src/transport/nda_codec.rs`, showing latency, wire byte counts, payload equality and in-browser SHA-256 Merkle-root verification. `?demo=1` runs the full flow hands-free; three headless suites cover it: `test_dashboard.sh` (14 DOM assertions incl. the unreachable-server error path), `test_cdp.mjs` (5 real-clock checks: SSE feed + latency distribution), `test_interactive.mjs` (37 UI-driven checks: tool calls across native/QuickJS/MicroPython/Lua/Go/Rust runtimes, schema prefill, invalid input, NDA binary transport incl. the capability gate, API-key auth against a second server instance, double-connect idempotency, rate-limiter bursts). Re-run 2026-10-09 after the two harness fixes below: `test_cdp.mjs` **5/5 pass** (first run since 2026-09-18; avg 1.6 ms / p50 1.5 ms / p95 2.6 ms over 50 pings against the live server on :3900), `test_dashboard.sh` **14/14**, `probe_nda.mjs` **6/6**. `test_interactive.mjs` is **37 passed / 0 failed, exit 0** on 2026-10-09, measured twice from two different launch directories. It grew from 33 to 37 when the capability-gate block was rewritten: the skipped-leg checks could no longer be reached by selecting `perl_greet` (that guest now answers NDA calls), so the suite keeps one block that selects it as the running server lists it and adds a second that drives the page's own `noBinary` set, which is pinned even on a cold 22/0 server. The suite spawns its own API-keyed server from `target/release/velocity_mcp.exe`, so the numbers describe that artifact — which still predates the fixed guests and advertises 16 binary / 6 fallback — while a debug build of current source advertises 22 / 0 (see *Fixed*, cold-vs-warm capability wording). The release relink needs the demo server's file lock released and is an operator decision, not a test gap. Round-trips are measured with browser Resource Timing (`responseEnd − startTime`) rather than await wall-time, which Windows event-loop timer granularity inflates to a flat ~10 ms. Guide: `docs/DASHBOARD.md`.
- **WASIX HTTP server activation for Edge**: Edge deployment now uses WASIX socket-based HTTP (hyper server) instead of WASI CGI, enabling the full hardened HTTP stack (auth, rate limiting, CORS, body limits, error sanitization) on Wasmer Edge. The cfg gates in `velocity-mcp-edge/src/main.rs` changed from `#[cfg(not(target_arch = "wasm32"))]` to `#[cfg(any(not(target_arch = "wasm32"), target_vendor = "wasmer"))]`, activating the native HTTP server for WASIX targets while preserving plain WASI CGI builds for non-WASIX wasm32 targets.
- **Multi-threaded Edge runtime**: `deploy/build-edge.py` now compiles the WASIX module with shared linear memory (`-C target-feature=+atomics,+bulk-memory,+mutable-globals,+reference-types,+multivalue`), so tokio's `rt-multi-thread` spawns a real worker pool instead of running on one thread. Measured on the live Edge deployment: `available_parallelism()` returns 2 (Edge caps an instance at 2 CPUs; native host has 12), and 24–30 concurrent MCP requests all succeed. Note: this shared-memory binary cannot be exercised with the local `wasmer` 7.4.1 CLI (it fails at socket bind with `ENOTSUP`); only the newer Edge runtime serves it, so local `wasmer run` is not a valid smoke test for the multi-threaded build.
- **Isolated WASIX build helper** (`deploy/build-edge.py`): Stages current sources in a temporary directory outside the repository, uses the WASIX cargo registry (sparse+https://cargo-registry.wasix.org/), and builds with deterministic resolution via `deploy/edge-wasix.lock`. Keeps root `Cargo.lock` and `.cargo/config.toml` untouched. Requires WASIX toolchain (`rustc +wasix`), `cargo-wasix`, and Python 3.
- **`VELOCITY_WASM_INSTRUCTION_LIMIT` env var**: Overrides `wasm_runtimes.instruction_limit` at startup (`0` disables metering — not recommended for production).
- **Metering & caching benchmark harness** (`cargo bench --bench metering_cache_bench`): measures module-compile vs cached-deserialize cost and per-call metering overhead through the real dispatch path. Measured (Windows, Wasmer/Cranelift, 10M instruction limit): module-cache cold start 235 ms → 15.3 ms (**15.4x** on the Lua cache path); metering roughly doubles compile time (+117%) and per-call latency on minimal tools (+145% QuickJS, +158% Lua through `WasmRuntimeRegistry::call_tool`), diluted to **~+19%** end-to-end through the full JSON-RPC stdio stack.
- **HTTP server emits `notifications/tools/list_changed`**: `src/transport/http.rs` gains `watch_registry_changes(state, shutdown)`, spawned from `run_http_server` alongside the session-cleanup task. It samples `crate::registry::registry_generation()` once a second — the same counter the `tools/list` TLV cache invalidates against — and broadcasts a `notification` SSE frame carrying `{"jsonrpc":"2.0","method":"notifications/tools/list_changed"}` whenever the generation moves, which is what makes the advertised `tools.listChanged` flag mean something. Before this round **no code path emitted any of the five change notifications the initialize response declared**. Measured end-to-end on a debug `--features http,database,oauth2` server bound to 127.0.0.1:3988 (2026-10-10): a raw socket subscribed to `GET /v1/sse`, then `convert_to_nda_tool` ran over `POST /v1/mcp`; `tools/list` went **40 to 41** and exactly **one** `list_changed` frame reached the subscription inside the poll window (`raw.count(b"list_changed") == 1`). Unit coverage is `transport::http::tests::test_registry_change_broadcasts_list_changed`, which re-bumps the generation until the watcher samples a post-baseline value, so it cannot lose the race against an unrelated test's bump. **Fan-out scope, read off the two `event_broadcast` registration sites: `/v1/sse` and the streamable-HTTP POST session only.** stdio and shared memory cannot receive it at all (`run_stdio` is a read-dispatch-print loop with no queue for server-initiated messages, and the shmem buffer is request/response by layout), and the WebSocket handler never registers a sender — so `listChanged: true` is honest for the two HTTP/SSE carriers and still overstated for the other three. The 1 s poll also bounds staleness: a registration is announced within one second, not instantly.

### Changed

- **`lru` 0.12 → 0.18, clearing both remaining `unsound` advisories — with the exploitability stated narrowly rather than claimed as a vulnerability fix**: `Cargo.toml:84` moved from `lru = { version = "0.12", optional = true }` to `"0.18"`, and `Cargo.lock` resolves **0.18.5**. `cargo audit` goes from 5 allowed warnings to **3** (exit 0 either way: CI's step is plain `cargo audit` with no `--deny` flag, and both lru advisories are `informational = "unsound"`, which cargo audit does not fail on — so this bump is hygiene, not a CI unblock). The two advisories and their patched ranges, read from the local advisory database rather than recalled: **RUSTSEC-2026-0253** (patched `>= 0.18.2`) and **RUSTSEC-2026-0002** (patched `>= 0.16.3`). Both were reachable in principle in this build configuration because `lru` is pulled in by the `http` feature (`Cargo.toml:46`), so a default-feature build does not compile it at all while the HTTP server — the shipping configuration — does. On reachability the two differ, and the difference matters for how much this fix buys:
  - RUSTSEC-2026-0253 is about `LruCache::pop()`, and we call it: `src/middleware.rs:77` and `:121` pop an expired key out of `LruCache<String, CacheEntry>` (`:35`) on the response-cache path. Reading both vendored bodies side by side shows the defect exactly — 0.12.5 (`lru-0.12.5/src/lib.rs:1109`) does `*Box::from_raw(old_node.as_ptr())` and `ptr::drop_in_place(old_node.key.as_mut_ptr())` **before** `self.detach(&mut old_node)`, so a panic in the key's `Drop` unwinds past the detach and leaves the freed node's `prev`/`next` in the list for a later eviction to dereference; 0.18.5 (`lru-0.18.5/src/lib.rs:1292`) calls `self.detach(node_ptr)` first, and its in-code comment states the panic-safety reason. **The honest scope: the UB cannot actually be triggered by this codebase's key type.** Both of our caches are keyed on `String`, whose `Drop` cannot panic, and the advisory itself conditions on "key types that have potentially-panicking `Drop` implementations". So this is defence-in-depth against a future key change, not a live memory-safety hole.
  - RUSTSEC-2026-0002 concerns `IterMut::next`/`next_back` violating Stacked Borrows. Our only iterator call is the **immutable** `.iter()` at `src/middleware.rs:115`; there is no `iter_mut` call in the crate, so that advisory's affected method was never reached. The bump silences the warning; it did not remove a reachable defect.
  - Our full `lru` surface is `get`, `put`, `pop`, `peek`, `clear`, `len` and `iter`, across the response cache (`middleware.rs`) and the peer rate-limiter buckets (`src/rate_limit.rs:189`, where `PEER_LIMITERS` is declared). That pointer read `:174` until this round; `:174` is now a doc-comment line inside the test-only refill helper, and the distance is the fifteen lines the same uncommitted work inserted above the declaration — a drift the citation gate could not report, because a *different* anchor on this line matched its own target and the checker's token set is the union of every backtick span on the line. Re-measured with per-anchor pairing (`shadow_pair2.py`, in `%LOCALAPPDATA%\Temp`): pairing flags 33 citations the union passes and clears 0, 32 of the 33 turn out to be correct anchors whose referent simply sits outside the preceding text slice, and the single real one is this `:174`. So pairing is the worse instrument and the shipped checker is left alone; the honest consequence is that neither token heuristic can find drift, which is why the repair above was made by reading the source line, not by trusting either count. A third instrument was built and rejected in the next pass on the same kind of evidence: an enclosing-declaration checker (`declcheck.py`, same temp directory) that harvests only identifiers the target file really declares and asks whether the cited line sits inside that symbol's own block. Over the 133 symbol-bearing citations it reported 61 clean and 72 flagged, and reading the eight most specific flags found no new drift at all: six were the instrument's own gaps (a `static` declaration line whose regex it fails to parse, prose words that happen to be declared somewhere in the same file, pointers that legitimately land mid-function), one was a correct-as-written annotation of a past test run, and one was already disclosed by the entry it belongs to, which closes by restating its own current numbers. Same root cause as the other two — tokens are harvested from a whole sentence, so a pointer inherits every symbol the prose happened to mention. The proof that the published tally is not a correctness signal is that it did not move: this correction was measured with the same run shape before and after it, and both read **264 checked / 179 populated / 0 hard / 77 advisory / 8 allowed, exit 0** — the gate passed the wrong line and passes the right one, because both satisfy a union built from elsewhere on the sentence. `cargo +stable test --features http --lib -- rate_limit middleware`: **22 passed, 0 failed** on 0.18.5, so no API break reached the tests.
- **HTTP rate limiting is per peer, not one shared bucket**: `src/rate_limit.rs` keeps the process-wide `check_rate_limit` for the single-peer transports (stdio, shared memory) and adds `check_rate_limit_for_peer`, backed by an LRU-bounded map of one bucket per client IP (`MAX_PEER_BUCKETS` = 4 096, `tracked_peers()` for diagnostics). The HTTP middleware takes the peer from axum's `ConnectInfo` and falls back to one shared `unknown` bucket for in-process router calls that carry no connection info (`src/transport/http.rs:345-372`). Before this, one client flooding the server drained the allowance every other client shared. The env vars now configure both limiter kinds through one `configured_limits()` helper, so `VELOCITY_RATE_LIMIT` / `VELOCITY_RATE_BURST` apply per bucket instead of only to the global instance. Verified with `cargo +stable test --features http rate_limit`: **10 passed, 0 failed**, including `test_peer_buckets_are_independent`, `test_peer_buckets_are_tracked`, `test_peer_bucket_respects_configured_burst` and the route-level `test_rate_limit_is_per_peer`. `docs/USER_GUIDE.md` said "a single global token-bucket limiter … shared across all clients" in three places (feature table, env-var table, security section) — all three now describe the two-limiter split.
- **Module cache key includes the instruction limit**: metering is baked into the compiled module at compile time, so the cache key now hashes the limit alongside the bytecode. Previously a module compiled under one limit could be deserialized for a request using a different limit, silently misapplying (or dropping) metering.
- **wasmer.toml updated for WASIX HTTP**: Changed from WASI CGI runner to WASI socket-based runner with `PORT=80` environment variable. The `[[command]]` section now specifies `runner = "https://webc.org/runner/wasi"` with `[command.annotations.wasi]` containing `env = ["PORT=80"]`.
- **Deploy scripts require WASIX toolchain**: `deploy-edge.sh` and `deploy-edge.bat` now check for WASIX prerequisites (`rustc +wasix`, `cargo-wasix`, `python`) instead of standard `rustup target add wasm32-wasip1`. Build command changed from `cargo build` to `python deploy/build-edge.py`. Test command scoped to `-p velocity-mcp-core -p velocity-mcp-edge` to avoid building native-only crates.
- **OpenTelemetry stack migrated to 0.33** (`observability` feature): `opentelemetry` 0.22 → 0.33, `opentelemetry_sdk` 0.22 → 0.33, `opentelemetry-otlp` 0.15 → 0.33, `tracing-opentelemetry` 0.23 → 0.34. `init_observability` now builds the pipeline from `SpanExporter::builder().with_tonic()` + `SdkTracerProvider::builder()`, and `ObservabilityGuard` owns the provider and calls `provider.shutdown()` on drop (`global::shutdown_tracer_provider()` was removed upstream). This is what lifted `tonic` 0.11 → 0.14.6 and `hyper` 0.14 → 1.11 out of the lockfile. Behaviour is unchanged: spans only, one OTLP/gRPC exporter, `EnvFilter` from the environment.
- **Workspace formatted with stable rustfmt**: CI's `Check formatting` step had been failing on `main` because HEAD was not rustfmt-1.9.0-stable conforming; `cargo +stable fmt --all --check` now exits **0** (re-measured 2026-10-09). 32 `.rs` files differ from HEAD in the working tree; a token-stream comparison (HEAD and working file each stripped of all whitespace, then diffed for regions containing letters or digits) separates the two kinds of change: **6 are reflow only** — `benches/tool_compatibility_test.rs` (+1311/−258, whose whole diff is one-line `tc(…)` calls split across lines plus rustfmt's trailing commas, with zero letter-bearing differences), `benches/metering_cache_bench.rs`, `crates/velocity-mcp-core/src/lib.rs`, `crates/velocity-mcp-edge/src/tools.rs`, `src/plugins/marketplace.rs` and `src/wasm_runtime/lua.rs` — and the other 26 carry content changes attributed to the entries in this section. The earlier count of "18 files, 7 untouched by any working-tree edit" described the tree at the moment of the fmt pass and is superseded by these re-measured figures.
- **`client/` upgraded to reqwest 0.12** (from 0.11) on both the native and `wasm32` target declarations. Only four reqwest call sites exist in the crate (`From<reqwest::Error>`, the `Client` field, its constructor) and all are API-stable across the bump. This removed the last `h2` 0.3.x from the lockfile.
- **`bench_nda/` raw captures added to `.gitignore`** (`bench_nda/*.txt`, `bench_nda/audit_*.json` — lines 62–63, confirmed with `git check-ignore -v` on a hypothetical `bench_nda/newrun.txt` and `bench_nda/audit_probe.json`, both matching). The directory previously tracked 8 files, 5 of which are raw run output (`audit_json_http.json`, `audit_nda_http.json`, `bench_full_run.txt`, `bench_test_out.txt`, `nda_bench_out.txt`), which contradicts the repo's own standing rule that benchmark captures stay untracked and the pre-existing `# Benchmark output files` block (lines 52–56). Nothing depends on them: re-measured on 2026-10-09 with a path-field exclusion, a grep for those five filenames over every `*.md`, `*.rs`, `*.mjs`, `*.sh` and `*.js` in the tree returns **7** matches and **0 of them outside this file** — all seven occurrences sit on the two lines of this entry itself, the bullet and its *What this change deliberately does not do* sub-bullet, which mention the filenames in order to say nobody uses them. Re-measured 2026-10-11 with `grep -oF` over the same extension set: 2 + 2 + 1 + 1 + 1 across the five names, and plain `grep -c` on that sweep reports **2** — the 7 counts occurrences, the 2 counts lines, and which metric a "matches" figure carries has to be stated or the next reader re-derives a different number. (The pointer used to read this pair by line number; this round's bullets inserted above moved it, and a line-numbered anchor into a file that grows at the top cannot hold, so the referent is named by content.) So the dependency claim is unchanged (no doc quotes them as evidence, no harness reads them), but the earlier wording here said "returns **zero** hits", which was true only at the moment it was measured and became false the instant this changelog entry was written — and the pipeline that has been reporting it (`… | grep -v node_modules`) hides matches in the whole line, not just in its path, so it printed a false zero on the citation sweep that motivated the correction. Recorded as evidence-with-no-referent-according-to-a-sweep-that-excludes-its-own-record, which is the honest form of the claim; the risk being closed is *new* captures getting committed by habit, which the patterns now stop.
  - **What this change deliberately does not do:** the five already-tracked files stay tracked, because `.gitignore` only filters untracked paths. Untracking them is `git rm --cached`, which is index surgery inside this session's still-unapproved commit batch — and two of them (`audit_json_http.json`, `audit_nda_http.json`) are additionally *dirty* right now, so they need a keep-or-discard decision before the index is touched, not a reflexive removal. Recorded here so the guard is not mistaken for a cleanup that already happened.
  - **The same gap existed one directory up, and that is where the cited captures live**: the two files the docs point a reviewer at — `bench_nda_2026-09-17.txt`, `bench_results_core5.txt` — were neither tracked nor ignored, because the block above only matched under `bench_nda/`. A third pattern `/bench_*.txt` (`.gitignore:66`) now covers root-level dated captures; the leading slash anchors it to the repository root so it cannot reach into `bench_nda/` and shadow the rules above it. Measured: all **8** root `bench_*.txt` files now report IGNORED (the two cited captures, six other run outputs including this round's `bench_scaling_recheck.txt`), `git check-ignore -v bench_newrun.txt` resolves through `.gitignore:66`, and `git ls-files | git check-ignore --stdin --no-index -v` shows **no tracked file** matching that rule — the three tracked `bench_nda/*.txt` are outside the anchor, so the guard breaks nothing that already exists.

- **WASM instruction limit now enforced**: The `instruction_limit` setting in `[wasm_runtimes]` config (default 10,000,000) was previously parsed but never applied. It now drives wasmer-middlewares metering on every plugin WASM runtime (13 language arms over 12 engines — `typescript` wraps the QuickJS runtime, `src/wasm_runtime/typescript.rs:11`), with a bypass for the internal benchmark harness. Metering traps are classified as resource-limit errors with actionable guidance, including at runtime creation (interpreter bootstrap consumes metered instructions too — QuickJS needs more than 1,000,000 instructions just to initialize).
- **Per-call budget reset**: Metered instruction budgets deplete cumulatively across calls on persistent interpreter instances. Budgets are now reset before tool registration and before each tool call, so every call starts with a fresh allowance.
- **Metering trap recovery**: A metering trap (e.g. an infinite loop hitting the limit) leaves the cached interpreter unusable for subsequent calls. The runtime is now evicted on such errors and rebuilt from the module cache on the next call — self-healing, no server restart needed.
- **`initialize` capabilities now describe what the server can actually emit**: `build_initialize_response` reports `resources` as `{"subscribe": false, "listChanged": false}` and `prompts` as `{"listChanged": false}`, and `roots` is gone from the map entirely. Rationale for each: a `true` flag is a promise that the notification arrives, and neither a resource-update nor a prompt-list notification has a producer anywhere in the tree; `roots` is a **client** capability in the MCP spec — advertised by the client in its own initialize request, and it is the server that sends `roots/list` — so it never belonged in the server-side map. Measured payload, identical from `--mode stdio` and from `POST /v1/mcp` on 2026-10-10: `{"tools":{"listChanged":true},"resources":{"subscribe":false,"listChanged":false},"prompts":{"listChanged":false},"sampling":{},"logging":{},"elicitation":{}}` — `elicitation` appears on the JSON-RPC path only (`include_extra`), and is absent from the NDA modes. Two known-wrong things were deliberately left alone because they were not approved this round and changing them alters what clients gate on: `sampling` and `elicitation` are *also* client capabilities and still sit in the server map, and the `roots/list` arm still answers locally with a static empty list, which is the direction inverted. The same category error was also published in `crates/velocity-mcp-core/src/lib.rs` (`roots` and `elicitation` in its capability map, with a test pinning `capabilities.roots` to be an object); that crate ships independently, so it was fixed in its own pass on 2026-10-10 — see the core entry under **Fixed**. `test_initialize_returns_protocol_info` now asserts the two downgrades and the absence of `roots`, so the map cannot silently drift back. Documentation corrected in the same pass: both `initialize` examples in `docs/API.md`, its SSE section (which previously showed an idealised stream including an `event: resource_update` frame that has no producer), `docs/USER_GUIDE.md`, which no longer claims elicitation-and-roots support, and the `0x01` method row of `docs/NDA_PROTOCOL.md`.

### Fixed

- **#619 the #555 pin and the interpreter test share one guest program slot, so the binary test binary was flaky in about three of ten runs — and the red is the pin, not a new defect.** The pre-guard corpse log (`%USERPROFILE%\AppData\Local\Temp\g619_repro.log`, outside the repository per #600) shows `tests\binary_protocol_test.rs:320:5` panicking with `expected the guest's own output, got: {"error":"undefined variable 'name' at line 1"}` on runs 2, 9 and 10 of ten bare-exe executions; each test passes alone (2 exact runs each, 1 passed / 12 filtered out); both sequential orders pass under `--test-threads=1` (2 passed / 11 filtered out each). The thread-flag note: the ten-run battery used the default libtest parallelism (NUMBER_OF_PROCESSORS=12 here), not `--test-threads=8`; the `--test-threads=8` rate quoted in the previous round came from a different g618-prefix battery that kept no per-run log, so today's figure supersedes it. Root cause read out of the guest: `interp_register_tool` (`bench_tools/interp_core/interp.c:2676-2684`) copies the source into a single `tool_source` buffer and voids its name arguments, so each interp_core language holds exactly one program while the host keys a per-language `HashMap<String,String>` (`src/wasm_runtime/r.rs:17-23`) and `register_wasm_tool_source` (`src/plugins/mod.rs:577`) lets the last registration answer for every name in that language; the JSON path re-registers as a side effect of every call (`execute_wasm_plugin_tool` at `src/plugins/mod.rs:450`, per-call registration at `:476-478`). Two tests touching the same language stomp each other's program, which is why the full-binary run was flaky while both tests passed alone and in either order under serialisation.
  - **What landed.** Two file-level guards: `let _guest = lock_guest_slot();` at `tests/binary_protocol_test.rs:243` and `:304`, one static mutex at `:337`, one helper at `:339` using poison-tolerant `into_inner()`, and a doc comment at `:329-336` explaining the mechanism. No production line moved; the fix reproduces the serialisation inside the binary rather than relying on a CLI flag CI's literal command doesn't carry (cf. #505/#528 gate-scope lesson). File identity post-edit: 12,009 bytes, 343 content lines (LF), md5 `42d1689640c823cc4139e5d7264994dc`.
  - **Why a mutex rather than `--test-threads=1`.** The hazard class reaches the lib test binary only through `WASM_RUNTIME_CACHE`-backed calls; measured: zero test-side call sites of `call_wasm_tool_binary` / `execute_wasm_plugin_tool` inside `src/` (sites at `src/registry.rs:709`, `:766`, `src/plugins/mod.rs:283` are all production), and the only test-side `load_plugins` (`src/protocol/nda_native.rs:1848`) reads `std::env::temp_dir()` and never calls a tool. The six interp_core runtime self-tests (`src/wasm_runtime/{r,php,perl,csharp,java,julia}.rs`) build their own instance (`RRuntime::cold_start`) and are `#[ignore]`, so their slot is per-instance and immune. Therefore the class does not reach the lib test binary today; the binary_protocol_test integration binary is closed at 2/2 enrolled.

- **#495/#496 Guest silent-truncation caps now emit explicit errors instead of silently truncating**: Three hard caps in the shared `interp_core` interpreter (`bench_tools/interp_core/interp.c`) were returning partial results with no indication to the caller when limits were hit. All three now set an error in `g_error` before returning:
  - **Loop iteration cap (while/for)**: loops hitting 100,000 iterations now set `"Loop exceeded 100000 iterations (hard cap)"` instead of silently exiting with the last computed value.
  - **TLV array/object element cap**: arrays or objects exceeding 100,000 elements now set `"TLV array exceeded N elements (hard cap)"` or `"TLV object exceeded N elements (hard cap)"` instead of returning the partial collection.
  - **TLV key length cap**: object keys exceeding the max string length now set `"TLV object key exceeds max string length"` instead of returning the partial object.
  - **Measured impact**: all 765 library tests still pass; the change makes previously-invisible budget exhaustion observable so callers can distinguish "completed normally" from "hit a safety limit". The caps themselves remain at 100k because raising them would require rebuilding all six interp_core WASM modules (#520 cost class), but the explicit error message means the server can now propagate the failure upstream rather than returning silently-correct-but-incomplete data.

- **#616 the Merkle toggle had eight flippers and no enrolled readers: 79 `#[test]` fns at the committed baseline assumed the digest default while another test switched it off, and the serialisation that makes that impossible is now in place (2026-10-14).** `features.nda_merkle` is process-global state read through `merkle_enabled()` (`src/protocol/nda_native.rs`) by every frame builder and validator, and `MERKLE_TOGGLE_LOCK` (`src/protocol/nda_native.rs:1455`) existed to serialise it. Mutual exclusion needs *both* sides to acquire it: the tests that flip the toggle *and* the tests that assume the default. At the committed baseline `ce9bd9f` only the flippers did. This round enrolled the readers, and the enrolment was measured rather than counted.
  - **The instrument, and the four defects it took to make it trustworthy.** The census is a static reachability walk over the 49 `.rs` files under `src/`, kept outside the repository per #600 (`%USERPROFILE%\AppData\Local\Temp\g616_enroll9.py`). Four defects were found and fixed in it before any figure here was published. (1) The helper-folding step matched a function's own `fn name(` header, so the guard skipped every helper and the fold was a silent no-op; fixed by stripping the header before matching. (2) An unmasked brace walk mis-nests on any test holding an unbalanced brace inside a string literal, and two do: `"{not valid json}}}"` and `"not valid json{{{"`. Unmasked it hid ten real `json_rpc.rs` enrollees and manufactured a false reach in `nmcp_binary.rs`. (3) The first fix masked string literals with a regex whose replacement deleted the newlines inside multi-line raw strings, so every reported line number after such a site was wrong. (4) Masking is now applied per line, which preserves line counts. The last of the four was the one that nearly published a false baseline: the shipped auditor hardcoded `pathlib.Path('src')` and silently ignored its path argument, so the run believed to be the HEAD control re-audited the working tree and printed the working tree's own verdicts. The tell was that two different roots printed identical numbers. `ROOT` now comes from `argv` and the header prints the root and the file count, so a run cannot be misattributed to the tree it did not read.
  - **The two readings, from the same instrument, one control at a time.** HEAD `ce9bd9f` unpacked by `git archive HEAD src` (49 `.rs` files, 0 occurrences of `lock_merkle`, 10 lines naming `MERKLE_TOGGLE_LOCK`): **87 toggle-reaching `#[test]` fns, 8 holding a lock, 79 holding nothing**, gate exit 1. The finished tree, same 49 files: **88 reaching, 88 holding, 0 exposed**, 89 holders in total, gate exit 0. The 87 -> 88 is not an enrolment artefact: the set differential names exactly one test that entered the reaching set, `malformed_tools_call_payload_is_rejected_not_dispatched` (`src/protocol/nmcp_binary.rs:2065`), which exists only in the uncommitted era, and no test left the set. The 89th holder is `json_rpc::tests::test_parse_error_response_format`, which takes the lock without the classifier reaching the toggle through it, defensive and harmless, named here so the arithmetic closes.
  - **What landed.** `pub(crate) fn lock_merkle()` at `src/protocol/nda_native.rs:3102`, under its three-line reason comment at `:3099` and the function's own three lines through `:3104`: seven added lines including one blank separator, and the only non-line-neutral addition this round made to that file. Two `#[cfg(test)]` forwarders so sibling modules do not spell out the path: `src/protocol/json_rpc.rs:1692` and `src/protocol/nmcp_binary.rs:2251`, each the last item in its file. **81 enrolment call sites**: 11 in `json_rpc.rs`, 31 in `nda_native.rs`, 39 in `nmcp_binary.rs`; 86 lines of those three files contain `lock_merkle` once the three function headers and two forwarder bodies are added back. Every one of the 81 sits inside its module's `mod tests` region (`:723`, `:1341`, `:803` respectively), verified by line arithmetic rather than by intent, so no production symbol, no error string and no dispatch arm moved.   - **The fix is justified structurally, and the flake instrument says so.** `cargo +stable test -q --lib --features http,database,oauth2 -- --test-threads=16 protocol::` run twenty times: **red=0/20 on the HEAD tree and red=0/20 on the finished tree.** A hazard that has not fired is not a hazard that cannot fire: an unenrolled reader can observe a frame built while the toggle was off, whose root field is zero, and validate it after the flipper restored the default, and libtest's alphabetical ordering happens to keep the two classes apart today. - **#613 the registry guard's reader side was unenrolled in three tests, and the comment above the helpers claimed a race the code structure refutes.** Two findings, one round. The enrolment: `lock_registries()` (`src/registry.rs:2740`) had sixteen occurrences inside `src/registry.rs` and twenty call sites suite-wide across four files, and #518 had put every registry *writer* test inside one, but a brace walk from each `get_tools()` call site back to its enclosing `#[test]` found three readers outside the domain -- `src/registry.rs:2783`, `src/registry.rs:4279` and `src/registry.rs:6304` -- and a grep cannot produce that census, which is how it classified `src/registry.rs:4278`'s `register_benchmark_tools_locked(5)` as unguarded in a body searched for `locked_tools`. All three are enrolled by one-for-one replacements, so `src/registry.rs` stays at 6,358 lines and no pointer into it moves: the first two take the whole-body tuple idiom the repo already uses at `src/registry.rs:4894`, which holds `lock_registries` to the end of the test rather than to the end of the read, and the third takes `locked_tools()` (`src/registry.rs:6349`) because its value feeds a four-line `into_iter` / `find` / `expect` chain a single-line tuple cannot absorb. **The second finding is the one that mattered.** The two comment lines above those helpers asserted that "a writer holding the lock still races a reader that never takes it", and that is false as written: every registry global is already internally locked on its own path -- `register_tool_lazy` takes `get_macro_registry()`'s mutex at `src/registry.rs:138` before its push, and `get_tools()` reads its cache under `TOOLS_CACHE` at `src/registry.rs:172` -- so an unenrolled reader cannot observe a *torn* registry. Nor was any test going red: the census of all 67 `assert_eq!(x.len(), N)` sites under `src/` (19 files) contains no exact count against the output of `get_tools()`; the one exact-count registry assertion, `src/registry.rs:3103`, counts `get_builtin_tools()`, which is a static list, `src/protocol/json_rpc.rs:841` asserts `tools.len()` on a slice past a beyond-range cursor and is zero at any registry size, and `src/plugins/mod.rs:1070` counts the pure `plugins_to_registry_tools` result. What the mutex defines is a serialisation *domain*, and the hazard is logical rather than structural: `register_benchmark_tools` pushes its tools one at a time (`src/registry.rs:113`), so a reader outside the domain can observe three of five mid-batch, and `test_nda_tool_registry_eviction` calls `clear()` on a process-global cache at `src/registry.rs:5976` while holding the guard at `src/registry.rs:5972`, so an unenrolled reader of that cache can see it empty. That is the same interleaving #526 turned red for the process environment, and the comment now says the true thing on the same two lines. **Four further readers were audited and left alone, each for a measured reason.** `src/protocol/nda_native.rs:1758` takes one `get_tools()` snapshot and encodes expected and actual from it, so a concurrent registration cannot flip its assert; `stable_encoded_triple` (`src/protocol/nda_native.rs:1791`) needs no lock because it retries on `registry_generation`, which loads `REGISTRY_GENERATION` (`src/registry.rs:56`), an `AtomicU64`, with `Ordering::Acquire` (`src/registry.rs:61`) -- a checked-retry mechanism, and the right shape for a cache-consistency probe; the two reads inside `perf_probe_tools_list` (`src/protocol/nda_native.rs:1685`) sit in a body `#[ignore]` keeps out of the multi-threaded harness the hazard needs (`src/protocol/nda_native.rs:1684`), and enrolling them would need a whole-body tuple measuring 109 characters against rustfmt's 100-column limit, costing a line shift to put a mutex inside the timing loop the probe exists to measure; and `src/protocol/nmcp_binary.rs:2213` already sits under `src/protocol/nmcp_binary.rs:2208`. Production `get_tools()` calls are out of scope, because a test-only mutex on the dispatch path would serialise requests. One cost is disclosed rather than glossed: `test_get_tools_returns_builtin_tools` now acquires the mutex inside its own `[METRIC] get_tools_dispatch` window, so that printed microsecond figure measures lock-plus-dispatch from this round on. **Gates, all re-measured on this tree:** `cargo fmt --all -- --check` 0; the three enrolled tests green by name; `cargo +stable test --lib` 765 / 0 / 38; `cargo +stable test --lib --features http,database,oauth2` 832 / 0 / 38; `cargo +stable test --workspace` 1,170 / 0 / 55 and the same with `--features http,database,oauth2` at 1,243 / 0 / 55, both over 31 result rows; `cargo clippy --lib --tests --all-features -- -D warnings` 0. The path-census `+8` (831 to 839, file bucket only) that memory records as arriving inside this uncommitted batch cannot be re-attributed, because only the #616 and #617 rounds' census logs survive and the row-level differential needs both readings: recorded as unrecoverable, not as accounted for. **Census on the finished bytes, each gate re-run twice to a byte-identical stdout and the citation gate again after the three redemptions below:** citations moved from 1036 checked, 807 populated, 0 hard, 217 advisory, 12 allowed to 1061, 840, 0, 209, 12 -- checked +25 is exactly this entry's own qualified spans; populated +33 is those 25 plus eight of the nine historical mentions of changelog line 185 that sat on blank target rows before the insert and now hit a token on their shifted target (the ninth moved from the blank subclass to the token subclass), all left as written per #579; and advisory -8 is those eight leaving, three of this entry's own spans arriving and the same three going again -- they first printed as unearned token advisories at registry lines 138, 5976 and 61, and naming those rows' own identifiers (the macro-registry accessor, the clear call, the generation static) redeemed them without re-pointing a single line number. - **#606 the wrong-reason census that #605 deferred: four tests asserted only that an error happened, one of them stayed green with the Merkle digest switched off, and the differential that proves it is now a permanent part of `cargo test --lib` (2026-10-14).** An `is_err()` assert proves that an Err occurred, not which arm produced it, and `parse_nda_request` (`src/protocol/nda_native.rs:299`) refuses with an explicit `return Err` at seven sites (`:301` frame too small, `:310` bad magic, `:316` an encrypted frame on a non-oauth2 build, `:332`, `:344` and `:358` the three Merkle arms, `:365` empty payload) while the AEAD open at `:322` and the two TLV decodes at `:376` and `:380` propagate with `?` — so any of those ten answers satisfies a test whose name claims the digest. Two of the three Merkle arms are a `#[cfg]` pair (`:344` compiles only with `oauth2`, `:358` only without), which is last round's unreachable-fallback class sitting inside the function this round audits. The ranking heuristic #605 shipped was therefore replaced with a two-state differential: build the frame, flip one byte, parse it with the digest on and then off, and credit a test with pinning the digest only if the disabled leg returns `Ok`.
  - **The census, before and after, with every bucket delta attributable to an edit.** Universe: the 89 `.rs` files under `src/`, `tests/`, `client/` and `crates/` (target, node_modules and .git skipped; `benches/` is not in it). Tests holding a bare `is_err()` or an `unwrap_err()`: 300 -> **297**. Bare `is_err()` occurrences: 326 -> **322**. `unwrap_err()` occurrences: 230 -> **230**. Bare-only tests with no message assert anywhere: 73 -> **70**. Flagged for a callee with two or more Err sites: 17 -> **14**. The composition is one line of arithmetic: the four repaired tests each lost exactly one bare occurrence (-4), three of them had no other bare or panic-path assert so they left the row set entirely (-3 rows, -3 bare-only, -3 flagged), and `unwrap_err()` cannot move because no edit touched a panic path.
  - **What the differential printed** (`probe_g606_attribution`, run against the pre-repair tree; kept in `%USERPROFILE%\g606_probe.log` and `%USERPROFILE%\g606_probe2.log`, both `test result: ok. 1 passed`, 803 filtered): `TLV_LASTBYTE_ENABLED Err: NDA frame Merkle root mismatch` / `TLV_LASTBYTE_DISABLED Ok`, `INPLACE_LASTBYTE_DISABLED Ok`, `FLAT_LASTBYTE_ENABLED Err: Flat frame Merkle root mismatch` / `FLAT_LASTBYTE_DISABLED Err: Flat decode: unknown tag 0xfb`, `FLAT_MIDNAME_BYTE 99 -> 98` / `FLAT_MIDNAME_ENABLED Err: Flat frame Merkle root mismatch` / `FLAT_MIDNAME_DISABLED Ok`, `TLV_TRAILING_DISABLED Ok` / `INPLACE_TRAILING_DISABLED Ok` / `TLV_TRAILING_ENABLED Err: NDA frame Merkle root mismatch`.
  - **The false green was `test_flat_merkle_integrity`** (`src/protocol/nda_native.rs:1666`), and its mechanism is arithmetic on the flat layout rather than a guess: `build_flat_request` writes method, an 8-byte little-endian id, a 2-byte name length, the name, then `encode_flat_value(args)`, and `Value::Null` encodes as the single tag byte `0x04` (`src/protocol/nda_native.rs:1076`). The test passed `""` as the tool name and flipped the frame's last byte, so `0x04 ^ 0xFF = 0xFB` landed on the field loop's catch-all at `src/protocol/nda_native.rs:1159` and the frame was refused as `Flat decode: unknown tag 0xfb` **with the digest off as well as on** — both toggle states returned Err, so the bare `is_err()` pinned nothing about the Merkle check while the test's name claimed exactly that. The repair gives the frame a real name (`"bench_echo"`, because a mid-name byte requires a name to be mid) and tampers `FRAME_HEADER_SIZE + 14`, the fourth of those ten name bytes, then asserts the mismatch message and ends with its own disabled-guard leg.
  - **The other three were true greens with claims a message assert could not previously reach.** `test_merkle_integrity` (`src/protocol/nda_native.rs:1435`): a ping payload is the method byte plus a 9-byte id TLV and `build_nda_request` omits a `Null` data, so the last byte *is* the id's least-significant byte, the frame still decodes, and the digest is the only thing that can refuse it (`TLV_LASTBYTE_*`) — it now asserts that message and adds a `MerkleDisabledGuard` leg asserting `Ok`. The tampered leg of `inplace_parse_rejects_bad_frames` (`src/protocol/nda_native.rs:1972`) is the same shape one function over (`INPLACE_LASTBYTE_DISABLED Ok`), and its four structural legs stay bare on purpose.
  - **Why the encryption test could never have tested what its comment offered.** `decrypt_nda_frame_payload` runs at `src/protocol/nda_native.rs:322` and the Merkle comparison at `:327`, so a tampered ciphertext is consumed by the AEAD tag before any digest is computed and the digest cannot be the reason that frame errs. `test_encrypted_frame_tampered_ciphertext` (`src/protocol/nda_native.rs:2991`) now asserts the `Encrypted frame decryption failed` prefix, emitted at `src/protocol/nda_native.rs:323` and at its in-place twin `:518` and nowhere else in `src/`, and its old comment "Should fail (either decryption or Merkle mismatch)" is gone because the disjunction is false. The green `--features oauth2` leg below is the witness that the prefix matches.

Build with --features oauth2")` beside its live `#[cfg(feature = "oauth2")]` block. The enclosing module is gated on the *presence* of the same feature the arm negates, so the two can never both be candidates and that string was unreachable in the default build (`default = []`), in `.github/workflows/release.yml:37`, and in `deploy/Dockerfile:31`, both of which build `--features http,database,oauth2`. Deleting it moves no symbol and no behaviour: a feature build keeps only the live block, and a non-feature build never compiles the function at all — so this is not a bless-or-revert item.

The census is what separates it from the two sibling strings that *are* live and *are* documented: `src/main.rs:258` prints `HTTP transport not enabled. Build with --features http` (quoted at `docs/API.md:102` and `README.md:277`) and `src/resources.rs:331` returns `Database feature not enabled` — both sit in files whose ancestors do not require the feature they negate, so their arms really do compile.

The docs bill for this round is zero, and the repair creates no new doc debt.

The green was real — the arm it exercised was the token miss inside `get_valid_token`, whose message `No token found for connector` comes from `src/oauth2.rs:736`, not the `Connector not found:` arm its name claims. **Measured by red-check:** with the id left at `test` and the assert made to print the reason, the leg failed with `wrong failure reason: No token found for connector: test`, `test result: FAILED. 19 passed; 1 failed; 0 ignored; 0 measured; 811 filtered out`, exit 101, `--test-threads=1`. The same class as #572.

- **#601 the comprehensive-benchmark guide claimed this binary measures all 7 production runtimes, and the harness's skip comment justified it with a WASM EH incompatibility the current build disproves (2026-10-14).** Found by counting measurement sections against the coverage sentence instead of trusting the enumeration.
  - **The claim, and the count that answers it.** The guide opened with `all 7 production WASM language runtimes (and 6 planned runtimes) and transport protocols`; the binary pushes twelve results, and `src/wasm_runtime/mod.rs:563` is the `Unsupported WASM language` fall-through of a match that registers thirteen arms, so exactly one registered runtime has no section. `benches/comprehensive_benchmark.rs:736` lists `ruby_wasm` in the module scan, and nothing calls it -- which is why `benches/comprehensive_benchmark.rs:4` now reads `The 12 languages this binary drives`.
  - **"Planned" is a decision, not this round's defect.** The six tree-walk interpreters are labelled planned by the 2026-09-13 reclassification, so nothing here promotes them; the wrong figure was the number of engines the harness measures, not their status.
  - **The reason the skip cited is not live.** The comment now at `benches/comprehensive_benchmark.rs:947` records that the scan finds `ruby.wasm` and that Ruby gets no measurement section; what that line replaced claimed mruby was skipped for a WASM EH compatibility issue with Wasmer. The current build answers the Ruby plugin tool `rb_text_stats` (`plugins/wasm_ruby_tools.json:6`) through the server's own stdio path: `{"chars":13,"words":3,"upper":"ONE TWO THREE"}` with `isError` false in 6.1 s, served by `target/debug/velocity_mcp.exe` at 45,229,056 bytes, mtime 2026-10-13 14:45:35, with none of the 49 files under `src` newer than it (the release exe is absent, so debug is the current artifact).
  - **Ruby is measured by the two harnesses that do call it, so this is a harness gap, not a runtime gap.** `benches/wasm_tool_latency.rs:122` reaches it through `bench_runtime`, and `benches/metering_cache_bench.rs:50` and `benches/metering_cache_bench.rs:100` both carry `RUBY_WASM`, one per language table.
  - **What landed, and the line neutrality the repair owed.** Four in-line rewrites at `docs/COMPREHENSIVE_BENCHMARK.md:3`, `docs/COMPREHENSIVE_BENCHMARK.md:9`, `docs/COMPREHENSIVE_BENCHMARK.md:35` and `docs/COMPREHENSIVE_BENCHMARK.md:127`; the file stayed at 225 lines and LF-only on both sides because two citations in this file point at line 203 of that guide. The bench header and comment went from 1 line to 3, taking `benches/comprehensive_benchmark.rs` from 1032 to 1034 lines; the only line citations aimed at that file are this entry's own three, all of them above the growth point, so nothing re-anchored. `cargo +stable check --benches` finishes in 1m 42s at exit 0 and `cargo fmt --all -- --check` exits 0; no behaviour, no test, and no test total moved, so this entry joins no bless-or-revert batch.

- **#597 and #599 were both priced as witnesses for the citation gate and both found nothing: the class #596 said slipped through does not have a detector worth landing, and the one real defect was the gate's own comment, which printed a superseded tree's pricing figures as though they were its live report (2026-10-14).** #596 closed with the claim that the anchor that actually slipped through -- a wrong line that shares its vocabulary with the right one -- needs a witness keyed on uniqueness rather than presence, and named that **#597**. It is now built, priced against the whole corpus, adjudicated by reading, and **rejected**. So is the follow-up it exposed, filed here as **#599**. What landed is a three-line, line-neutral binding in `scripts/check_citations.py:234`.
  - **#597, shape one: presence is not the right key, and the neighbour test has no true positives.** The first candidate asked whether the cited line fails to carry the claim while a nearby line carries all of it. At a +/-10-line window that is **19** rows, and every one of the 19 is benign: this corpus routinely names a declaration and cites the statement inside its block, so `bench_echo_impl` is cited at the body line that returns, `output_append` at its clamp, `prop_sandbox_always_cleans_up` at its `Err` arm, `AST_WHILE` at its `while`, `write_output_raw` at its comparison, `workingDir` at the schema key rather than the tool name. A rule that fires on that shape reports its own rule, not a drift, so the class was dropped rather than tuned.
  - **#597, shape two, which is the one the brief actually described: a verbatim-quote witness.** Harvest every backticked code span in the corpus and ask whether the cited line carries it, whether the span is present verbatim elsewhere in that file, and how far away. Across the owned markdown pages **594** spans were harvested, **415** of them appear nowhere in the cited file (English paraphrase, which no positional rule can speak about), **99** are absent from the cited line but present verbatim elsewhere, and tightening to a quote of at least 25 characters found verbatim within 10 lines leaves **9** rows. The classifier was controlled rather than trusted: it fires on #596's historical drift (the guard cited at its own panic site) and stays silent on the corrected anchor plus five benign statement cites. All 9 were then read against their prose and their target region, and **none** is a stale anchor: four name an enclosing declaration while citing a member of it (the `dangerous_unix` array at line 1219 versus the literal at 1227, the `SseFrame` enum versus its arm, `run_test` versus the call inside it, the benchmark's `let thread_counts` versus the constant two lines down), two are the two-citation-per-line shape where the neighbour's quote matches and the flagged cite is correct -- `.github/workflows/ci.yml` line 34 versus 37, and `client/Cargo.toml` line 23 versus 27, where the sentence about the wasm32 block really does describe `features = ["json"]` -- and the remaining two are range citations. **Zero** true positives at a 9-row cost, so the witness is not landed and the brief it was filed under is closed by that measurement instead of by a report.
  - **#599, the half-check the adjudication exposed, measured at zero cost today.** `CITATION_RE` takes the first number of a range citation, so `src/sampling.rs:126-128` is checked only at 126 and the upper endpoint has never been validated by this gate: an anchor reading past the end of a file, or landing on a blank line at the far end of a range, passes silently. The population is **38** range citations, of which **0** overshoot their file and **0** end on a blank line. The remaining question a span raises is tokens, and there **7** ranges carry no claim token anywhere inside the span -- but a span includes its own first line, so those 7 are already in the advisory tier through their lower endpoint, which is why the gap adds no row that the census does not already print. Left unfixed deliberately: validating the upper endpoint changes the report digest, and on this tree it would find nothing. The blind spot is real, it is now sized, and the next round that touches this gate should price it against the corpus rather than assume either number.
  - **The defect that did land, and why it is bound rather than restated.** The attribution comment this gate carries explained why re-verdicting was rejected and cited its evidence as "Priced on 2026-10-14 against this tree: 90 of the resolving rows ... and 82 advisories ... while the headline moves only 703 -> 695 resolving / 188 -> 196 advisory". The live report on the tree this entry was priced on, before its own eight lines went in, read **93 of 716** resolving rows and **84 of 193** advisories, because every doc edit since the pricing moved the denominators -- so a comment that claimed to describe "this tree" described a tree that no longer exists, and a reader comparing it with the printout above it would conclude the gate disagrees with itself. Those two figures are quoted here as evidence of the staleness and are not offered as live: the gate's own printout is the only live source, and the denominators below are named for the tree they came from. The repair does not restate the counts, because a restated live count decays the next time anyone touches a document: the three lines now bind each figure to the tier state it was measured against (703 resolving / 188 advisory) and leave the gate's own printout as the only live source. Three lines replaced three, so the ten line anchors the corpus keeps into this script (25, 26, 29, 72, 140, 150, 161, 230, 268, 300) all still point where they did, and comment text never reaches stdout, so the census report itself is byte-identical across the edit.

That digest is published here only because it survives being published, and the test for that is not free: the report echoes no prose as prose, but it does print every advisory row's own citing-line token list under looks for:, and the cited target's text under line says:, so a bullet carrying an advisory citation contributes its own backticked vocabulary to the very report it wants to hash. Three new pointers therefore contribute one resolving and two advisory rows, which forces the remaining +1 resolving and -1 advisory onto a single pre-existing row: the eight-line shift moved one of the nine frozen historical mentions out of the advisory tier and onto a populated line, and those nine now read **7 resolving / 2 advisory**. The cause was its default target, a bare string handed straight to `open()`, so the gate inherited whatever directory the shell happened to be in.

Measured, not asserted: run from the repository root and from an unrelated directory the gate prints the identical two lines, `16 tools with a declared schema, 16 with a production dispatch arm` and `every declared parameter is read and every read parameter is declared`, at exit **0**; the whole seven-gate battery was then re-run from both directories, exit **0** each, and the importer that pulls `close_block` and `prop_names` out of this module still works off-root because `__file__` resolves the same way under import. The eighth instrument, `scripts/check_ts_client.py`, was re-run from the root only at that point (exit **0**, `ALL CHECKS PASSED`); it is included in the both-directory battery re-run on the finished tree reported below.
  - **Negative control on a copy outside the repo, because a fix that only moves a path can silently disarm a checker.** The gate's detection power was re-proved rather than assumed: a Temp copy of `src/registry.rs` with one production read key renamed turns it red at exit **1** printing `advertised, never read : ['filePath']  <-- also required ['filePath']`, while the same copy written back unmutated reads exit **0**, so the flip belongs to the mutation and no repository file was touched. The sweep is what keeps the claim honest: **40** production reads whose key also appears as a JSON property were each renamed in turn, the first probe tried (`.get("error")`) did **not** flip, and the reason it did not is itself a finding worth stating -- that read sits outside any quoted dispatch-arm block, so the gate's coverage is arm-scoped rather than call-site-wide and **40** is the number of tracked-shape reads it can see, not the number of reads in the file.
  - **Re-anchor bill, priced before the edit and paid with it.** Five entry lines plus one blank separator went in at the top of `### Fixed`, shifting every line below by six. The one live self-pointer this file maintains -- the bless-or-revert locator on the #572 bill bullet -- therefore moves from line 304 to line 310, and the digit was corrected in place against the text witness at that line rather than by arithmetic alone. The nine occurrences of the deliberately stale self-pointer at line 185 are left exactly as their own runs recorded them, per #579, and the shift was measured rather than waved through: all nine leave the census's blank-line advisory class, six resolving against the bullet that now sits on that line and three re-entering advisory as token mismatches against it, which is the same self-inflicted drift this file already documents in the earlier rounds that billed it. `README.md` and `docs/*.md` contain **0** citations of any `CHANGELOG.md` line number, so the whole bill is digits inside this file.

On the tree with the gate fixed and before this entry was written it reads exactly the published #596 tuple, which is the proof that a `.py` edit carries no citation bill: the report digest is byte-identical to that round's two-run fixed point. After this entry landed the census was re-run on the finished tree: three runs at exit **0** print one byte-identical report, **921 checked / 716 resolve to a populated line / 0 hard failures / 193 advisory / 12 allowed**, 75,838 bytes over 894 CRLF terminators, sha256[:16] 82d504d0551b1d4e. Every delta has an inventory rather than a subtraction. `checked` is **+4**, exactly this entry's four pointers, all in the repair bullet and all resolving. `populated` is **+10**: those four plus the six stale self-pointer rows the shift lifted off the blank line onto the #572 headline. `advisory` is **-6**, the same nine rows read the other way (blank 9 to 0, token 190 to 193, three re-entering as mismatches against a populated line), so the insert moved a tier and repaired nothing; `hard` 0 and `allowed` 12 are unchanged. The print goes 887 report lines to 894 (a first reading of 888 to 895 counted the appended exit-label line as report content) and 75,093 bytes to 75,838, and that +745 reconciles by class with nothing left over -- advisory rows +57, SIBLING-REDEEMED +570, UNCLAIMED +118, every other class flat -- the four new attribution rows being this entry's own pointer to the repo-relative label line plus three of the stale self-pointers, since a pointer into a path carries slashes and colons and so contributes no tokens of its own to the union. One trap is worth naming because it nearly falsified the sentence above: the same tuple printed at 74,944 bytes when the report was captured through a Python write rather than the shell's, exactly the 894 CR bytes lower, so a reader comparing that figure with this round's published one would have recorded a 149-byte shrink that is a serialization difference and not a change in the print. The same entry's four line-numbered anchors add no claim there at all, because a span that fully matches the file-plus-line shape is skipped before the extension test is ever reached, so a line anchor belongs to the census and never to the path gate; a name with no slash is skipped for the same structural reason, which is why a README.md or CHANGELOG.md span as this entry spells them counts for nothing there even though the gate separately notes 19 bare labels that match more than one file in the tree. That harvest rule is why the sentence you are reading carries no backtick at all: adding one would bill this entry a claim whose count it has just published. - **#596 the recorded repair for the citation gate's union-token defect (#594) is disproved by measurement: attributing tokens per citation demotes 90 correct rows, keeps the one genuinely stale anchor green, and moves the headline by seven -- so the gate now prints how each row was redeemed, and the stale anchor was repaired by reading (2026-10-14).** #594 was filed as "every backticked span on a line is aimed at every citation on that line", with per-cite attribution as the fix direction. Priced against this tree with the gate's own `claim_tokens`, `SPAN_RE`, `IDENT_RE`, `STOPWORDS`, `ALLOWED` and `resolve` imported from it, and reconciled per line before any figure was taken: **152** of the corpus's lines carry two or more of the **917** citations and **785** citations live on them, so the union rule is not an edge case, it is most of the instrument.
  - **What per-cite attribution does, measured with the span-window rule (each code span given to the nearest citation that follows it, else to the nearest that precedes it, else to all of them when it straddles every cite).** **90** resolving rows lose their match and **83** advisories gain one, so the tiers move **706 -> 699** resolving and **190 -> 197** advisory: **173 rows change tier while the published headline moves by seven**. A census total is therefore not a correctness signal -- the composition is, and it is printed above by the gate itself now. Of the 90, **0** name nothing of their own and **all 90** name something that is absent from the line they cite, and **74** sit on a line carrying a second citation to the *same* file, which is the prose shape ("`symbol` sits at line 1 and line 9 of `a.rs`") where no positional rule can know which anchor the sentence meant. Demoting them manufactures false alarms; the verdicts stay as they are.
  - **A third class that neither attribution can speak about, sized so the blind spot is on the record.** **226** citations on multi-cite lines own no code span at all, and **143** of those are in the resolving tier -- the gate's tier rule treats an empty token set as resolving by construction, so those rows are green whatever the attribution. They are deliberately *not* printed: in the dominant shape one symbol legitimately serves a list of anchors, and a report that fires on the majority of its own corpus reports its own rule, not a defect.
  - **The one real stale anchor in the 90's neighbourhood, and the reason #594's fix direction would have missed it.** The negative-control entry here claimed the triple validator's bounds guard `if o.saturating_add(2) > string_pool.len()` sits at `src/nda_document.rs:245`; the guard is at `src/nda_document.rs:236` and 245 is the indexing statement two lines below it, which is also the line the same sentence names correctly as the panic site. Both lines contain `string_pool`, and `len` is inside `slen`, so the wrong anchor satisfies the token test on its own -- replaying that exact line with both citations aimed at 245 classifies the guard cite as resolving under the union rule *and* under per-cite attribution (its own hits on 245 are `string_pool` and `len`), while the *correct* panic-site citation is the one per-cite attribution demotes, redeemed today only by its neighbour's tokens. The digit is repaired one-for-one at 236, the census read **912 / 703 / 0 / 197 / 12** identically on both sides of that repair on the tree that existed before this entry was written, and that identity is the finding: a subset-of-tokens rule cannot see an anchor that moved to a line sharing its vocabulary, and the recorded attribution fix was never the answer.
