- **#572 the test named for an absent working directory never reached the arm that refuses one — it now does, and it names the arm (2026-10-13).** `test_shell_exec_invalid_working_dir` (`src/registry.rs:5513`) sent `workingDir` as `/nonexistent/path/that/does/not/exist` and asserted only that the call errored. On Windows that string is not absolute, because `Path::is_absolute` at `src/registry.rs:2270` wants a drive letter or a UNC prefix, so the error the assertion was consuming is the validator's shape refusal and has nothing to do with a missing directory; running the repaired body against that same literal prints the refusal it was passing on: `File path must be absolute: /nonexistent/path/that/does/not/exist`. On a POSIX leg that literal *is* absolute, so the Windows host and CI's ubuntu and macOS legs have been asserting two different arms under one test name, and CI's green row said nothing at all about an absent directory.
  - **The replacement makes both platforms walk the same path, then pins which arm produced the failure.** The directory is `std::env::temp_dir().join("velocity_mcp_absent_dir")`: absolute on every platform, with no `..` substring and no symlinked ancestor, so `validate_file_path` returns Ok and the only producer left is `Command::spawn` at `src/registry.rs:1444`, whose io::Error the question-mark propagates raw. The assertion is that the message contains `os error`, and that token is the measured divider between the two arms: io::Error's Display appends an errno suffix on both Windows and POSIX, which the green run proves for this host, while the validator's four refusal sites are hand-written messages that carry no such suffix anywhere in their text. So if an existence check is ever added to the validator, its own wording turns this test red instead of letting it go on passing for a fourth reason.
  - **No behaviour moved, and the gap the test now documents stays open.** The edit sits inside the `#[cfg(test)]` module, so nothing a client can observe changed: `validate_file_path` still performs no existence check, and an absolute, traversal-free, symlink-free `workingDir` that simply does not exist is still refused by the operating system with the raw io::Error reaching the caller. That leak is already itemised in the #561 owner call; what this round adds is a test that states the absence instead of one that cleared it by accident.
  - **Line-neutral because the bill was measured before editing, and the near-miss is worth keeping.** Fifteen pointers aim at `src/registry.rs` lines below this block — eleven in this file and four in `docs/NDA_PROTOCOL.md` — so the replacement had to be eleven lines in and eleven lines out. It is, and both endpoints show it: the test still opens as `test_shell_exec_invalid_working_dir` at `src/registry.rs:5513` and the following `edit_file` case still opens at `src/registry.rs:5527`. The near-miss was the verification itself — the first run filtered on a bare test name under `--exact`, which cannot match a module-qualified path, so it printed `running 0 tests` with `803 filtered out` and exited 0. A filtered-out test is a green test; the flag was dropped and the case re-run by name substring.
  - **Gates measured on this tree, not carried over.** The single case: 1 passed / 0 failed at `REAL_TEST_EXIT=0`. The library leg is 765 / 0 / 38 (`REAL_LIB_EXIT=0`) and both workspace profiles reproduce their published rows exactly, 1,170 / 0 / 55 at default features and 1,243 / 0 / 55 with `http,database,oauth2`, 31 result rows and exit 0 each, because this is a replacement and not an addition: no published count, `#[ignore]` census or CI test command needed re-numbering. `cargo fmt --all -- --check` exits 0 and `cargo clippy --lib --tests -- -D warnings` exits 0. One gate-scope hole surfaced on the way: `cargo clippy --lib --tests --all-features` prints five pre-existing warnings inside `src/wasm_runtime/wasi.rs` at 400, 1463, 1471, 1488 and 1496, a module the default-feature leg CI runs never compiles, so a featured `-D warnings` leg would not be green. Filed as #576 and repaired in the round above it, which also corrects the label used here: four of the five are `unnecessary_cast` and the one at 400 is `manual_range_contains`. The citation census is a two-run fixed point on the repaired file at 824 checked, 637 resolving to a populated line, 0 hard failures, 179 advisory and 8 allowed, exit 0 on both runs. Against the previously published reading that is five added pointers, three of which resolve, and two advisories whose citing lines backtick no identifier occurring on their target; the nine bumped anchors kept their earlier five-resolving, four-advisory split, and the entry itself adds no lines, so no position anchor moved.
  - **The nine bumped anchors read as broken because of my own write, and the gate stayed green at exit 0.** After the digit bump the census classified all nine as advisory blank instead of their previous five-resolving and four-advisory split, and the cause was not the anchor but the file: the whole-file rewrite opened its output with the newline argument set to the CRLF literal while the text it was handed already carried CRLF terminators, so every line ending became CR-CR-LF, 641 of them. Reading that file back through universal newlines turns each doubled terminator into two line breaks, so the gate indexed a phantom document with a blank row after every real one, and a position anchor that points at a populated line reads as blank. That reading, 824 checked with 632 resolving, is void and is not published as this round's fixed point. The repair is byte-level and verified: no CR-CR sequence remains and the terminators and the line elements agree again. (642 terminators over 643 elements) The rule this buys is the one the corpus already carries for whole-file rewrites: set the newline argument to empty so the terminators you hold are the ones that land on disk.

- **#571 three doc sentences credited `shell_exec` with a sandbox its code never enters — all three rewritten against the spawn path (2026-10-13).** `docs/USER_GUIDE.md` told readers that shell commands run in a capability-based sandbox with no network access, that their filesystem is isolated to a temp directory, and that Windows job objects cap their memory. None of that is this tool: `Sandbox::new` has exactly two production construction sites, `src/nda_executor.rs:96` and `src/nda_executor.rs:150`, while `shell_exec` builds its own `Command` at `src/registry.rs:1439`, `src/registry.rs:1494`, `src/registry.rs:1549` and `src/registry.rs:1602` and never enters `crate::sandbox` — the identifiers `Sandbox` and `crate::sandbox` do not occur in `src/registry.rs` at all, and its single case-insensitive "sandbox" match sits inside the *other* tool's description string, `execute_nda` at `src/registry.rs:462`.
  - **The schema already told the truth, so the fix aligns prose to it rather than inventing a claim.** The advertised description at `src/registry.rs:302` ends "Commands run with the server's permissions." The real guards, in code order, are: the destructive-pattern blocklists matched as substrings against the command lowercased with whitespace collapsed, and again with backslashes stripped; a shorter variable-expansion and encoding list matched only against the first of those two normalizations; the 10,000-byte command length cap; `timeout` read as `as_u64().unwrap_or(30).min(300)` with a `child.kill()` once the deadline passes; `take(1_048_576)` on stdout and `take(262_144)` on stderr in all four arms; and `validate_file_path` on `workingDir` when it is supplied. Only the guide was wrong: `docs/API.md:1285` says nothing about a sandbox, so that file needed no edit.
  - **The new paragraph states the two things the old silence concealed.** `src/registry.rs:1425` detects shell metacharacters (`;`, `|`, `&`, backtick, `$`, newline) and logs a warning while deliberately allowing them, so command chaining is available to every caller. And because every match is a substring test over those two fixed normalizations, a blocked pattern spelled any other way is not caught; the row now calls the blocklist a deterrent against accidents rather than a boundary, which is the #554 finding written where a client will actually read it.
  - **Line-neutral by measurement, which is why this round's bill was zero.** All three replacements went in one line for one line: `docs/USER_GUIDE.md` kept its 1,193 line elements, 0 lone CR and LF-only endings. Counted before editing, fifteen citations into that file sit on ten `CHANGELOG.md` lines, and no file outside this one cites it, so one added or removed line would have billed fifteen re-anchors; with the count held, all fifteen still name the same lines. One classification did change, and the gate's rows give its real cause: the pointer the *previous* entry writes into `docs/USER_GUIDE.md:223` went from token-blind to a hit because the rewritten row now states that the child inherits the server's permissions, and the identifier that older sentence backticks as expected on the line is exactly that word. Wording moved the classification, not the anchor.
  - **Two adjacent overclaims recorded, deliberately not repaired.** `Sandbox::check_file_access` and `Sandbox::check_network_access` have no production caller — each appears once as its own definition and otherwise only in `src/sandbox.rs` tests — and inside `Sandbox::spawn_and_collect` the network and memory limits are environment-variable hints to the child, so only the Windows Job Object cap is enforced by the OS. That makes the sandbox's own rows overstate what it confines, but repairing the *enforcement* is a behaviour decision and rewriting those rows means re-adjudicating what "capability-based" promises: filed as #575. Same class, different subject — `docs/USER_GUIDE.md:663` says all plugins run in the capability sandbox, while `src/plugins/mod.rs:322` spawns the executor command directly and the WASM plugin path constructs no `Sandbox` either: filed as #574.
  - **Gates re-measured on this tree, and what no Rust change means.** The five doc gates (`check_citations`, `check_doc_examples`, `check_links`, `check_md_tables`, `check_tool_schema_params`) each exit 0, and the schema census still reconciles 16 tools with 16 dispatch arms. The citation tuple has three stages for this round, each read off the gate's own printed line: 805/623/0/174/8 before any of it, 805/624/0/173/8 after the three rewritten sentences, and 819/634/0/177/8 with this entry present, which is the figure published here. The middle stage's +1 populated against -1 advisory is the single older pointer described above changing class; the final stage's +14 `checked` is this entry's own fourteen pointers, ten of which resolve and four of which are advisories sitting on lines 48, 51 and 52 of this entry — the `execute_nda` description line, the plugin-attribution line and this sentence's own target. None of those four is a broken anchor: each names a line that exists and carries text, and the gate reports it only because no identifier its citing sentence puts in a code span appears on the target line. That substring rule is also the reason the middle-stage flip is not evidence — the token that makes this entry's own pointer at `docs/USER_GUIDE.md:223` resolve is the three-letter word lifted out of the `--lib` flag, and it matches only because it occurs inside a longer word on that row. After writing this sentence the gate was run twice and printed 819/634/0/177/8 both times, so the published number is the tuple's fixed point rather than a figure this entry perturbs. No Rust, TOML or CI file changed this round, so `cargo fmt`, `cargo clippy`, the `--lib` rows and both workspace profiles are unmoved by construction rather than by re-run, and the dashboard suites stay at their 2026-10-13 attestation: a doc paragraph is not a compiled path.
  - **Landing cost, paid with a witness.** Newest-first means this entry goes above the section's existing head, shifting every line below it by eight — seven entry lines plus the blank separator — which moves the nine in-file self-pointers that currently read 158, carried on seven lines, to 166. Each was bumped by digits only, then verified by capturing that line's text before the insert and re-reading the new target after: identical, nine for nine, with no file outside this one pointing into it.

- **#567 `shell_exec` ran commands in a client-supplied directory, and neither doc parameter list said so — while the one test whose name promises that branch sent a key no code reads (2026-10-13).** The knob is real at both ends: `src/registry.rs:307` declares `workingDir` in the tool's `input_schema`, `:1211` reads `arguments["workingDir"]`, and `:1434` passes the value as the child's current directory after validating it. `docs/API.md:1290` and `docs/USER_GUIDE.md:221` each stopped at `timeout`, so a reviewer or client author working from either list had no way to learn the parameter exists. The docs understated the tool, which is the harmless direction only until somebody builds a call from the published list and concludes the feature is missing. Both rows went in directly under `timeout`, the order the schema uses.
  - **The prose describes the check the code performs, not the check it should perform.** `validate_file_path` (`src/registry.rs:2253`) rejects any path whose *string* contains `..`, so it refuses an ordinary filename like `notes..2.txt` as well as a traversal segment, and it never checks that the directory exists. The first draft of both bullets said "no `..` segment", which is the stricter, nicer-sounding and false reading; both rows were rewritten to "must not contain `..` anywhere" before publishing. That same missing existence check is why the invalid-path test passes for a reason it does not state, recorded as #572 below.
  - **`test_shell_exec_timeout_with_working_dir` was a no-directory test wearing a with-directory name.** `src/registry.rs:5571` sent `"working_dir"`, a key the dispatcher never consults, so the read at `:1211` returned `None`, `:1434` took the no-dir arm, and the assertions (`is_err()`, then `"timed out"` or `"timeout"`) were satisfied by the plain timeout path. The test was green before the fix and green after it, which is precisely what makes a mis-keyed parameter test dangerous: it cannot go red when the branch it names breaks. The repair is one key name, line-neutral, and the run is measured: `cargo +stable test --lib -- test_shell_exec_timeout_with_working_dir` → **1 passed; 0 failed; 802 filtered out**, with the verdict read back from a `REAL_EXIT_WDTEST=0` marker the run itself wrote rather than from a pipe's status.
  - **Why the corrected test cannot be masking a broken with-dir branch.** `test_shell_exec_with_working_dir` (`src/registry.rs:4371`) already sends the correct key and asserts *success*, and it passed in the same full-lib run, so the `Some(dir)` arm is exercised by a green test that does not depend on this change. What was missing is the interaction — a child started in a directory *and* a one-second timeout — and the corrected key now covers it.
  - **Gates, each with its real exit code read back from its own log.** `cargo fmt --all -- --check` → **0**. `cargo clippy --lib --tests -- -D warnings` → **0**, its only printing a `proc-macro-error2` future-incompat note, which is dependency code and not a lint on this crate. `cargo +stable test --lib` → **765 passed; 0 failed; 38 ignored** across 803 tests. The five documentation gates (`check_citations`, `check_doc_examples`, `check_links`, `check_md_tables`, `check_tool_schema_params`) were re-run on the final tree, exit **0** each. The markdown-table gate reads **25 files / 101 tables / 720 body rows** (719 before the added row), 0 hard and 0 advisory. The citation census reads **805 checked / 623 populated / 0 hard / 174 advisory / 8 allowed** against **796 / 616 / 0 / 172 / 8** before this round: nine new pointers, seven of which share a token with the line they name, and the two remaining advisories being the test-key line at `:5571` and the with-dir `if let` arm at `:1434` — correct anchors whose target text simply does not repeat the carrier sentence's words, which is the known blind spot of a token-union check rather than a bad pointer.
  - **Why the dashboard suites were not re-run, stated rather than skipped silently.** The only non-doc edit sits inside `#[cfg(test)]`, and the two doc rows are prose under a heading no client parses, so no path from this change reaches behaviour the browser suites observe — they are the regression net for the running server, and this round does not alter the server's compiled code. They were last attested green together on 2026-10-11 against a rebuilt current-source release binary, and the stale `:3900` demo image remains an open owner call.
  - **Landing cost, paid with a witness.** This file lists *Fixed* newest-first, so the entry goes above the section's existing head, which moves the nine in-file self-pointers that name a single line of this file by number. Each was re-pointed by digit bump only — every affected line keeps its length, so the file's line count and byte count are unchanged by the repair — and verified by recording the target line's text before the insert and re-reading the new line after it: identical, zero mismatches. No file outside this one points into it, so the bill was wholly internal.
  - **Three things this round deliberately did not do.** (1) **No behaviour change.** The validator's substring `..` test, its absent existence check and the 300-second `timeout` clamp are left exactly as they are; each is observable to a client, so changing one is an owner call with a recorded revert path, not a cleanup. (2) **#571 — `docs/USER_GUIDE.md:223` attributes the sandbox to `shell_exec`.** That paragraph claims capability-based isolation, no network access, a temp-directory filesystem and Windows job-object memory limits, and already concedes that "on Linux only the capability sandbox applies, because the seccomp filters are never installed". Read against the code, none of it applies here: `Sandbox::new` has exactly two construction sites, both in `src/nda_executor.rs`, and `apply_job_object_limits` has exactly one call site, inside `Sandbox::execute`; `shell_exec` builds its `Command` at `src/registry.rs:1434` and never touches either. Its real guards are the metacharacter/dangerous-pattern blocklist, the clamped timeout, the 1 MiB stdout / 256 KiB stderr caps and `validate_file_path` on the directory. So the repair is either prose matching those four, or routing `shell_exec` through the sandbox — and the second changes what every call can do, which makes it an owner decision, not a doc fix. (3) **#572 — `test_shell_exec_invalid_working_dir` (`src/registry.rs:5513`) passes for an OS-divergent reason.** It sends `/nonexistent/path/that/does/not/exist` and expects an error; on Windows that string is not absolute *to Windows*, so the refusal comes from the `is_absolute` arm of the validator, not from anything about a missing directory, and there is no existence check for the test to reach. On a POSIX leg the same literal is absolute, so the two platforms are asserting different things under one test name.
  - **The class is still ungated, which is why this one surfaced by hand.** `scripts/check_tool_schema_params.py` reconciles each declared schema against the keys its dispatch arm reads and never opens a doc, so a parameter that exists in schema and code but in no published list passes it — #564's `directory_tree` knob and #567's `workingDir` were both found by reading, not by a gate. Filed as #573: teach that script the docs direction (parse the `API.md` bullet lists and the `USER_GUIDE.md` parameter tables per tool heading), adjudicate every printing it produces before gating anything, and wire nothing into CI without approval.

- **An incoming-webhook signature check failed open: a missing signature header was treated as a valid one (2026-10-13).** `handle_incoming_webhook` verified a payload only inside an `if let (Some(secret), Some(sig))` guard, so with a secret configured and no signature supplied the guard never bound, verification was skipped, and the body was parsed and returned as an authenticated event. The guard is now a match over the (secret, signature) pair: a configured secret refuses an absent header as `Missing webhook signature` and a wrong one as `Invalid webhook signature`, while a connector with no secret is still accepted unverified because there is nothing to verify against.
  - **Scope, measured the way the divisor-zero panic in this same file was.** No HTTP route, tool, or config field reaches this function - grepping the tree for the webhook surface finds it only in its own module and its own tests, one integration template sets its config to `None`, and the whole module sits behind the `oauth2` feature, so a default build does not contain it. That makes this a library-surface defect, not a remotely reachable bypass, and it is the same reachability caveat this file already records for the connector path. The difference from the panic is that a bypass of this shape becomes live the moment somebody wires the route that is already on the owner-call list, so the refusal is placed now rather than left for that round to inherit.
  - **Two refusal reasons, not one.** The missing-header and wrong-signature arms carry distinct text. That is the lesson from the two identical warn strings on the binary-registration and pre-execution paths: a client that cannot tell a permanent host gap from a transient one cannot decide whether to retry, and a log reader cannot tell an unsigned call from a forged one.
  - **Why this was fixed rather than pinned.** Pinning tests belong to defects whose repair changes what an observable caller receives. Here no caller can reach the arm, and the fail-open has no reading under which the header authenticates anything: accepting an unsigned payload is only meaningful if the secret is absent, which is the other arm. The revert is one hunk - the six-line `if let` the match replaced, byte-for-byte in this repository's history.
  - **Non-vacuity, proved by re-applying the defect.** The new test passes on the fixed source (featured lib leg: 20 passed, 0 failed, where 19 was the previous total) and, with the old arm restored in place, fails with exactly `assertion failed: result.is_err()` and exit 101. The file was backed up outside the repo before the swap and restored by hash check after it, and the fix was then confirmed in the artifact rather than the source: both the refusal string and the new test name are present in the rebuilt test binary.
  - **Bill and gates.** The verification block was rewritten six lines for six and the test sits at end of file, so nothing above either moved. One historical cite in this file needed re-anchoring because the verification call it names shifted by a single line, done inside its own line; the entry itself is cite-free, so the census denominator holds, and the two markdown tables this round touches are untouched by it. Featured workspace: 1,243 passed / 0 failed / 55 ignored over 31 result lines; default profile: 1,170 / 0 / 55, where the change is invisible because the module is not compiled. The featured leg had not been measured since the tree that printed 1,235, and the derived 1,242 this file carried for today was one short of this run exactly as predicted, so the measurement confirms that derivation rather than falsifying it; the default 1,170 was already current and did not move, because the module this change lives in is not compiled on that profile. `cargo clippy --features oauth2 --lib -- -D warnings` and `cargo fmt --all -- --check` both exit 0. All five dashboard suites were then re-run against a debug build of this same source on a spare port, each exiting 0 - probe 6 of 6, the NDA-vs-JSON differential 22 tools with 22 payload-identical, DOM 14, CDP 5, interactive 38 - and their currency was proved before the run rather than assumed, by showing no file under `src` postdates the executable and by attributing every verdict to that server through its own log growth. They are the regression net for the two rounds of code that landed since the last such leg, not evidence about this fix: no route reaches the function, and a default-features server build does not contain the module at all. One harness dependency is worth recording because it silently changes coverage - the interactive suite starts a second, API-keyed server from the release executable path, which does not exist on this host, so the executable has to be named in its environment or the auth arm degrades to a coverage failure inside an otherwise green run.

- **The marketplace page described a listing file no code reads, a checksum format no code matches, and an install that loads nothing (2026-10-13).** `docs/MARKETPLACE.md` told plugin authors to create a `marketplace.json` for listing and showed its checksum as `sha256:...`. Measured: the filename appears in no Rust source under `src/` or any sibling crate - the index the server reads is `<current working directory>/marketplace/index.json`, because every marketplace handler builds its storage path from the literal string `marketplace` - and the checksum is compared verbatim against the archive's lowercase hex digest, so a value carrying the documented prefix can never match and the install is refused with a mismatch. The prefix case fails closed, which is why this is a documentation repair and not a security finding.
  - **The arm the doc did not carry.** Verification runs only `if !metadata.checksum.is_empty()`. An index entry with no checksum installs unverified, which is what the feature bullet's unconditional wording hid. Made an owner call rather than fixed: requiring a non-empty checksum would refuse every install from any index that omits one, and the checksum arrives in the same metadata as the URL, so mandating it buys integrity against a corrupt mirror rather than against a malicious index.
  - **The install that installs nothing the loader can see.** Files land under `marketplace/plugins/<id>`; the post-install reload then scans the literal `plugins`, while startup scans the configured `plugin_dir` (default `plugins`). Unless an operator points `plugin_dir` at the install tree, `POST /v1/marketplace/install/:id` answers `Ok` with an installed record whose tools never appear in `tools/list`. Reported, not changed: which directory the reload should scan is a configuration decision with a wire-visible effect, and it is adjacent to the standing owner call that the plugin directory has no environment override.
  - **Edits, bill, gates.** Ten in-line replacements in `docs/MARKETPLACE.md`: the feature bullet, both JSON `checksum` examples, both `install_path` examples (now the real relative shape), the listing paragraph, the plugin-tree comment, the two troubleshooting steps, and the limitations bullet. 449 lines before and after, CRLF 447 and lone-LF 2 preserved, and because nothing was inserted the two inbound anchors into this file - lines 91 and 115, cited from this CHANGELOG - did not move, so the re-anchor bill is zero rather than priced. No behaviour was changed, so no test total moved and nothing here joins the bless-or-revert batch. The five doc and script gates were run twice on the final tree and both runs exited **0** with byte-identical bodies: relative links **58 across 25 files**, markdown tables **25 files / 101 tables / 719 body rows / 0 hard / 0 advisory**, doc examples **102 fenced json blocks across 337 markdown files** with every gated example parsing and **6** generated-file blocks skipped as advisory, the schema/parameter census **16 tools with a declared schema, 16 with a production dispatch arm** agreeing in both directions, and the citation census **796 checked / 616 resolve to a populated line / 0 hard failures / 172 advisory / 8 allowed**. That advisory tally is **3** above the 169 the changelog entry above this one publishes, with the denominator unchanged at **796**: the whole shift is one key, this file's own deliberately-stale self-pointer, which the instrument reports once per citing line and which the bill bullets appended since then keep re-mentioning — **1** citing line then, **3** now, and the cited line is populated but shares no token with those bullets any more, so each mention is a token advisory rather than a broken anchor. The precedent stands and none was re-anchored; this sentence carries no citation, so the tuple it publishes is the tuple this edit leaves behind.

- **A tool that refuses every call on the default build was documented as carrying a resilience feature the codebase does not contain (2026-10-13).** `http_request` is defined unconditionally in the registry's tool list, so it is advertised by `tools/list` on every build, while its entire request path sits inside a `#[cfg(feature = "oauth2")]` block whose complement arm answers "HTTP requests require the oauth2 feature to be enabled (ureq dependency)". The root manifest sets `default = []`, so a stock build advertises the tool and refuses every call to it, and the request-shape tests and docs all described the featured build as though it were the shipped one. Nothing changed in the behaviour: the documentation now says what the binary does, and the three ways to change the behaviour are filed as owner calls, each with its revert path.
  - **The wrong-reason green this sits behind.** Three tests name the request-path guards — the unsupported-method arm and the two CRLF header-injection checks — and each asserts through a disjunction that also accepts the feature-gate error, so on the default profile all three pass without reaching the guard they claim to pin. The guards live inside the same cfg block, so the default profile has no header-injection check to reach; CI's featured leg compiles the block and does exercise them, which is why this is a *profile* gap and not an untested security path. Corrected: the assertions that can pass for the wrong reason are the ones a green suite cannot be read with.
  - **A gate that counts a dispatch arm cannot see that the arm is not compiled.** The schema-vs-parameter census recognises an arm by a quoted tool name followed by a fat arrow, and its only knowledge of cfg attributes is where the test module starts, so a feature-disabled arm counts as a production arm and "16 declared, 16 armed" stays green on a build where one of those arms does not exist. Last round's table gate was blind to a merged row because resolution is about the target line; this is the same limit from the other side — a textual gate reports on the source, not on the artifact a given feature set produces.
  - **The doc claim had no code behind it.** `docs/USER_GUIDE.md` described `http_request` as retry logic "and circuit breaker protection". Searched case-insensitively across `src/` and every sibling crate, `circuit` and `breaker` occur nowhere except as substrings of "short-circuit"; the shipped tool description never made the claim either, so this was documentation exceeding the implementation, and the task list recorded it as delivered. The section now states the retry the code implements: up to 3 retries with backoff doubling from 100ms, on transport errors and 5xx responses only.
  - **Two silent behaviours documented instead of changed.** A header whose value is not a JSON string is dropped without a trace, and because the header-name injection check sits *inside* that same string-value branch, a non-string value skips the name guard too — `to_string()`ing the value, or refusing it, changes what a caller receives, so both docs now state the drop and the choice is an owner call. And `timeout` is clamped to 300 seconds, which no doc said, while `docs/USER_GUIDE.md` printed "max: 30" for `shell_exec` — a bound the shared `min(300)` in both arms contradicts. Both parameter lists now carry the real clamp.
  - **One finding deliberately not edited.** `shell_exec` declares `workingDir` in its schema and reads it in its dispatch arm, and neither `docs/API.md` nor `docs/USER_GUIDE.md` lists it in that tool's parameters — the hidden-knob class the schema census round named, now on the documentation side. Adding a row is a line insert: measured, ten anchors in this repo point at lines below that row in `docs/USER_GUIDE.md` and one at a line below it in `docs/API.md`, so the repair is billed rather than attempted silently.
  - **Placement, bill, gates.** Every edit this round is in-line inside an existing line, so no line count moved in `README.md`, `docs/API.md`, `docs/USER_GUIDE.md` or `docs/GETTING_STARTED.md` and no anchor needed re-pointing; the entry itself is written without a single `path:NNN` span, which keeps the census denominator fixed. No Rust file changed, so no test total moved and nothing here joins the bless-or-revert batch. All five doc and script gates ran twice and every pair was byte-identical at exit 0: markdown tables, citation census, relative links, doc-example parsing and the schema-vs-arm census. Not wired into CI — that is the standing owner call on every doc and script gate.

- **A merged markdown table row is invisible to every instrument this repo had, so there is now one that sees it (2026-10-13).** New `scripts/check_md_tables.py` audits every pipe-table row against its own header's cell count, and reports a row that lost its leading pipe.
  - **Why a separate instrument: the census cannot see this defect, measured rather than asserted.** Two adjacent §9 rows of `docs/NDA_PROTOCOL.md` were merged into one line by replacing the newline between them with a ` || ` junction, and `scripts/check_citations.py` answered with the byte-identical tuple it gives on a clean tree — **796 anchors checked, 619 resolve to a populated line, 0 hard failures, 169 advisory, 8 allowed**, exit 0. The merged line still carries every anchor it carried, and every anchor still resolves, because resolution is about the *target* line, not about whether the citing line is a well-formed row. A merged row is a structure defect, and only a cell-count audit can see it: GFM drops the surplus cells silently, so the reader gets a table that no longer says what the bytes say.
  - **What it gates.** Two hard classes: a body row or a delimiter row whose cell count differs from the header, and a line that closes no pipe and does not open one yet carries exactly the header's cell count — the sibling defect, a row whose leading pipe was lost. Three advisories: a row that opens and does not close, a delimiter row with no column markers, and a run of pipe-initial lines whose second line is not a delimiter. Splitting honours the one escape GFM defines inside a row, so a backslashed pipe counts as data.
  - **Its first finding was its own bug, and it was a silent under-count, not a false alarm.** `|--------|:--:|---|` is legal GFM — two dashes in a column, alignment colons *inside* the dashes — and the first delimiter pattern required three or more dashes with the colons outside it. So the instrument declared `docs/API.md`'s method-census table "not a table" and skipped it: **21 real rows went unaudited**, and the corpus summary printed a smaller denominator than it had checked. Replacing the pattern with a per-cell delimiter test restored them; the corpus read moved from 100 tables and 697 body rows to **101 tables and 718 body rows** — 719 counting the row that records this finding. An auditor that quietly discards what it fails to parse is worse than no auditor, which is why the count is published rather than the verdict alone.
  - **The second hard class was priced before it was added.** GFM ends a table at the first blank line, so a non-pipe-initial line in a table's gap is never a legal row — but the rule had to be shown not to fire on ordinary prose. Measured across all 25 audited files: **0** non-blank lines sit in any table's post-block gap at either cell width. The class therefore has no noise floor in this corpus; it exists for the defect and nothing else.
  - **Positive control, outside the repo.** A fixture carrying one instance of each class — a merged row, a short delimiter row, an unclosed row, a lost leading pipe, a fenced block full of pipes, a pipe-initial block with no delimiter — reads **6 tables, 16 body rows, 3 hard, 2 advisory**, exit 1, with all six findings attributed to their declared line.
  - **The one pre-existing defect it reported was repaired line-neutrally.** A §9 row of `docs/NDA_PROTOCOL.md` opened a row and did not close it; the missing trailing pipe was appended, adding and removing no line and changing no cell text, so no anchor anywhere moved.
  - **Gates, at the fixed point.** `scripts/check_md_tables.py`: **25 files, 101 tables, 719 body rows, 0 hard, 0 advisory**, exit 0, and byte-identical output on a second run. On the same tree: census **796/619/0/169/8** exit 0, relative links **58 across 25 files** exit 0, doc examples exit 0 with 6 advisory blocks in generated files skipped. It is not wired into CI — that is the standing owner call on every doc and script gate.
  - **A process finding, recorded because the repo was left worse than it was found.** To run the merged-row control the mutated copy was swapped *into* `docs/NDA_PROTOCOL.md` instead of pointing the gate at the copy — which had already worked — and no byte-for-byte backup of the pre-swap file was taken first; the restore step was a silent no-op guarded by an existence check on a path that had never been created. The file is untracked, so version control had nothing to give back. It was rebuilt from the defect itself — split at the junction, closing pipe re-attached to the first half, leading pipe to the second — and verified against the pre-swap capture rather than against hope: **72,976 bytes**, SHA-256 prefix `90194bacf30c04d6`, the census row present again, no ` || ` junction left, then all four gates re-run to the tuple above. The rule this leaves: never mutate a repo file to test a gate, since a gate takes a path argument; and where a swap-in is genuinely required, capture bytes and hash first and assert them on the way out. The assertion that caught the last reconstruction error was a length check fired *before* the write, which is the only reason the repair needed no second pass.
  - **Re-anchor bill, computed before the edits and confirmed after.** This insert shifts every line below it, and `CHANGELOG.md` carries nine self-pointers at one such position; the file's own precedent declines to re-anchor them — the owning sentence disclaims the span, and re-pointing a pointer the next append breaks again only moves the rot — so they were left alone, and after the shift the pointed-at line is still populated, which is why the census stays green. In `docs/NDA_PROTOCOL.md` the table gate's §9 row is appended below the section's last row, while the only two anchors into that file sit at lines 376 and 378, above it: bill **0**.

- **`directory_tree` honored a depth knob its advertised schema never mentioned, and no client could have found it (2026-10-13).** The dispatch arm reads `arguments["maxDepth"]` (`src/registry.rs:993`: `.as_u64().unwrap_or(10).min(20)`, so default 10 and hard cap 20) while that tool's `input_schema` (`src/registry.rs:343`-`src/registry.rs:354`) declared only `path` and `excludePatterns` under `"required": ["path"]`. The parameter worked, was exercised by two existing tests, and was invisible: a client that builds its call from the schema — which is what the schema is for — cannot learn the knob exists, and a client that validates arguments against the schema would be right to reject it. `maxDepth` appeared in **zero** markdown files in the repo (`README.md:193` carries the tool name and a one-line description only), so this is not a doc that drifted from the code; the contract itself was incomplete.
  - **Audited as a class, not a one-off.** New `scripts/check_tool_schema_params.py` walks every built-in tool and compares, in both directions, the property keys each `input_schema` declares against the quoted keys its dispatch arm reads by subscript or `.get()`. Both directions matter and only one was broken here: a read-but-not-declared key is invisible to clients, a declared-but-never-read key is a promise the server does not keep. Current tree: **16** tools with a declared schema, **16** with a production dispatch arm, **0** of each kind of mismatch, exit **0**. Against the pre-repair file it exits **1** and prints `directory_tree (decl 343; arms 990 [call_tool_with_csharp_path])` / `read, never advertised: ['maxDepth']` — so the gate is proven to fire on the defect it was written for, not merely to pass without it. It is not wired into CI, consistent with the two doc census scripts that also run by hand.
  - **Two of its first findings were the instrument's own bugs, recorded so a later reader can tell the difference.** It reported *every* tool's declared key set as empty because it anchored on the JSON spelling `"name":` while the source writes Rust struct fields (`name: "directory_tree".to_string()`); the fix anchors on `input_schema: json!({` and scans upward for the literal name, which is also how it skips `register_benchmark_tools` (`src/registry.rs:121`), whose name is a `format!` and legitimately has no literal. It then flagged `edit_file`'s `newText`/`oldText` as "advertised, never read — and also required", which looked like the larger finding until the code was opened: those keys live inside `items.properties` (`src/registry.rs:403`-`src/registry.rs:406`) and the arm reads them per edit (`src/registry.rs:1139`, `src/registry.rs:1142`), so the census had been flattening nested keys into the top level and unioning every `required` array it passed. Nesting-depth-aware property collection and a receiver-agnostic key harvest killed both. Nothing was written into any doc before its target code was read, which is why this round published one finding rather than three.
  - **Intent is not inferred; it was already attested twice.** `test_directory_tree` (`src/registry.rs:4311`) passes `maxDepth: 3` at `src/registry.rs:4319`, and `test_directory_tree_depth_limited` (`src/registry.rs:4931`) passes `maxDepth: 1` at `src/registry.rs:4942` and asserts the deep file is absent — the arm's semantics were under test all along, only its declaration was missing. Independently, the Node.js comparison server implements the same contract at `bench_nodejs/server.js:109`, `Math.min(typeof args.maxDepth === 'number' ? args.maxDepth : 10, 20)` — the identical default and the identical cap in a second language, which settles what the number means instead of leaving it to guesswork.
  - **Repaired by declaring, not by changing behavior, which is why it needed no owner call.** One property line added; no validation introduced, no cap moved, no request that used to succeed now fails. The observable `tools/call` answers are identical before and after, so this is contract alignment in the category of #472 and #474, not added strictness in the category of #501/#508/#524, and it does **not** join the bless-or-revert batch. The wire effect is additive and measured rather than estimated: against the current debug build over `--mode stdio`, `tools/list` still answers **40** tools, `directory_tree` now advertises `excludePatterns`, `maxDepth`, `path` with `required: ["path"]`, its compact per-tool entry measures **575** bytes, and the added fragment costs **104** bytes. The NDA binary tool count is unchanged because both transports serialize from the same `get_tools()`; §9's 22-of-22 payload-identical differential compares those two transports against *each other*, so a schema line both legs carry cannot manufacture a divergence. No test asserts a `tools/list` byte figure and no doc publishes one, so no published number moves.
  - **The transport axis was checked, not assumed.** `directory_tree` is listed in `BUILTIN_TOOL_NAMES` (`src/registry.rs:1988`), but `execute_cached_nmcp_frame` (`src/registry.rs:2059`) has zero-alloc arms for `bench_echo` and `file_read` only, so this tool takes the JSON fallback on every transport and cannot exhibit the #562 kind of per-transport parameter divergence. That is why no same-call cross-transport differential was run for it — there is no second implementation to disagree with.
  - **New witness, and it does not stop at the declaration.** `test_directory_tree_schema_advertises_its_depth_knob` (`src/registry.rs:6300`-`src/registry.rs:6345`) asserts all three keys are advertised, asserts `maxDepth` is typed `integer`, then drives the documented ceiling: a 21-level tree called with `maxDepth: 25` must contain the `(max depth 20)` marker the arm emits at `src/registry.rs:1014` and must not contain the deepest file. The second half is the part that earns its keep, because an advertised cap that is not the enforced cap is a worse lie than no advertised cap. Negative control re-measured on the final, already-formatted file instead of inherited from the earlier run: deleting line 351 leaves **6,345** lines and the test fails on its `maxDepth` assertion, reported at line 6315 of that one-line-shorter file, i.e. the `assert!` opening at `src/registry.rs:6316` whose condition `props.contains_key("maxDepth")` sits at `src/registry.rs:6317` once the declaration is restored — with `1 failed`, leg exit **101**; the source was restored and proven byte-identical by SHA256 (`11df65a3…f90195bc` on both sides of the edit).
  - **Gates, all on the final code state.** `cargo +stable fmt --all -- --check` exit **0**; `cargo +stable clippy --lib --tests -- -D warnings` exit **0**; `cargo +stable test --lib` **765 passed / 0 failed / 38 ignored** exit **0**; `cargo +stable test --workspace` **1,170 passed / 0 failed / 55 ignored** over **31** `test result:` rows, with `REAL_EXIT_WS=0` read back out of the log rather than taken from the wrapper's exit. Against #562 that is exactly **+1** in each profile, which is this one test. The CI featured profile was not re-run, so its number is labelled derived: that leg last read **1,235 / 0 / 55** on 2026-10-12, the rounds since add six witnesses, so today's expected figure is **1,242** on this host's Windows basis and one fewer, **1,241**, on each ubuntu and macOS leg because the #560 junction witness is `#[cfg(windows)]`. The three repo doc gates ran on the final doc state: `scripts/check_citations.py` -> **796 checked / 619 populated / 0 hard / 169 advisory / 8 allowed** exit **0**; `scripts/check_links.py` -> **58** relative links resolve across **25** files, exit **0**; `scripts/check_doc_examples.py` -> every gated JSON example parses, exit **0**, with **6** advisory blocks in generated files skipped
  - **Re-anchor bill, computed before the edit and then confirmed after it.** The schema repair is line-neutral by construction — five lines replaced by five, with the `excludePatterns` block tightened to make room — and the new test is an append at end of file. `src/registry.rs` went **6,299** → **6,346** lines, LF-pure (**0** CRLF, **0** lone CR), with all 47 added lines at 6,300-6,346 and therefore below nothing and above the corpus's highest pointer into that file (**6,172**), so it bills **0** re-anchors; the citation census is the proof rather than my arithmetic, and the code-state run is not the publishable number because this round's own docs add citations: on the final tree the census reads **796 checked / 619 populated / 0 hard / 169 advisory / 8 allowed** at exit **0**, and that tuple held across **five** consecutive runs on this round's tree, each at exit **0**: the four bodies are identical except that one advisory's expected-token list grew from `excludePatterns` to four words, because the line it targets is this changelog's own bill bullet and the bullet's prose just changed — counts moved for the last time at that edit and have not moved since — #562's 759/594/0/157/8 plus the **+37** this entry and the new section 9 row contribute (+25 onto populated lines, +12 advisory, 0 hard). Two publication defects the gate caught, and one it did not: the entry's first draft cited the panic site with Windows backslash path separators, which the census parsed as a bare `registry.rs` and reported **MISSING FILE** with **1 hard failure at exit 1** — re-run at exit **0** once the corpus's forward-slash form was used; and that anchor was itself off by one, because `cargo fmt` had reflowed the one-line assert into the multi-line form, so the mutated file's line 6315 is the restored file's 6316 with its condition at 6317, and both docs now cite the re-measured pair rather than the inherited observation. What the gate could not see: the new section 9 row's append consumed the two newlines that closed the preceding row, so the frame-executor row and it became one 3,987-character line joined by `||` — a broken markdown table the citation census still reported green at exit 0. It was found by reading the file rather than its exit code, and is the second confirmation this round that a structural gate passes doc currency. `docs/NDA_PROTOCOL.md` re-measures its §9 workspace row in place at line 500 and gains one appended row at 503 (804 → 805 lines, LF-pure); **0** pointers into that file aim at 500 or below and its highest is **378**, so that file bills **0** as well. This changelog's insert at line 48 is again the only positional event, and it moves this file's own eight `CHANGELOG.md:185` self-pointers, the already-known deliberately-stale category #562 recorded.
  - **Found and left alone, because both implementations do it the same way.** `build_tree` charges its 10,000-entry node budget at `src/registry.rs:1024`, *before* the exclusion test's `continue` at `src/registry.rs:1034`-`src/registry.rs:1036`, so a skipped entry costs the same budget as a printed one and a directory whose first 10,000 names all match `excludePatterns` answers `... (entry limit reached)` (`src/registry.rs:1021`) having printed nothing. The Node peer decrements at `bench_nodejs/server.js:124` and skips at `bench_nodejs/server.js:126` — the same order, so this is shared semantics rather than a Rust divergence, and "fixing" only the Rust side would break the behavior parity the benchmark harness exists to measure. Unmeasured on purpose: proving it needs a 10,001-file directory to cross the boundary, and the ordering is already visible in both sources, so the probe would buy confidence the code read supplies for free.

- **The NMCP-frame executor keeps a second, uncapped copy of two built-in tools, and the two copies disagree on the sign of a number (2026-10-13).** `execute_cached_nmcp_frame` (`src/registry.rs:2059`) re-implements `bench_echo` and `file_read` in its two zero-alloc arms (`src/registry.rs:2105`, `src/registry.rs:2115`) rather than calling the dispatcher, and neither re-implementation carries the payload cap the dispatcher enforces for the same tool name.
  - **What each copy enforces, read side by side.** The dispatcher `call_tool_with_csharp_path` (`src/registry.rs:829`) refuses `bench_echo` above `MAX_BENCH_SIZE` (16 MiB, `src/registry.rs:1203`) and `file_read` above `MAX_FILE_READ_SIZE` (10 MiB, `src/registry.rs:929`). The frame arms call `bench_echo_impl` (`src/registry.rs:2039`, which is `Ok("x".repeat(size as usize))` with no bound) and `file_read_impl` (`src/registry.rs:2043`, which validates the path then reads the whole file with `read_to_string`). The path guard from the previous round survives on both legs; only the size gate is missing from one.
  - **The verdicts, measured by driving both legs with production framing.** New `test_binary_frame_paths_skip_the_json_payload_caps` (`src/registry.rs:6237`) builds each frame with the product's own converter (`src/registry.rs:2672`) and base64-decodes it, then calls the same request down both paths. `size: 16777217` — the dispatcher returns the `exceeds maximum` error and the frame arm returns `Ok` with a payload of 16,777,217 bytes. A 10,485,761-byte file in the temp directory — the dispatcher returns `File too large` and the frame arm returns its entire contents. The eleven pre-existing witnesses for this function pass either a malformed header or a frame of a few dozen bytes, so neither arm had ever been executed with a payload near either cap.
  - **The sign divergence is the worse half.** `tlv_to_i64` (`src/registry.rs:2012`) decodes a **signed** `i64`, and the arm casts it with `as u64` (`src/registry.rs:2107`) before the repeat, so a `size` of -1 carried in a frame demands `u64::MAX` bytes; the resulting allocation failure aborts the process, and `catch_unwind` cannot convert `handle_alloc_error` into a refusal. The dispatcher reads the same JSON number with `as_u64()` (`src/registry.rs:1202`), which yields `None` for a negative, and serves its 64-byte default instead. Both halves are asserted: `test_binary_frame_bench_echo_negative_size_reaches_the_repeat_cast` (`src/registry.rs:6282`) pins the decoded value at -1, pins the cast the executor performs at `u64::MAX`, and measures the dispatcher's answer for the identical input as exactly 64 `x`. It deliberately stops short of calling the impl with that value.
  - **Reachability, measured, and narrower than the finding looks.** The executor has exactly three production call sites: `src/registry.rs:1953`, inside the dynamic-tool arm of the dispatcher, and `src/benchmark.rs:882` and `src/benchmark.rs:974`, the `--benchmark` harness naming these two tools directly. A `tools/call` for either name matches the dispatcher's own arms (`src/registry.rs:1201`, `src/registry.rs:922`) before the frame cache is consulted at `src/registry.rs:1948`, so today's stdio, HTTP and shmem wire cannot reach the uncapped arms. An already-attested artifact corroborates that independently: section 9's NDA-vs-JSON differential reads 22 tools payload-identical over the live transports, which is what one capped dispatcher serving both legs produces. What is exposed is the published crate — `registry` is a `pub` module (`src/lib.rs:44`) and `execute_cached_nmcp_frame` is `pub`, so a consumer that executes a frame itself gets the uncapped behaviour and, for a negative size, an abort — together with the shipped benchmark. The reordering that would make this client-visible is small, which is why the finding is recorded rather than deferred.
  - **Not repaired; filed as owner call #563, crate-API-visible, with its revert path.** Option (a): hoist both constants to module scope and check them inside `bench_echo_impl` and `file_read_impl` so all four reaches share one bound; revert by deleting those two checks, which restores exactly the 16,777,217-byte and 10,485,761-byte answers pinned here and the `u64::MAX` allocation. Option (b): delete the two zero-alloc arms and let the executor's existing fallback (`src/registry.rs:2148`) re-enter the capped dispatcher — recorded as an option, not a decision, because those arms exist precisely to avoid the `Value` decode and removing them costs the harness the property it measures. Option (c), the sign bug alone, is not offered: clamping or refusing a negative changes the harness's answer for a value no in-tree caller sends today, so it rides with (a) instead of shipping as a separate "obvious" fix. Because the only observable surface today is the crate API, #563 joins the bless-or-revert batch next to #524, which is the same category of divergence at the TLV layer.
  - **Gates.** `cargo +stable fmt --all -- --check` exit **0**; `cargo +stable clippy --lib --tests -- -D warnings` exit **0**. `cargo +stable test --lib` **764 passed / 0 failed / 38 ignored** exit **0** (762 on the previous tree, so +2 is exactly these witnesses) and `cargo +stable test --workspace` **1,169 passed / 0 failed / 55 ignored** over **31** `test result:` rows, exit **0** — both re-measured on the final code state, after the last comment and assert edits, rather than derived. CI's featured `--all-targets --features http,database,oauth2` legs were not re-run: this round adds no production code and no new target, and featured re-codegen on this host is the disk-and-time event earlier entries record. The five dashboard suites are not evidence for this entry — they drive the operator's `:3900` release image, and the transports they exercise are the ones just measured as unable to reach these arms.
  - **Re-anchor bill, computed before the edit.** `src/registry.rs` went from **6,211** to **6,299** lines, LF-pure (**0** CR), and all 88 added lines sit at 6,212-6,299 inside the `tests` module — below the corpus's highest pointer into that file (**6,172**, measured across its 69 cite occurrences), so the code bills zero re-anchors. `docs/NDA_PROTOCOL.md` gains one row at the end of the section 9 table (line 502, LF-pure, 803 to 804 lines) and re-measures that table's existing workspace row in place at 500; **0** of the corpus's pointers into that file aim at line 502 or below and its highest is **378**, so that append bills zero too. This changelog's insert is the only positional event: the census read **719 checked / 557 populated / 0 hard / 154 advisory / 8 allowed** at exit **0** before, and **759 / 594 / 0 / 157 / 8** at exit **0** after — measured on the final code and docs state, not derived. The +40 checked citations are this entry plus that row; +37 of them land on a populated line and the hard-failure count is unchanged at **0**. Four of the entry's own pointers, into lines 2672, 1201, 922 and 2148 of the registry file, are advisory because a signature or match-arm line shares no word with the prose citing it, which is the same reason the corpus's other 153 advisories sit at exit 0; each of those four targets was read before being cited and is populated and correct. Advisory moved by 3 rather than 4 because the shift made one occurrence of the file's own deliberately-stale line-148 self-pointer collide with a word in its new target, a coincidental reclassification of the kind this numbering has now produced five times, not a repair.

- **The path guard's Windows reparse-point arm had no witness on the platform it guards, and the junction it was written to stop does get stopped (2026-10-13).** `validate_file_path` (`src/registry.rs:2253`) runs four checks in sequence — empty, a `..` substring test, `is_absolute()`, then a `symlink_metadata()` walk over the ancestors that refuses any component whose `file_type().is_symlink()` holds. The fourth is the one the function's own doc comment does not mention — it reads `Rejects paths that are not absolute, contain traversal sequences, or are empty.` — and the fourth is also the one with no test on Windows: the only witness was `test_validate_file_path_rejects_symlink` (`src/registry.rs:5387`), which is `#[cfg(unix)]`, so on a Windows host that arm compiled, shipped, and was never executed by the suite.
  - **The witness, and why `symlink_dir` is not the substitute.** New `#[cfg(windows)] #[test] test_validate_file_path_rejects_junction_on_windows` (`src/registry.rs:6172`) builds a real NTFS junction with `cmd /C mklink /J`, because `std::os::windows::fs::symlink_dir` requires elevation or Developer Mode while a junction is unprivileged — and `std` reports that junction as a symlink to the ancestor walk. Measured on the fixture with a purpose-built rustc probe: `is_symlink=true`, `is_dir=false`, reparse attribute `0x400` present, while `canonicalize()` of the same path returns the outside target — so the walk fires on a component that is not a symbolic link in the shell sense. The test asserts its positive control first (the same file reached *without* the reparse component is accepted), so it cannot turn green by rejecting everything, then asserts the refusal and that its message contains `symlink`. `cargo test --lib validate_file_path`: **9 passed / 0 failed / 791 filtered**, exit **0**.
  - **What the product answers, measured over stdio** against a debug build of current source: `get_file_info` and `file_read` on the through-junction path both return `Security error: Path contains symlink component: C:\Users\ian\Documents\p560\inside\link` together with the guard's two fixed advice lines; `file_read` of the junction directory itself refuses the same way; the same file behind a `\\?\` verbatim prefix is **also** refused and the component the error names keeps that prefix, so the verbatim spelling does not bypass the walk; `\\nowhere.invalid\share` clears the guard and `list_directory` answers `The network path was not found. (os error 53)`, i.e. UNC satisfies `is_absolute()` — measured for both the `\\host\share` and the `//host/share` spelling — and the **OS performs the name resolution**; a root-only `\tmp\abs.txt` is refused as not absolute; and `file_read` of the outside file **returned its contents**, which is the confinement question answered no. The probe ran on the artifact current at probe time and this round's workspace leg has since rewritten that path, so no byte size is quoted for the probed file; what makes the verdict transferable is that the shipped edit sits inside `cfg(test)` and cannot move the binary.
  - **The `..` check is a substring test, measured on a legal name.** `report..final.txt` inside an otherwise valid absolute path is refused as `File path contains traversal sequence '..'`, so any filename containing two consecutive dots is unreachable through every tool that calls this guard — seventeen call sites in `src/registry.rs` (lines 842, 845, 873, 879, 906, 924, 955, 966, 992, 1081, 1102, 1103, 1115, 1129, 1135, 1177 and 2044), `shell_exec`'s `working_dir` among them.
  - **Docs-vs-reality, repaired in place.** This file's only summary of the guard is the row `- **Path validation**: Rejects empty, relative, and traversal paths.`, which does not mention the reparse-point check, and the word `symlink` occurred **zero** times in this changelog before the present entry — the sole other mention of the function, in the Linux-platform entry, is about fixture paths and says nothing about its semantics. `docs/USER_GUIDE.md` section 13 "Path Validation" tabulated four checks and none of them was the ancestor check. That table now carries a fifth row for it, plus a paragraph stating the four Windows behaviours measured here: the substring `..` rule with its false positive, UNC and `\\?\` admission with resolution left to the OS, drive-relative refusal, and the skipped metadata read that admits a not-yet-existing write target — together with the per-component, no-configured-root scope.
  - **No root confinement for these tools, and that is a measured absence.** `allowed_paths` occurs **11** times in the tree and all eleven are in `src/sandbox.rs` — `is_path_allowed` (`src/sandbox.rs:129`), which compares canonicalized prefixes with a lexical fallback for non-existent paths, `permissive()` (`src/sandbox.rs:106`), and the single production consumer (`src/sandbox.rs:315`). `src/config.rs` returns **0** matches for `allowed_paths`, `roots`, `confine` or `sandbox`, so the confinement model that exists belongs to the plugin process sandbox and the registry's file tools never consult it.
  - **One error-detail leak on the same path.** A filesystem error out of a file tool reaches both the log and the client in the raw `io::Error` Debug form — verbatim from this probe's stderr: `Tool execution failed tool="file_read" error=Os { code: 2, kind: NotFound, message: "The system cannot find the file specified." }` — where the sanitization recorded under #211 and #215 covers the other paths.
  - **Five findings deliberately not fixed, filed as owner call #561, each with its revert path.** (1) UNC admission: the repair is a first-component refusal on a `Prefix` whose parsed form is `UNC(..)`, checked before the rest; revert by deleting that arm, which restores attacker-chosen name resolution — and note there is no analogue here of `http_request`'s SSRF blocklist. (2) A configurable root: needs a new `ServerConfig` key threaded into those seventeen call sites and would refuse paths today's users send; revert by dropping the field. (3) Narrowing the `..` test to a per-component `..` match: re-admits `report..final.txt`, which is a wire-visible widening; revert by restoring the substring test. (4) Rendering file-tool `io::Error`s through the classified, sanitized form: changes error text clients match on; revert by restoring the `?` propagation. (5) Wiring `http_request`'s blocklist semantics into the UNC case, recorded as an option and not as a decision. **Nothing in this round changes published behaviour**, because the shipped edit is a `#[cfg(windows)]` test.

- **A differential runner whose default pass is documented as read-only mutates the capability report it is measuring — and the round that proved it also found a suite quietly skipping its own auth leg (2026-10-13).** Three things came out of re-running the NDA dashboard probes on a spare port: the probe's non-passivity, the exact arm that causes it, and a stale artifact path that had been costing the interactive suite its API-key coverage.
  - **The measured mutation.** Against one spare-port server of current source, the first *default* (not `--poison`) pass of `dashboard/diff_nda.mjs` reported `capabilities before: 22 binary / 0 fallback` and `after: 21 / 1`, and the second pass reported `21 / 1 -> 21 / 1`. The downgrade therefore happened inside the first pass, on the runner's own `tools/call` loop, and persisted after it. Both passes exit **0** with 22 tools, **22 payload-identical, 0 diverged**, one `identical-but-erroring` tool (`analyze_logs_go`), and exactly **one** `no NDA-native binary executor` warn line in the server log across both passes. The tool that moved is a tool the runner calls, so nothing about the pass was read-only.
  - **Which arm does it, and why the protocol reference had it wrong.** The TinyGo-absent failure is a *registration* failure, not a binary-call failure: `call_tool_binary` takes the metadata-absent branch at `src/registry.rs:764`, and its error arm warns at `src/registry.rs:770`, records the fallback at `src/registry.rs:776` and returns at `src/registry.rs:777` — so `binary_failure_is_request_level` (`src/registry.rs:785`) and `binary_failure_is_pre_execution` (`src/registry.rs:792`) are never reached for this tool. `docs/NDA_PROTOCOL.md` row 497 asserted the TinyGo error "passes the pre-execution classifier", which the code order disproves; corrected in place. The latch is what makes the effect permanent: `record_json_fallback` (`src/registry.rs:626`) is the only writer and has no removal site, while `src/registry.rs:756` short-circuits every later call, so the verdict is per-process and restarting the server is the only re-probe.
  - **The asymmetry is an open owner call, filed as #558.** The pre-execution arm at `src/registry.rs:792` retries through `retry_binary_with_registration` (`src/registry.rs:797`) before it latches at `src/registry.rs:806`, while the registration arm latches on the first attempt with no retry. That is right for a host that genuinely lacks the toolchain and wrong for a transient one — a cached runtime evicted while its metadata survived, or a toolchain installed after startup — and a client cannot tell the two cases apart because both arms print the same warn text. Touching either arm changes what `GET /v1/nda/capabilities` advertises for the life of the process, so no behaviour was modified this round; what landed is the runner's own header comment and a `docs/NDA_PROTOCOL.md` §9.1 paragraph stating the mutation instead of implying that only `--poison` is destructive.
  - **The suite fix: `VELOCITY_DASH_EXE`.** `dashboard/test_interactive.mjs:23` built its second, API-keyed server from a hardcoded `target/release/velocity_mcp.exe`, guarded by `existsSync` at `dashboard/test_interactive.mjs:25`. With the release exe absent — and it is absent on this tree, `target/release/velocity_mcp.exe` does not exist — the guard skips the spawn and the suite prints **35 passed / 1 failed, exit 1**, the red leg being the `bad()` call at `dashboard/test_interactive.mjs:340`, whose text is `auth server failed to start — auth path untested`. That is a *coverage* report rather than a product failure, and it is how the relink blocked under #494 was silently deleting the auth tests. The constant now honours `VELOCITY_DASH_EXE` and otherwise resolves to the same release path, a one-line-for-one-line edit so this file's existing `:74` and `:27` pointers into it stayed valid. Run as `VELOCITY_DASH_EXE=$PWD/target/debug/velocity_mcp.exe` the suite reports **38 passed / 0 failed, exit 0**. Its auth server binds `--addr 127.0.0.1:3901`, which beats `dashboard/demo.toml`'s address because the CLI flag wins at `src/main.rs:156`, and it kills its own child at `dashboard/test_interactive.mjs:431` — verified, no `:3901` listener remained after the run.
  - **Verification, and the instrument that makes each verdict attributable.** All five suites ran against one debug build of current source (`target/debug/velocity_mcp.exe`, 40,583,168 B, whose mtime postdates every build input: `find src crates benches tests Cargo.toml Cargo.lock -newer target/debug/velocity_mcp.exe` prints 0 lines) served on `127.0.0.1:3911` from a config kept outside the repo whose only difference from `dashboard/demo.toml` is the address — so the CORS opening the browser suites need is preserved and the run stays comparable with the `:3900` attestations. That server's log records no request bodies, so each verdict is attributed to *it* by its own log-line growth across the run: **375 -> 521 -> 674 -> 791**. Results: `probe_nda.mjs` **6/6** (40 tools listed), `diff_nda.mjs` **22 tools / 22 identical / 0 diverged**, `bash dashboard/test_dashboard.sh` **14 passed / 0 failed**, `test_cdp.mjs` **5 / 0**, `test_interactive.mjs` **38 / 0**, exit 0 each. Latencies from a debug build are not product figures and none are published here.
  - **Operator disclosure, because the instrument caught my own mistake.** One `test_cdp.mjs` pass was launched without its endpoint argument, so it used its default `http://127.0.0.1:3900` and exercised the live demo server. The traffic was the suite's own read-only dashboard sequence — an `echo` call, a `js_calculator` call, a 50-ping burst — with no write, config change, restart or kill against that server or its image. The pass was discarded and re-run against `:3911` with an explicit endpoint and explicit Chrome path. The practice that came out of it: a suite's default endpoint is a claim about *which* server a green describes, so the log-delta check now has to run before any verdict is quoted.
  - **A second finding, filed as #559: the access log reports scope-relative paths and never sees root traffic.** `request_logger_middleware` (`src/middleware.rs:135`) is layered onto the `protected` router at `src/transport/http.rs:1564`, and that router is mounted at `src/transport/http.rs:1580` as `.nest("/v1", protected)`, while `/health` sits on the outer router (`src/transport/http.rs:1579`). Measured against the `:3911` server: `GET /v1/performance` -> 200 logs `uri=/performance status=200`; `GET /performance` -> 404 logs **nothing**; `GET /health` -> 200 logs **nothing**. So the logged `uri` cannot be replayed as a URL — pasting it into a client returns 404 — and the traffic most worth seeing, probes and scans at the root, which is exactly what a 404 is, never reaches the log at all. `docs/API.md` is not falsified by this — it documents routes, not log fields — and the check behind that sentence is measured rather than asserted: the logger's own message text `Request completed` appears **0** times across `docs/*.md` and `README.md`, so no doc publishes the field shape this finding concerns, and none needs correcting while the owner call is open. Moving the layer outward changes the `uri` value of every logged request, which is visible to anything parsing the log, so it is an owner call and nothing was changed.
  - **Gates, placement, and what this insert moves.** No production file changed this round, so no test total moved and nothing here joins the bless-or-revert batch: the diffs are one `.mjs` constant, three corrections in `docs/NDA_PROTOCOL.md`, one runner comment, and this entry. Citation census on this tree, measured immediately before this insert: 692 checked / 538 resolve to a populated line / 0 hard failures / 146 advisory / 8 allowed, exit 0; measured after it: 713 / 547 / 0 / 158 / 8, exit 0 — the +21 checked are this entry's own citations, 12 of which resolve and 9 of which are token advisories because the cited source line does not carry the sentence's words, both exit 0. The blast radius was checked rather than assumed: `docs/*.md` and `README.md` contain **0** occurrences of `CHANGELOG.md:<n>`, so no external anchor points into this file and the only positions this insert can move are internal. `CHANGELOG.md:185` is the deliberately stale self-pointer this file has declined to re-anchor four times; before this insert it appeared **7** times on **5** lines (88, 96 twice, 97, 107 twice, 251), of which **4** are census advisories and 3 resolve. This round's insert moves all seven down by 10, re-measured after it as 98, 106 twice, 107, 117 twice, 261, and adds 1 mention of its own (line 56), for 8 occurrences as the entry lands. What the insert actually did to that pointer is worse than a shift and is measured rather than argued: line 148 is now an empty inter-bullet line, so all **8** occurrences read `advisory blank` in the census where 4 did before, the 3 that resolved having resolved only because the old line 148 carried the #529 `method_name` sub-bullet. That is the drift the corpus predicts for any position anchor in a section that grows at the top, and it does not strand a reader because each owning sentence names its referent by content — but it does mean the pointer now locates nothing, so the available repair is a content anchor in place of the number, which touches only this file, has no wire effect, and is left for the owner to call for. Not re-anchored, for the same reason the four earlier rounds gave: the sentence that owns the pointer already disclaims it, and the next append to this section breaks a re-anchored position again.

- **`edit_file`'s three documented bounds all constrain the *request*, and nothing between the last replacement and the write constrains the *result* (2026-10-13).** README item 12 lists them as "edit_file resource bounds (max 1000 edits, 1MB per field)", and each is true as written — of the incoming call. The arm opens at `src/registry.rs:1120` and the only three size checks before the write are `edits` count (`src/registry.rs:1125`), the input file's `meta` length (`src/registry.rs:1132`) and each `old_text` / `new_text` field (`src/registry.rs:1146`); what sits between those gates is `content.replace` (`src/registry.rs:1158`), which rewrites **every** occurrence with no occurrence cap, and then the unconditional write (`src/registry.rs:1165`). Two consequences, now pinned by `test_edit_file_bounds_the_request_not_the_result` (`src/registry.rs:6128`) rather than argued: a 100-byte file of `a` plus **one** edit whose newText is 10,000 `b` writes exactly **1,000,000 bytes** — a 10,000x amplification out of a call that satisfies all three documented limits — and because the 10 MiB ceiling is read-gated, the tool will write a file larger than the "File exceeds 10MB limit" it refuses to open. Derived ceiling at the caps (arithmetic on the three constants, not a run): 10,485,760 readable input bytes x 1,000,000 replacement bytes = **1.05e13 bytes, about 9.5 TiB**, demanded by a JSON body of barely over 1 MB. The first observable failure at that extreme is not a slow write but an abort — the replacement buffer's capacity reservation goes through handle_alloc_error, which terminates the process, and a catch_unwind cannot intercept an abort, so README item 15's panic-catching has nothing to catch there. Reachable on every transport, each bounded only by its own request ceiling: HTTP layers `DefaultBodyLimit` on `max_request_size` (`src/transport/http.rs:1582`, key at `src/config.rs:64`, 10 MiB default at `src/config.rs:326`), stdio caps a request line at `MAX_REQUEST_SIZE` = 1,048,576 (`src/protocol/json_rpc.rs:19` — hardcoded, not the config key), and a shmem/NDA frame cannot exceed `OUTPUT_BUFFER_OFFSET` minus `INPUT_BUFFER_OFFSET` = 4,080 bytes (`src/ipc/shmem.rs:12`, `src/ipc/shmem.rs:11`). The file-size precondition needs no other tool and no privilege: `edit_file` doubles its own file (`a` to `aa`) in roughly 24 requests, each far inside every cap. The dry-run arm tells the same story in memory — it performs every replacement anyway and echoes each field into the response, so the preview costs the payload twice; the pin asserts a 100,034-byte response for a 1-byte file that stays 1 byte. **Pinned, not repaired, because a result-size bound is wire-visible** (calls that return Ok today would start returning an error), so this joins the bless-or-revert batch as **owner call #556**. The options: (a) re-check the assembled length against the same 10 MiB ceiling before writing, (b) count occurrences and multiply by the replacement length to refuse over a budget *before* allocating, (c) bless the behaviour and re-word README item 12 to say what it actually is — a request-side bound. Precedent for the shape of (a) or (b) sits later in the same file: the bench arm already caps the size it is *asked* to produce at `MAX_BENCH_SIZE` (`src/registry.rs:1203`). **Bill for a repair that inserts lines inside the arm: 26 corpus pointers cite `src/registry.rs` lines above 1,160 and every one of them shifts — 23 at the bill taken before this bullet existed, plus the three this bullet itself publishes inside that region, so the number is stated at its fixed point rather than at the moment of measurement.** This round's own edit is zero-shift: the 48-line test tail went after the highest registry line the corpus cited when the bill was taken (6,093 — that maximum is now 6,128, moved by this round's own pointer), so `src/registry.rs` is now 6,167 lines, LF-pure, sha256 f3ebd69c495a4765a23400a8eab7bc33e930f1f31ec9861f9e7332bb34b5e11b, and reverting means deleting that tail. **The pin was red-checked twice instead of asserted:** a temporary 512,000-byte cap on the assembled text flipped the write-path assertion with its own refusal string printed at the expect site, and a temporary truncation of the echoed diff flipped the dry-run assertion at 10,034 bytes; the file was then restored byte-identical to the sha above and re-greened. Gates: cargo fmt exit 0 and rustfmt left the insert alone; the test_edit_file family now reads 12 tests (11 pre-existing plus this pin) exit 0; full cargo test --lib 761 passed / 0 failed / 38 ignored exit 0, i.e. #554's 760 plus exactly this test; cargo +stable test --workspace on default features 1,166 passed / 0 failed / 55 ignored over 31 result lines exit 0 — measured, not the 1,165 + 1 derivation, though it lands on that number; cargo clippy --lib --tests exit 0 with only the known proc-macro-error2 future-incompat note. The featured profile (last read 1,235 / 0 / 55 on 2026-10-12) was not re-run and its expected +1 stays derived.  **Citation census re-run after this bullet landed: 685 checked / 535 resolve to a populated line / 0 hard failures / 142 advisory / 8 allowed, exit 0** (the buckets reconcile: 535 + 142 + 8 = 685).
