- **The tree-walk interpreter traps were the shipped instruction budget, not a guest defect, and settling that retracted one working hypothesis, exonerated the runtime, calibrated three published figures, and surfaced two silent-truncation paths in the guest that are recorded here rather than shipped.** The hypothesis carried into this round was a double-free in `bench_tools/interp_core/interp.c`'s refcount/scope discipline. The instrumented guest behaved identically to the shipped one on every sample and the `scope_free` re-entrancy detector fired **0** times, so that hypothesis is withdrawn; it had never reached a doc or a memory file, so nothing else had to be corrected for it. The discriminator was three arms over the same checked-in `bench_tools/java_wasm/java.wasm`. **A** (default 10M budget, one call per fresh runtime, no reset before the call — the shape `tests/interpreter_audit.rs` actually drives): `tiny={"message":"42"}` and `loop_only 1000={"message":"1000"}`, while `fib 16`, `fib 20`, `shallow 1000` and `loop_only 21891` all print `TRAP call failed: RuntimeError: unreachable` with a backtrace through `eval_node`/`dlfree`/`dlmalloc`. **B** (same guest, hot calls, `reset_instruction_budget()` before every call — the shape production uses at `src/wasm_runtime/mod.rs:470` and `src/plugins/mod.rs:475`, `:495`, `:590`, `:625`): `B HOT tiny reset=2000/2000 ok all`, `loop100 reset=2000/2000 ok all`, `fib10 reset=500/500 ok all`, and without the reset the same workload dies at call **#167** (`B HOT tiny no_reset=166/2000 ok call #167`). So the cumulative-depletion signature that had read as a state or memory leak is the budget carrying across calls, and no leak is implicated. **C** (same sources on an unmetered engine, `set_instruction_limit(None)` at `src/wasm_runtime/mod.rs:88`): every arm that trapped in A answers correctly — `fib 16 → {"message":"987"}`, `fib 20 → {"message":"6765"}`, `fib 25 → 75025`, `shallow 1000 → 500500`, `loop_only 60000 → 60000` — which is the whole proof, because a metering trap and a genuine guest abort are the same observable (`RuntimeError: unreachable` either way), which is exactly why `is_trap_message` (`src/plugins/mod.rs:403`) is documented as deliberately broad and why the wrong classification was available at all. A first instruction-cost table for the interpreter guests came out of a doubling budget search on the java guest: `tiny` and a 63-deep countdown need **≤1M**, `loop_only 1000` ≤**8M**, `shallow 1000` ≤16M, `fib 16` ≤32M, `shallow 5000` ≤64M, `loop_only 21891` ≤**128M**, `fib 20` ≤**256M**, `loop_only 60000` ≤512M — the `≤` is a power-of-two upper bound, so each figure traps at the previous rung. Against that, the shipped default is 10M, and the wall-clock quantum the docs attach to it was off by three orders of magnitude: a `loop_only(1000)` call consuming ~8M instructions measured **0.8 ms**, so 10M instructions buys on the order of **one millisecond** of guest compute, not the "`~1-5 seconds`" that three sites claimed (`src/config.rs:196`, `src/config.rs:343`, and the `[wasm_runtimes]` shell comment at `docs/USER_GUIDE.md:778`). All three now say about 1 ms of measured guest compute; a repo-wide grep for the old phrase returns **0** hits, and `docs/USER_GUIDE.md:792` carries a new paragraph stating that the limit is a compute budget rather than a time budget, with this ladder in it. The compiled engine is cheaper but not by enough to change the verdict: a fresh labelled ladder on `bench_tools/quickjs_wasm/quickjs.wasm` prints `QJS fib10 limit=10000000 {"message":"55"}`, `QJS fib20` trapping at 10M, 40M **and** 80M and answering `{"message":"6765"}` only at 160M, and `QJS loop21891` with the same 10M/40M/80M traps and `{"message":"21890"}` at 160M — so QuickJS needs roughly a tenth of the tree-walker's budget and still **4x** the shipped default, which makes the gap product-wide rather than an interpreter problem. Two of my own errors are named because each looked like a product defect: my first QuickJS probe called `register_tool` twice on one instance and reported `register js loop: RuntimeError{...}`, which is the register step burning metered instructions (production resets before register too, `src/plugins/mod.rs:590`), and the earlier unlabelled ladder printed only limit values with no workload name, so the only honest fix was to re-run it labelled — that re-run is what tightened the published QuickJS bound from "between 40M and 160M" to "traps at 80M, completes at 160M", and the same pass caught my own re-anchored pointer landing one line short, on the blank line above the paragraph it meant (`docs/USER_GUIDE.md:796`). Second, the guest has two paths that return a **wrong answer inside a success envelope** instead of trapping. `AST_WHILE` (`bench_tools/interp_core/interp.c:1793`) and `AST_FOR` (`:1812`) are both bounded by `while (iterations < 100000)` and then fall through to returning `last` with no error set, so on an unmetered engine `loop_only 99999` correctly answers `{"message":"99999"}` while `150000` and `300000` both answer `{"message":"100000"}` — a silent cap, not a failure; under the shipped 10M budget those calls trap instead, so the cap is only reachable once `instruction_limit` is raised above it or set to `0`. The TLV decoder has the same shape: `tlv_decode_array` and `tlv_decode_object` return `arr`/`obj` with `/* Return what we have so far */` once `element_count` passes `TLV_MAX_ELEMENTS` = 100000 (`:2778`, `:2794`), so a >100k-element argument array decodes as a valid-but-short value rather than an error. Neither is repaired here, because repairing them means recompiling the six tracked interpreter modules — an operator call, ~1.5 s per guest on this host (one java rebuild measured, 1,500 ms) — and because a guest that errors instead of truncating is a **behaviour change** to a published product, not a quiet fix. Also found, not memory-unsafe, and left: `bench_tools/interp_core/interp.c:2800` guards an object key with `key_len > TLV_MAX_STRING_LEN`, but `key_len` is a `uint16_t` and `TLV_MAX_STRING_LEN` is 10 MiB (`:2699`), so that first clause is dead — the compiler flags it once per `build.sh` run — while the buffer-bounds clause on the same line is live, which is why this is lint debt rather than a hole. The reproducibility check was the surprise of the rebuild: two consecutive builds from the same tracked sources differ at byte **270,640** and measure 276,993 / 276,994 B against the shipped `java.wasm` at 276,987 B, so the tracked `.wasm` files cannot be hash-verified against the tracked `.c`, and any supply-chain claim about them would be unfalsifiable today. The audit suite that has been reporting these traps as interpreter failures is itself the weakest instrument in the round and is measured, not judged: `tests/interpreter_audit.rs` starts a fresh runtime per language per cell (`:489`-`:497`) and registers then calls without any budget reset (`:330`, `:475`), so its five `FAIL` cells are exactly the five `call failed: RuntimeError: unreachable` results arm A predicts for `fib(20)` at 10M — php, csharp, java, r and julia under `=== Deep recursion (fib(20)) ===` — and the suite still exits **0** with `10 passed; 0 failed`, because `run_test` only prints (`:499`-`:517` as #530's label edit left it, `:499`-`:512` as it stood when measured in this round): an `Err` becomes `  {lang}: FAIL - {e}` and no assertion is possible, so the suite cannot go red on any capability loss. Worse, the same section labelled perl **OK** for `fib(20)` while printing `  perl: OK - {"error":"undefined variable 'my' at line 3"}` — the fixture at `:312` uses `my $n = $_[0];`, which the shared C core does not implement, so perl never entered the danger band at all and its green was a guest error string read as a pass. That was disclosed and, in this round, left unfixed. #530 has since measured the entire matrix (**60** cells, **15** of which were in-band-error cells printed as OK, **3** of those genuine false greens — all perl, and one per section rather than the single cell named here) and repaired the label only, so a refusal now prints `ERR-RESULT`; making `run_test` assert stays a new CI gate over checked-in guests (and metering counts are deterministic per wasm byte, so Linux CI would inherit the same five reds), which is the operator's call, as is raising the audit's limit or narrowing `test_deep_recursion` to `fib(15)`. **Doc truths repaired in the same pass, all measured from `ls` rather than carried forward:** eight of the twelve module sizes in `docs/COMPREHENSIVE_BENCHMARK.md`'s prerequisite list were stale (all six interpreter guests, plus MicroPython 587,776 → **588,146** and Lua 680,094 → **680,060**), its "sizes on disk" date label is now 2026-10-11, and the three places stating the interpreter range as 271,000 / 277,630–277,747 now publish the measured **276,888–277,005**. Both edited docs kept their line counts exactly (225 and 122), and every one of those replacements was inside a line, so no citation pointing into either page moves. **Gates:** `cargo fmt --all -- --check` exit **0**; `cargo check --all --all-targets` exit **0** (1 m 13 s); `python scripts/check_citations.py` exit **0** at 313 checked / 218 populated / 0 hard / 87 advisory / 8 allowed (218 + 87 + 8 = 313; this bullet adds 12 citations over the 301 measured before it was written, and the sentence re-ran green after the correction, so the published figure is the post-write one rather than the one the act of writing displaced); **superseded in turn — the 381 checked / 266 populated / 0 hard / 107 advisory / 8 allowed (266 + 107 + 8 = 381) was this section's running figure at the time of its own gate run, the +68 over the 313 published here being those later bullets' pointers item by item in their own gate lists, the last +18 of them the `docs/API.md` capability table's own pointers, 16 of which resolve to a populated line and 2 of which are advisory — and the `logging/setLevel` convergence bullet that closes this section now carries today's figure instead, so neither 313 nor 381 describes the current tree**; the probe file was scratch and is deleted, restoring the 8-entry untracked list. **The workspace leg was then run rather than priced, and it was not green** — see the next bullet. The clause this one first published said the leg had not been re-run, that no behaviour-bearing line had changed, and that 4.3 G of free space was not enough to relink 29 test binaries safely; the first two of those were true and the third was an estimate wearing the clothes of a measurement, and the estimate is what the next bullet is for.

- **The workspace leg I had priced from free space ran for 0.5 G once a 6905 MB build cache was reclaimed, and the first thing it caught was a test whose assertion a single clock tick can invert.** In order, because the sequence is the finding. (1) The estimate: `cargo test --workspace` was launched with C: at **4.3 G free** and reached 10 of the ~30 target lines before free space read **678 M**, with **0 failures** among the 9 completed summaries; it was killed there because the system volume had no headroom left for its own pagefile, not because anything went red. (2) The reclaim: `du -sm` put `target/debug/incremental` at **6905 MB** (regenerable, cited by nothing), and deleting it moved free space **678 M -> 8.0 G**. The retry then cost **0.5 G** (7.7 -> 7.2) to build and run all **29 test binaries**. So the leg was never capacity-blocked — it was a *cold-cache* cost, and a comment-only Rust edit pays it in full because cargo fingerprints source contents, not semantics: "no behaviour-bearing line changed" is true about the *result* and false about the *cost*. (3) The red: `rate_limit::tests::test_rate_limiter_refills_over_time` panicked on `assertion failed: !limiter.try_acquire()` — the panic located it at line 258, column 9, written there with a backslash-separated path, which is why this sentence does not reproduce it as a citation: the checker reads a backslash path as a repo pointer and reports MISSING FILE, and that was this round's only hard failure on the first run after the bullet landed. The line it named is the rejection assert, now at `src/rate_limit.rs:260` in the file this bullet describes (the insertion of three comment lines above it is what moved it; a pointer into a file this bullet also edits is exactly the rot recorded elsewhere in this section). (4) The mechanism, which is the fixture's arithmetic and not the product's: `refill` adds `elapsed_ms x rate` scaled units (`src/rate_limit.rs:92`) against a clock truncated to whole milliseconds (`src/rate_limit.rs:127`, `src/rate_limit.rs:130`), so the test's `with_limits(1000, 10)` made **one millisecond exactly one token** — the 10-acquire drain has to land inside the same millisecond as the limiter's construction for the 11th acquire to be rejected. The limiter itself is correct; the chosen rate turned a correctness assertion into a stopwatch. (5) The A/B, same protocol both sides (8 CPU spinners plus 150 process launches of the filtered test): **2 failures in 150** before, **0 in 150** after moving the constant to `with_limits(100, 10)`. Zero-of-150 is not a proof of zero probability — at the observed 1.3 % rate the expected all-green outcome is about 14 % — so the load-bearing claim is structural: at 100/s a stray millisecond buys a *tenth* of a token, and re-admission would need ~10 ms of preemption inside a microsecond-scale loop. Isolated and unloaded the old fixture was **13/13 green**, which is why three years of runs never showed it and why the flake needed a parallel workspace run to surface. (6) The same class, censused rather than assumed: five sleep sites exist in the tree's test code — `src/rate_limit.rs:261`, `src/middleware.rs:399`, `tests/e2e_smoke.rs:82`, `client/tests/shmem_integration_test.rs:79`, `client/tests/shmem_integration_test.rs:82` — and exactly one asserted a property a single tick could invert. The cache-expiry leg asserts the monotone direction (a 20 ms sleep against a 10 ms TTL can only overshoot, never undershoot) and the other three are bounded poll/settle waits. (7) Gates on the fixed tree: `cargo fmt --all -- --check` exit **0**; the lib test binary relinked in **10.43 s** for 0.1 G; `cargo test --workspace` at default features -> **1,142 passed / 0 failed / 53 ignored across 29 test binaries, exit 0**, with the main crate's `--lib` binary inside it at **751 / 0 / 38 in 26.09 s** — the same 751 that had read 750 + 1 red; `python scripts/check_citations.py` exit **0** at **332 checked / 228 populated / 0 hard / 96 advisory / 8 allowed** (228 + 96 + 8 = 332; the figure published here earlier as 327 / 223 was overtaken by the next bullet's five pointers, and reconciling each census figure to the bullets that moved it is the only way this section can stay checkable). The rows this section carries over the figure published in the bullet above are these two bullets' pointers plus the interpreter-bounds rows in `docs/wasm_runtime_status.md`, and the run that produced the published totals differs from the one that produced the intermediate 328 / 222 / 1 hard / 97 / 8 by exactly two edits: this bullet stopped quoting the panic's backslash path as a token, and one pointer in the bullet above was re-anchored off a blank line onto the assertion it describes. Both were defects this round wrote, and both were caught by the instrument rather than by reading. (8) And the number itself was a docs-vs-reality defect before it was a measurement: **1,142 had already been measured on the previous round's tree and recorded only in working notes**, never landed here, so this file's own supersession clause still published 1,141 / 1,210 and `docs/NDA_PROTOCOL.md` §9 still published 1,141 / 1,212. Two independent runs on two trees now agree on 1,142 at default features, which is the figure both places carry. The featured `--features http,database,oauth2` total stays **1,212 as a dated 2026-10-10 capture, not a current-tree claim**: re-running it rebuilds that profile, whose last measured cost left the box at 18 G free out of 448 G, and nothing this round touches a feature-gated line. The dashboard suites remain owed for the same reason they were before: they need `target/release/velocity_mcp.exe`, which is not on disk.

- **Rebuilding the release binary cost 7 m 13 s and about 1 GB, and it unblocked every leg that had been recorded as owed since `target/` was deleted — all five dashboard suites, the classifier's end-to-end A/B, and the capability row that docs had been attributing to "the release binary".** Cost first, because it contradicts the note this round inherited: `cargo +stable build --release --features http` ran 14:05:39 -> 14:12:52 (**7 m 13 s**) for **13,242,880 bytes** of exe and moved C: from 7.1 G to 7.0 G free, i.e. roughly a gigabyte, not the ~23 G that had been cited as the reason release-path verification was out of scope. Measured on that binary, as four fresh `--mode http` processes: `/v1/mcp` `tools/list` **40** tools; `GET /v1/nda/capabilities` **22 tools / 22 binary executors / 0 fallback** cold, then **21/1** after the 22-tool differential, with `analyze_logs_go` the tool that downgrades itself because TinyGo is absent from this host. That retires a doc attribution error: `docs/NDA_PROTOCOL.md` §9's row said the release binary advertises **16/6 from startup**, which was true of the one stale image still running on `:3900` and was never a property of release builds — the row is now keyed to image age, not to build profile. (1) The classifier's end-to-end A/B is no longer a unit-only claim. On a live HTTP transport the same call now answers, verbatim from the wire: `Error running tool 'analyze_logs_go': Failed to register WASM tool 'analyze_logs_go': Compilation failed: TinyGo not found. Install from https://tinygo.org/getting-started/install/` followed by `Error type: TOOL_BUILD_FAILED` and a hint that names the two real remedies. The `:3900` image still answers `NOT_FOUND` with "Check that the path or resource exists", so the before/after pair is on this machine at the same time, and the earlier bullet's `Not yet re-measured end-to-end` caveat is superseded rather than deleted. (2) Dashboard suites on current source: `probe_nda.mjs` **6/0**, `diff_nda.mjs` **22 tools, 22 payload-identical, 0 diverged** (exit 0 both), `test_dashboard.sh` **14/0**, `test_interactive.mjs` **38/0**, `test_cdp.mjs` **5/0**. The interactive suite is also an image-age control, run twice: against the stale `:3900` image it returns **36/2 with exit 1** and both red legs are the SSE framing checks, printing `0 comment / 2 data-framed`; against the current build the same two legs print `1 comment in 0.0s, 0 double-framed` and go green. So the double-framing is carried only by the old image, and the harness demonstrably discriminates it. (3) A red I created and then diagnosed rather than explained away. My first `test_dashboard.sh` run came back **6/14**, eight failures all of them `missing:` on rendered-DOM markers — and the cause was mine: the harness header says to start the server with `--config dashboard/demo.toml`, and I started a bare `--mode http`. Measured the mechanism instead of guessing: a `file://` page sends `Origin: null`, the default predicate (`src/transport/http.rs:1508`) rejects it and the preflight returns **no `access-control-allow-origin` header at all**, while the same preflight against a `cors_origins = ["*"]` server returns `access-control-allow-origin: *` and the suite goes **14/0**. Same build, same harness, one config key apart. Mid-diagnosis I also nearly filed a dead-env-var defect for `ALLOWED_ORIGINS`: it is read, just not by this binary — `grep` puts its only `std::env::var` at `crates/velocity-mcp-edge/src/main.rs:155`, while the root server's list is the config field `cors_origins` (`src/config.rs:71`, applied at `src/transport/http.rs:1478` with the wildcard branch at `src/transport/http.rs:1495` that avoids tower-http's documented panic on a `*` entry inside a list). The Edge docs that name `ALLOWED_ORIGINS` are therefore correct, and the only doc work this finding needed was in the harness's own header, where the run condition is now stated with the measured reason. (4) The metering floor re-measured on the production path rather than in a library probe: with the real binary over stdio and `VELOCITY_WASM_INSTRUCTION_LIMIT` set per process, `js_string_transform` and `analyze_logs_js` both answer `exceeded resource limits` at **1M** and **1.5M**, and both succeed at **2M**, 3M, 5M and 10M — so QuickJS's bootstrap really does sit in the 1M-2M band, which is the number a reader needs to judge the 5M-per-request figure in `docs/edge_deployment_quickstart.md` — that line previously printed "(~50ms compute budget)", a fourth instance of the time-quantum error this section corrected in three other places, and it is now replaced by the measured calibration with the reason no Edge-side figure is claimed here. (5) `health/check` over HTTP still answers `{"mode":"stdio","status":"healthy","version":"3.2.0"}` on this current build, which is task #490's defect with a live capture attached; the one-line fix is behaviour-bearing on a published crate, so it is recorded and not shipped. Gates: `cargo +stable clippy --lib --tests -- -D warnings` exit **0** (1 m 26 s), `cargo fmt --all -- --check` exit **0**, and the workspace run in the bullet above. The four probe servers I started on 3996/3998/3999/3902 were stopped by PID after the runs; the operator's `:3900` demo server was not touched and `git status --porcelain` is back to 100 modified + 8 untracked. What still keeps these legs out of CI: `dashboard/`, `docs/DASHBOARD.md`, `docs/NDA_PROTOCOL.md` and `scripts/check_citations.py` are untracked, so no pipeline runs any of them — the commit decision is what changes that, not more local verification.

- **Both CI test steps are now measured on the current tree, and the sweep that came with them found a test file describing its own fixtures as untracked when they are tracked.** Cost, stated because it is the number every earlier deferral got wrong: `cargo +stable test --workspace --features http,database,oauth2` — which is literally `.github/workflows/ci.yml:58` — ran **4 m 48 s** and consumed **1.4 G** (6.6 -> 5.2 G free) to produce **1,213 passed / 0 failed / 53 ignored across 29 test binaries, REAL_EXIT=0**, the main crate's `--lib` binary inside it at **816 / 0 / 38**. That supersedes the dated 1,212 capture, and the +1 is the same single test the default-features profile gained (`protocol::nda_native::tests::test_method_tables_cover_every_declared_opcode`): the feature delta is 71 tests in both eras (1,213 - 1,142 = 71 = 1,212 - 1,141), which is the check that the two profiles still differ by exactly the feature-gated set. (1) `tests/metering_test.rs` opened with "quickjs.wasm is local-only (not tracked in git), so tests skip when absent" and three of its guards printed `not present (not tracked in git)` — `git ls-files` says both fixtures are tracked, along with 11 other `.wasm` files, so the file was describing a gate that in fact runs in CI as a *skip*, and inviting exactly the wrong conclusion about its own coverage. The doc line and all three messages now say what is true (tracked; the `exists()` guards remain only for a pruned working tree), and the claim was tested rather than assumed: `cargo test --features http,database,oauth2 --test metering_test -- --nocapture` prints **no** `skipping` line and returns **3 passed / 0 failed in 11.29 s, exit 0**, so `test_quickjs_runs_within_default_instruction_budget` and its two trap-recovery siblings execute. (2) The wall-clock-assertion class, swept rather than sampled: two latency asserts exist in the tree's test code and neither is a stopwatch — `src/transport/http.rs:1944` reads `average_latency_us > 0.0` but the metrics it queries were seeded by `record_request(100|200|50, …)` three lines of fixture above it (`src/transport/http.rs:1914`-`:1916`), so the value is a constant, and `src/transport/http.rs:2242` asserts key presence. That leaves `test_rate_limiter_refills_over_time` as the only timing-window assertion the suite has ever carried, which is now fixed. (3) Environment surface in both directions: every doc-named `VELOCITY_*`-style identifier was matched against code (31 candidates, **0** genuinely dead — the two that looked dead are `BACKUP_DIR`, a shell variable assigned inside a documented backup script at `docs/DEPLOYMENT.md:726`, and a prose mention), and the reverse census found 33 vars the code reads with **5** absent from the docs: `QJS_ISO_MODE`/`QJS_ISO_ITERS` and `VELOCITY_REG_TABLE` (bench and PoC knobs) plus `VELOCITY_MCP_BUFFER` and `VELOCITY_MCP_SERVER`, which are real client surface. That produced a substantive finding of its own: the server's buffer path is `VELOCITY_BUFFER_PATH` defaulting to `nmcp_buffer.bin` while the shipped example reads `VELOCITY_MCP_BUFFER` defaulting to `velocity_mcp.bin`, and the client library takes the path with no default — three names and two defaults for one rendezvous file, so a reader who follows the guide's defaults and then runs the example targets a file the server never created. The pairing is now stated where the shmem recommendation lives, and the naming alignment itself is recorded as task #498 because it is behaviour-bearing on a published crate. (4) `docs/USER_GUIDE.md` §10's HTTP subsection gained the `health/check` caveat that `docs/API.md` already carried, measured again on the rebuilt release binary: `"mode":"stdio"` over HTTP, with `GET /health` answering `"transport":"http"`. Gates: `cargo fmt --all -- --check` exit 0 after the test-file edit, and `cargo +stable clippy --lib --tests -- -D warnings` exit 0 on the same edited file (a 3.56 s re-check, since only a doc comment and three string literals changed) — noting that this is the narrower scope than CI's own step, which adds `--all-targets` and the feature set; the benches were last cleared as their own task, not by this run. `python scripts/check_citations.py` exit **0** at 350 checked / 239 populated / 0 hard / 103 advisory / 8 allowed — re-run after the §9 row of `docs/NDA_PROTOCOL.md` took the same workflow pointer, and reproduced identically on the next run, so it was the fixed point at that moment — bullets added to this section afterwards moved it, and the live figure is the one stated in the newest of them. One of its own citations needed correcting before that was true: the workflow step is cited with its full dotted-directory path, and the bare filename-with-line-number I first wrote resolved as a root-level path and reported MISSING FILE — the same dotted-directory failure mode this file recorded once before in the census instrument, and the reason a path citation is written from the repo root.

- **The suite's 53 ignored tests were run: 50 pass as they stand, one of the two failures was a real hidden defect now fixed, and reclaiming the set costs about 20 seconds of CI time.** Rationale for looking: an ignored test is a gate that reports nothing, and the repo's own published totals have carried "53 ignored" for weeks without anyone asking which 53 or why. Tally first, because the number is not one number: `grep` finds **52 `#[ignore]` attributes** (38 in the main crate's `--lib` binary, 6 in `tests/real_tool_test.rs`, 8 in `client/tests/shmem_integration_test.rs`), and the 53rd comes from an `ignore`-tagged doctest at `macros/src/lib.rs:24` — so "53 ignored" in cargo's output is 52 attributes plus 1 uncompiled doc example, which is worth knowing before anyone reconciles a test count against an attribute census. Running them: `cargo test --lib -- --include-ignored` -> **787 passed / 2 failed / 0 ignored in 40.23 s** (exit 101); `--test real_tool_test -- --include-ignored` -> **6 passed / 0 failed in 3.89 s**; `cargo test -p velocity-mcp-client --test shmem_integration_test -- --include-ignored` -> **10 passed / 0 failed in 1.61 s**. So of 52 attributes, 50 pass on this tree and the two failures are different kinds of thing. (1) `wasm_runtime::go::tests::test_compile_go_to_wasm` fails because TinyGo is absent from this host — that ignore's stated premise is true, and it is also the *only* one of the 52 whose premise I could not test, since it is the one defect this section already documents client-visible (`analyze_logs_go` answers `TOOL_BUILD_FAILED`). (2) `plugins::tests::test_wasm_py_list_operations` was a genuine hidden defect, in the test: it asserted `r1.unwrap().contains("[1, 2, 3, 4, 5]")` while the plugin path serialises compactly. Confirmed against the production binary rather than inferred — `py_list_operations` over stdio returns `{"result":[1,2,3,4,5]}`, `isError` false — so the guest, the serializer and the transport are all right and the expectation encoded MicroPython's `str(list)` repr instead of the wire form. Fixed at `src/plugins/mod.rs:1513`, re-verified: **1 passed / 0 failed in 2.09 s**. It is also the slowest ignored test in the set (14.6 s when run alongside the whole binary, 2.1 s alone), which is the honest reason to expect some of these to be about timing rather than about correctness. (3) The stated reasons are both rare and wrong: exactly **13 of the 52** attributes carry an inline reason at all (nine in `src/plugins/mod.rs`, one each in `quickjs`-adjacent `typescript.rs`, `micropython.rs`, `rust.rs` and `go.rs`), and **39 carry none**. Ten of the 13 blame a missing WASM binary — "requires QuickJS/MicroPython/Lua WASM binary", "requires MicroPython WASM binary to be built first", "requires a pre-compiled Rust WASM tool" — yet 13 `.wasm` fixtures are tracked in git, which is the same class of claim this section already corrected once in `tests/metering_test.rs`; the only reason that survived contact with this host is go.rs's "Requires TinyGo installation", and the remaining failure in the set is precisely that test. (4) The one item that looked like the same defect is not: the `ignore`-tagged doctest is in a `proc-macro = true` crate, where an attribute-macro example cannot be compiled as written, and its keys were checked by hand against the parser — `name`, `description`, `param_constraints` with `min_length` and `minimum` all appear in `McpToolAttrs` / `ParamConstraints` — so it is accurate, just unenforced. Cost of reclaiming the 50: the three include-ignored runs add up to roughly 46 s against roughly 26 s for the same lib binary normally, so about **20 s of extra CI wall time for 50 more gates on both profiles**. That is a gate-set change, so it is recorded as task #499 rather than done quietly behind a "test hygiene" commit — and one of the three files involved is `client/tests/shmem_integration_test.rs`, whose 8 ignores are only runnable because `target/release/velocity_mcp.exe` exists on this host today; on a clean CI clone the build step does produce it, which is exactly the dependency its header comment records. Gates: `cargo fmt --all -- --check` exit 0 after the assertion edit; the citation census exit 0 with the two pointers this bullet adds; and because this bullet edited a Rust file, the default workspace suite was re-run afterwards rather than assumed — `cargo +stable test --workspace` -> **1,142 / 0 / 53 across 29 binaries, exit 0** in 3 m 44 s, so the published total covers the edited tree. Disk, since it is what bounds every leg here: that run plus the featured one before it took C: from 4.3 G free to **2.8 G**, and `target/debug/incremental` (measured 3817 MB, regenerable, cited by nothing) went back to **6.5 G** once removed — the same directory this section named once already, which is the recurring cost of running real suites on this box.

- **Replaying `docs/API.md`'s own examples against a live server found a classifier hole that only Windows users hit, five markdown JSON examples that are not JSON, and two stale payload version claims — and it left behind a gate to keep them from coming back.** The hole: the `NOT_FOUND` arm of `classify_tool_error` tested `"not found"` and `"No such file"`, which are the Linux spellings of ENOENT, while this host writes "The system cannot find the file specified. (os error 2)" and "...the path specified. (os error 3)" — so `file_read` on a missing absolute path answered `Error type: EXECUTION_ERROR` with the generic "adjust inputs" hint, and the identical request on Linux answered `NOT_FOUND` with "Check that the path or resource exists." The same condition, classified differently by operating system, on a product whose shmem/Windows path is a headline feature. Fixed at `src/protocol/json_rpc.rs:90` against a three-marker array, and the placement is deliberate: the const and its two new tests are appended at the end of the file, because inserting them above the function would have shifted all 45 citations this file is pointed at by three lines for a change that costs nothing to place neutrally. Both halves verified, not assumed: before/after captured on live HTTP with the same request on two builds an hour apart (`:3903` gave `EXECUTION_ERROR`, `:3904` gives `Error type: NOT_FOUND` plus the actionable hint after a release rebuild), and the unit side is `test_classify_tool_error_names_every_arm` plus `windows_enoent_spellings_classify_as_not_found` — which is why the published totals moved to **1,144 / 0 / 53** at default features and **1,215 / 0 / 53** with the CI feature set, both exit 0 over 29 binaries (lib binary 753 / 0 / 38 and 818 / 0 / 38), the +2 in each profile being exactly those two tests and the 71-test feature gap unchanged.

- **The `Error type:` / `Hint:` contract was a JSON-RPC-only feature: the shared-memory and NDA dispatchers built the prefix-only message and stopped, so a client on the binary path could not tell `NOT_FOUND` from `EXECUTION_ERROR` at all.** Found by replaying `docs/API.md`'s examples against a live server rather than by reading the dispatchers: `dashboard/classify_parity.mjs` drives five failing tool calls over both `POST /v1/mcp` and `POST /v1/mcp/nda` and compares the classification each returns — **0 of 5 matched** before the fix, **5 of 5 after**, exit 0, on one host an hour apart. The `docs/NDA_PROTOCOL.md` §9 row that promises the two paths answer a call *identically* is the contract this broke, and the 22/22 payload differential sitting above it could not have caught it because that differential exercises successful calls, where the two paths genuinely do agree. Fix is one renderer: `tool_error_text` at `src/protocol/json_rpc.rs:1666` sanitises, classifies and formats, and is now called from the JSON site, the shmem loop (`src/protocol/nmcp_binary.rs:527`) and the NDA-native arm (`src/protocol/nmcp_binary.rs:276`); `src/protocol/nmcp_binary.rs:2113` pins the equality as a unit test so the transports cannot drift again. Placement was chosen so the change costs nothing to audit: the helper and the test go at the end of their files and both call sites stay line-count-neutral, so of the citations into these two files only the 3 that named the moved `format!` needed re-anchoring, and the census confirmed it at 355 checked / 243 populated / 0 hard / 104 advisory / 8 allowed (243 + 104 + 8 = 355) on the re-run made after this bullet landed; that tally is superseded below by this section's running pointer, and a present-tense claim that the published figure is the figure the instrument prints belongs to that pointer alone. **Wire-visible consequence, which is why this is not a silent fix:** NDA and shmem error payloads are now longer by the suffix, so an error response's bytes and the Merkle root over them changed; successful-call payloads are untouched. Any consumer that pinned an exact error string or a stored root is affected, and reverting is one call-site swap if the operator prefers the old bytes. Two self-corrections on the way there, because the probe was wrong before the server was: it sliced the payload to 150 characters *before* classifying, and it read `data` where the NDA envelope writes `result`, falling back to a truncated JSON dump that made a passing case look like a mismatch (`INVALID` where the payload said `INVALID_ARGUMENTS`) — both fixed in `dashboard/classify_parity.mjs` and the 5/5 figure measured after. Dashboard legs re-run on the fixed build: `probe_nda.mjs` 6/0 and `diff_nda.mjs` 22 tools / 22 payload-identical / 0 diverged, exit 0 each. Suite totals after the fix: `cargo test --workspace` -> **1,145 / 0 / 53 across 29 binaries, exit 0** (the +1 is the parity test) `cargo test --workspace --features http,database,oauth2` -> **1,216 / 0 / 53 across 29 binaries, exit 0** (`--lib` 819 / 0 / 38, as the default run is 754 / 0 / 38), so both CI test steps are measured on the tree that carries this fix rather than inherited from one that does not.

- **The same replay sweep fixed five markdown examples that were labelled ```json but are not, and left `scripts/check_doc_examples.py` as the gate.** What the audit found across 102 fenced blocks in 336 markdown files: three schematic responses using a `{ ... }` elision (`docs/MARKETPLACE.md:91`, `docs/MARKETPLACE.md:115`, `docs/USER_GUIDE.md:376`) and two `->`/`<-` wire transcripts (`docs/USER_GUIDE.md:855`, `docs/USER_GUIDE.md:878`) were all labelled `json`, so nothing about them is pasteable; the elisions and transcripts are now `text`, which is what they are. Two blocks were genuinely broken JSON: `docs/API.md:402` had a bare `...` inside an array, now a real one-element `tools` array. And `docs/API.md:705`'s `tools/call` **error** example did not match the only format string that can produce such a message (`src/protocol/json_rpc.rs:1670` writes `Error running tool 'name': cause` then `Error type:`/`Hint:`, while the example opened with `Error type:` and said `Suggestion:`) — notably `docs/API.md:1119` in the same file documents the shape correctly, so the page contradicted itself; the example now carries the measured wire shape. `docs/API.md`'s `tools/call` **success** example also lacked `isError`, which every result carries. Two stale payload claims in tracked wiki content said `"version":"3.0.0"` where `/health`, `health/check` and `initialize` all answer `3.2.0`. The new `scripts/check_doc_examples.py` walks the repo, gates every `json` block, and exits 0; it was tested against a planted bad block in a temp tree, which it reports and fails on (exit 1), and it does not silently excuse generated content: the six remaining failures are all `<id>` templates in the committed `.qoder/repowiki` cards, printed as labelled advisories rather than hidden, because regenerating those pages would overwrite a hand edit. Like `scripts/check_citations.py`, it is run by nothing in CI yet — the operator's commit-and-wire decision, recorded as such. The sweep also triangulated the method surface rather than trusting it: the stdio dispatcher has **20** method arms and `docs/API.md`'s Method Surface says twenty with 20 table rows, both directions of the diff empty; the NDA dispatcher has **15** arms, which is the 12 `METHOD_*` plus 3 `NOTIF_*` constants and the documented opcodes `0x01`-`0x0C` and `0x10`-`0x12`. **A second transport divergence, reproduced but not yet fixed:** the same binary, the same working directory, the same `tools/call echo` — HTTP answers `plain-http` and stdio answers `Error running tool 'echo': Plugin tool timed out after 5 seconds` (`Error type: TIMEOUT`), and dropping the config file did not change either answer, so the divergence is in the transport's dispatch route, not in configuration; `plugins/` holds 20 manifests, and the stdio route appears to resolve the name against a plugin where the HTTP route resolves the native tool. Filed as #502 with the isolation evidence, because fixing it means changing tool-resolution order on a published crate. Costs of doing this: three rebuilds moved C: from 6.1 G free to 4.5 G, and a release link failed with "Access is denied. (os error 5)" because one of my own probe servers was still holding `target/release/velocity_mcp.exe` open — the exe has to be not-running to be rebuilt, which is also the shape of the operator's `:3900` constraint. Gates: fmt exit 0, `clippy --lib --tests — -D warnings` exit 0, and the census exit 0 at 350 checked / 239 populated / 0 hard / 103 advisory / 8 allowed — which also caught a latent defect in the checker itself, worth stating because only a new kind of citation could: printing the line a citation names went through the Windows console codepage, so citing a transcript line containing an arrow made the script die *after* it had computed its verdict, and the fix is to reconfigure stdout to UTF-8 with replacement, since a verdict must not depend on the console reporting it. Two protocol/config decisions are filed as #499 and #500.

- **`velocity-mcp-client` 3.2.0 — the version on crates.io — could not complete an MCP handshake over either of its two simple transports, and the notification contract that documents this shape is now enforced in all four SDK clients with the pre-fix failure of each one measured.** How it was found: `docs/CLIENT_INTEGRATION.md` states that a JSON-RPC notification gets no envelope on stdio and a `204` on HTTP, so every SDK must treat "accepted, nothing to decode" as the end of that call. Sweeping the four clients against that sentence, Rust was the last arm outstanding, and instead of polishing it the measurement turned into the largest client defect in the repo: `git show v3.2.0:client/src/transport/mod.rs | grep -c "NO_CONTENT\|notification_acked"` returns **0** — published 3.2.0 has no notification handling on either transport, while `client/src/client.rs` has sent `notifications/initialized` inside `initialize()` since 2026-08-30 (`9d4e517`). So the leg is not a niche call a user might make, it is the second message of the handshake that `client/README.md:31` and `client/README.md:77` show first. Proven rather than reasoned: the two new server-backed tests were run against HEAD's own `mod.rs` (backed up, swapped, re-verified, restored byte-exact), and HEAD is a legitimate stand-in for the release on this file — `git show v3.2.0:client/src/transport/mod.rs` and HEAD's copy are both 133 lines and differ only in `pub mod`/`pub use` order and trailing whitespace, so what that run measured is the published behaviour, not a post-release regression, which gives **1 passed / 4 failed, exit 101** — `HTTP initialize failed: Transport("error decoding response body")` (the empty 204 body is fed to a JSON decoder), `stdio initialize failed: Server("initialize did not return within 20s")` (the arm reads a line the server will never print, and the library has no timeout on that read, so it hangs — the 20 s is the gate's own `HANDSHAKE_TIMEOUT` guard, which is why the docs say hang-and-not-error), plus the two stub legs printing `error decoding response body` where an ack was expected. With the fix restored the same binary reports **5 passed / 0 failed**, 0.55 s warm. Why the suite never saw it: the client crate's 15 pre-existing tests are lib unit tests and `shmem_integration_test.rs`, so the two binary transports were the only ones ever driven against a real server, and `StdioTransport`/`HttpTransport` had never completed a handshake in any test. The mirror case was swept at the same time and each client's pre-fix shape is now on record, because "it would have failed" is not evidence: a Python client raised `AttributeError: 'NoneType' object has no attribute 'error'` (red-check via a temp copy of the module: 1 failed / 1 passed, exit 1), the Go client **panicked** with `invalid memory address or nil pointer dereference` on the missing envelope, the TypeScript client raised an untyped `TypeError: Cannot read properties of undefined (reading 'error')`, and Rust reported success or a decode error depending on the leg. All four now raise their transport-level error naming the request id — `client/src/transport/mod.rs:129`, `sdk/python/velocity_mcp_client/client.py:62`, `sdk/go/client.go:52`, `sdk/typescript/src/client.ts:58` — while a true notification still reports accepted (`client/src/transport/mod.rs:68` and the `notification_acked()` helper at `:158`). Gates added, one per client: `client/tests/notification_test.rs` (288 lines; 3 stub legs that run in CI, 2 server-backed `#[ignore]`d), `sdk/python/test_notifications.py` (67 lines — the Python SDK's first test file, 2 passed in 1.00 s), `sdk/go/notification_test.go` (231 lines, `TestSendRequestWithoutEnvelopeReportsAnError` over an always-204 `httptest` server; `GOPROXY=off go test ./...` exit 0), and `scripts/check_ts_client.py` (165 lines). That last one exists because `sdk/typescript/package.json` advertises `npm test` under jest while the SDK contains no `*.test.ts` and no jest config, so the advertised command collects nothing, and an `npm install` is not available offline: the gate copies the shipped `client.ts`/`errors.ts`/`types.ts` to a temp directory, rewrites only the import specifiers for `node --experimental-strip-types` (v24.19.0), stubs the `Transport` interface so axios is never needed, and drives the real `McpClient` through five asserts — 5 PASS / `ALL CHECKS PASSED` / exit 0, and with the guard deleted from the shipped `client.ts` it prints 2 FAIL and the `TypeError` above at exit 1, so it is proven non-vacuous on the defect it exists to catch. Two defects in this round's own instruments, both caught by re-measuring rather than trusting the first green run: the probe's `process.exit()` reproduced the same libuv abort this section already recorded for `probe_nda.mjs` (`Assertion failed: !(handle->flags & UV_HANDLE_CLOSING)` in libuv's own `src/win/async.c`) whenever stdout was an interactive pipe rather than a file, so it sets `process.exitCode` now and the abort is gone with the exit code still 0; and `cargo +stable clippy -p velocity-mcp-client --all-targets -- -D warnings` failed at exit 101 with `clippy::zombie_processes` on the new test's own HTTP spawn-timeout path, which dropped the child without waiting — fixed with `kill()`+`wait()` before the panic, and the reason CI would never have reported it is a gate-scope gap worth its own decision: `.github/workflows/ci.yml:31` runs clippy without `--workspace` while `.github/workflows/ci.yml:55` and `.github/workflows/ci.yml:58` run the tests with it, so the four sibling crates are test-gated and lint-ungated (filed as #505). Docs: `docs/CLIENT_INTEGRATION.md`'s Rust row now states that the acked-notification shape is unreleased and exactly how 3.2.0 fails on each transport, and `sdk/typescript/README.md`'s `### Test` section says plainly that jest collects nothing and names the gate that does. Suite figures, one set for the tree as it stands: **1,148 passed / 0 failed / 55 ignored across 30 test binaries, exit 0** at default features (re-run after the final lint fix, not inherited) and **1,219 / 0 / 55 across 30, exit 0** with the CI feature set, the new binary reporting 3 passed / 0 failed / 2 ignored identically in both profiles and the `--lib` binary unmoved at 754 / 0 / 38 and 819 / 0 / 38, so the feature gap is still exactly 71 and the +3/+2 in each profile is that one file; `docs/NDA_PROTOCOL.md`'s currency row carries these numbers. The ignored census moved with them and was re-measured instead of carried: 55 is 54 `#[ignore]` attributes plus the one `ignore`-tagged doctest, and **53 of the 54** now pass under `--include-ignored` (`--lib` 791 passed / 1 failed in 37.67 s, reconciling against the normal run's 754 + 38 = 792, the single red still `test_compile_go_to_wasm` with TinyGo absent; the new file's server-backed pair green against today's release exe). One citation drifted out of this round's own edit and the census caught it: the pointer to `HttpTransport::new` in `client/src/transport/mod.rs`, written years ago at the constructor's line, resolved to a blank after the two arms in this fix were inserted above it, and it is re-anchored to where the constructor now sits. The dashboard suites were not re-run: this round touched no file under `src/`, and they remain blocked on the stale `:3900` image (#494). Operator decisions filed: **#504** (release 3.2.1 so crates.io gets the handshake fix, or revert by deleting both arms and the test file), **#503** (the advertised `npm test`, and the fact that `scripts/check_ts_client.py` is wired into nothing, alongside `check_citations.py` and `check_doc_examples.py`), **#505**. Cost, since it bounds everything here: C: went from 5.3 G to 4.6 G free across the client clippy leg and the two workspace runs.

- **A cross-transport divergence I had written down as fact but not as a defect is now closed in code, and closing it cost zero re-anchored citations while producing one wire-visible behaviour change that is the operator's to bless.** The paragraph `docs/API.md` has carried since this morning said both `logging/setLevel` arms store the level; reading `src/protocol/nmcp_binary.rs:479` disproved the half I had not verified — that arm read its parameter with `unwrap_or("info")`, validated nothing, stored nothing and answered `{"result":{}}` to any string, while the JSON-RPC arm at `src/protocol/json_rpc.rs:180` refused anything outside its eight-name set. So one request with an unacceptable level was refused on stdio and HTTP (`src/transport/http.rs:407`) and silently accepted on shared memory (`src/main.rs:195`) and NDA-framed HTTP posts (`src/transport/http.rs:499`). The repair is one shared parser, `pub(crate) fn logging_set_level_response` at `src/protocol/mod.rs:55`, appended so that file grows 47 to 78 lines and the only inbound pointer into it, `src/protocol/mod.rs:19`, never moves; `LOG_LEVEL` widened from private to `pub(crate)` in place on its own line at `src/protocol/json_rpc.rs:23`; and the binary arm delegates from the same five lines it already occupied. **The JSON-RPC arm's own 29-line body was deliberately left duplicated rather than folded into the shared parser**, because delegating would delete 24 lines from a file carrying 74 inbound citation occurrences — 59 distinct target line numbers, counted with the census script's own corpus and regex, of which 60 occurrences across 48 distinct line numbers sit at 235 or later — and that re-anchor bill is the measured cost of tidiness here, so the agreement is pinned by a test instead of by construction: `logging_level_parity_tests::both_dispatchers_answer_the_same_level_request` at `src/protocol/nmcp_binary.rs:2159` drives `crate::protocol::json_rpc::handle_request` and `handle_json_shmem` with the same request object over eleven level names and asserts byte equality of the two answers. That test was red-checked before it was believed: restoring the old ack-only body made it fail on `"banana"` with `left: Object {"error": ... -32602 ...}` against `right: Object {"result": Object {}}` at `TEST_REAL_EXIT=101`, and it was restored — so the green is a comparison, not a tautology. Two of my own defects in the process, both cheap and both caught by the compiler: the module was first appended inside the file's second `#[cfg(test)] mod tool_error_parity_tests`, which compiled to `E0425 ... read_output_json exists but is inaccessible`, and the repair went to a new module at file EOF with three helpers widened to `pub(super)` at `src/protocol/nmcp_binary.rs:1708`, `:1712`, `:1716` — chosen because inserting into `mod tests` would shift line `2051`, which a citation names. And `cargo fmt` reflowed the new test at `:2117`, below that same 2051 pointer, so the reflow moves nothing. **The change is wire-visible and is an operator decision, not a quiet fix:** a client that sends an unacceptable level over shared memory or NDA-framed HTTP now gets `-32602 Invalid log level: '<x>'` where it previously got an empty-`result` ack; the revert path is the 5-line arm body at `src/protocol/nmcp_binary.rs:479`, which is the only place the old answer was constructed. What the fix does NOT change is stated in the doc paragraph it replaces: `LOG_LEVEL` now has exactly two writers (`src/protocol/json_rpc.rs:199` and `src/protocol/mod.rs:73`) and still zero readers, `notifications/message` is still emitted zero times anywhere in the tree, and the filter that controls stderr is installed once at startup from `[logging].level` / `VELOCITY_LOG_LEVEL` (`src/main.rs:125`), so this convergence aligns the *answer* on every transport without giving any of them an *effect*. **Three doc defects came out of the same sweep, one of them live-proved:** `docs/API.md` published `**Valid levels:**` as `debug`, `info`, `warn`, `error`, and piping that documented request into `target/debug/velocity_mcp.exe --mode stdio` answers `{"error":{"code":-32602,"message":"Invalid log level: 'warn'"}}` while `warning` on the same connection answers `{"result":{}}` — so the reference page taught a name the server refuses; the line now lists the eight names the code accepts (`debug`, `info`, `notice`, `warning`, `error`, `critical`, `alert`, `emergency`) and says any other string is refused, and it had to stay inside one line because citations sit below it at `:936` and `:1119`. `docs/USER_GUIDE.md:164` listed four of the eight (all valid, three missing) and now lists eight on the same line; its `### Log Levels` intro had offered `RUST_LOG` and `logging/setLevel` as one interchangeable surface, which is exactly how `warn` got sent, and now separates the env-var names from the method's, naming `warn` and `trace` as env-only. The highest inbound pointer into `docs/API.md` is `:1119`, so the capability paragraph at 1495+ was free to grow by one line, and `docs/USER_GUIDE.md` held its 1,188 lines through both edits. **Gates, all run on the fixed tree:** `cargo test --lib logging` -> **7 passed / 0 failed**, `TEST_REAL_EXIT=0`, before and after `warn` joined the parity list; `cargo fmt --all -- --check` exit **0**; `cargo clippy --all-targets -- -D warnings` exit **0** in 20.06 s; CI's own literal command `cargo clippy --features http,database,oauth2 --all-targets -- -D warnings` exit **0** in 1 m 36 s for 0.3 G (C: went 4.0 -> 3.7 G of 448 G — the featured deps were already in `target/debug/deps`, so this leg re-checked the crate rather than the world); `cargo test --workspace` -> **1,149 passed / 0 failed / 55 ignored across 30 `test result:` summaries (25 test binaries + 5 doc-test blocks)**, `WS_REAL_EXIT=0`, inside which the main crate's `--lib` block reads **755 / 0 / 38** against the 751 / 0 / 38 published earlier today — the +4 is this round's one parity test plus three that landed in earlier rounds of the same uncommitted batch and are itemised in their own bullets, so this round does not claim them, and the workspace total's other +3 over 1,142 is attributed the same way: not re-derived item by item here; **`python scripts/check_citations.py` exit 0 at 398 checked / 279 populated / 0 hard / 111 advisory / 8 allowed (279 + 111 + 8 = 398)**, which is this round's write-up and doc edits over the 381 that this section's preceding bullet published as its own latest gate run — nothing outside this round touched the markdown corpus in between — and because the sentence that names the figure introduces no citation of its own, re-running the gate after the write returned these same numbers, so the published figure is the fixed point rather than the displaced one — **and superseded in turn: the `tools/list` bullet that closes this section now carries 414 checked / 294 populated / 0 hard / 112 advisory / 8 allowed, so neither 381 nor 398 describes the current tree**.
- **The second divergence the same differential found is in `tools/list` paging, and unlike the level arm this one could make the shared-memory transport answer nothing at all.** The JSON-RPC arm's page arithmetic at `src/protocol/json_rpc.rs:216` cuts the registry at `DEFAULT_PAGE_SIZE` and emits a cursor, while this file's arm read no parameter at all and answered with the whole registry, so one `tools/list` request produced two different documents on two transports — and on the shared-memory transport the size is a hard limit, not a style preference. `src/ipc/shmem.rs:13` fixes the region at `TOTAL_BUFFER_SIZE` 65,536 bytes with the output side starting at `OUTPUT_BUFFER_OFFSET` on `src/ipc/shmem.rs:12`, so 61,440 bytes is all a response can be; past that `write_output` returns `Response length exceeds output buffer limit` at `src/ipc/shmem.rs:368`, `handle_json_shmem` propagates it, and the loop's error path at `src/protocol/nmcp_binary.rs:752` sets `STATE_ERROR` and signals the client — an error state with no response document, while stdio and HTTP keep answering pages. **The cliff was measured, not assumed**, by a temporary probe that registered minimal-name tools until the write failed and then deleted itself: the stock build answers 16 tools in 7,662 bytes, the unpaged document crosses the ceiling at 716 tools and 62,178 bytes, and the paged first page of that same oversized registry is 14,149 bytes, fits, and carries `nextCursor` "100". The repair is the same shape as the level fix and cost zero re-anchored citations: `DEFAULT_PAGE_SIZE` widened from private to `pub(crate)` in place on its own line at `src/protocol/json_rpc.rs:20`; `tools_list_page_response` appended at `src/protocol/mod.rs:88`, which grows that file 78 to 111 lines while the only pointer into it, `build_initialize_response` at `src/protocol/mod.rs:19`, never moves; and the binary arm now delegates in place at `src/protocol/nmcp_binary.rs:490`, eight lines for the eight the old body occupied. **The JSON-RPC arm was again left duplicated rather than folded into the shared helper**, for the same measured reason as the level arm above: delegating deletes ~28 lines out of a file whose 60 citation occurrences across 48 distinct line numbers sit at 235 or later. So agreement is pinned by a test: `both_dispatchers_page_the_same_tools_list` at `src/protocol/nmcp_binary.rs:2207` drives both dispatchers with the *same* request object over eight cursors — absent, `"0"`, `"1"`, `"not-a-number"`, `total`, `total + 1`, `"18446744073709551616"` and `total / 2` — and asserts byte equality in the `assert_eq` at `src/protocol/nmcp_binary.rs:2243`; it was red-checked by restoring the whole-registry arm, which failed on cursor `"1"` at `TEST_REAL_EXIT=101` before the fix went back in. **Two results from the same sweep are negative and are recorded as such.** The other three cursor-taking methods were already honest: `resources/list`, `resources/templates/list` and `prompts/list` delegate to the same `handle_resources_list`, `handle_resource_templates_list` and `handle_prompts_list` functions on both transports (`src/protocol/nmcp_binary.rs:554`, `:573`, `:601` against `src/protocol/json_rpc.rs:343`, `:366`, `:410`), so there is no third paging divergence to fix. Method *coverage* is where the next hole is: the JSON-RPC dispatcher recognises twenty names and this file's seventeen, and the two extra unconditional arms are `elicitation/create` and `roots/list`, which stdio answers with stub results while shared memory sends them to the catch-all's `-32601` (`connector/call` is excluded from that count — it is `#[cfg(feature = "oauth2")]` and so absent from the default build on both transports). That one is recorded as an open decision rather than fixed here, because the arms can only go inside the match block above line 484, and the same re-anchor bill that made the duplication the cheaper option applies to an insertion. **One doc defect came out of it:** `docs/USER_GUIDE.md:1131` published the supported set as eight method names, which is wrong for both dispatchers and taught a shared-memory reader that `resources/list` was unsupported; the line now enumerates twenty for the JSON-RPC dispatcher (with `connector/call` marked feature-gated) and seventeen for shared memory, names the wording split in the two `-32601` messages, and stayed inside one line — that file held its 1,188 lines, and the one pointer into it that predates this edit, the `setLevel` line at `docs/USER_GUIDE.md:164`, sits far above, so nothing moved. **This change is wire-visible and the operator's to bless:** a host that never sends a cursor now receives the first 100 tools plus `nextCursor` on shared memory and NDA-framed HTTP posts where it used to receive every tool in one document, so it must follow the cursor to see a large registry; the revert path is the 8-line arm at `src/protocol/nmcp_binary.rs:484`, and after reverting the parity test goes red on cursor `"1"`. **Gates, all run on the fixed tree after the probe's deletion:** `cargo test --lib tools_list` -> **15 passed / 0 failed / 1 ignored**, `TEST_REAL_EXIT=0`; `cargo fmt --all -- --check` exit **0**; `cargo clippy --all-targets -- -D warnings` exit **0**; CI's own literal command `cargo clippy --features http,database,oauth2 --all-targets -- -D warnings` exit **0**; `cargo test --workspace` -> **1,150 passed / 0 failed / 55 ignored across 30 `test result:` summaries**, `WS_REAL_EXIT=0`, inside which the main crate's `--lib` block reads **756 / 0 / 38** — each exactly one above the 1,149 and 755 the preceding bullet published, and that +1 is this round's parity test and nothing else. These legs took C: from 3.0 to 2.5 G free of 448 G. **Census: `python scripts/check_citations.py` exit 0 at 414 checked / 294 populated / 0 hard / 112 advisory / 8 allowed (294 + 112 + 8 = 414)**, which is this round's bullet and doc edit over the 398 the preceding bullet published as its own latest gate run — 398 measured again on this tree before this bullet was written, so the +16 are exactly this bullet's new citations and one bullet bought nothing stale. Of those 16, fifteen resolve to a populated line that names a symbol actually on it, and the single advisory is the shared-memory arm's opening brace line, correct as an anchor and advisory only because the gate compares one shared identifier pool per doc line and no token in this bullet is a substring of a bare `"tools/list" => {`; this sentence carries no citation of its own, so re-running the gate returns the same figure and what is published above is the fixed point, not the displaced one.

- **The thirteenth shared-memory hardening gap sits on the rejection path, not the write path, and the released Rust client turned it into a wrong answer.** `write_output_raw` at `src/ipc/shmem.rs:334` and `write_output` at `src/ipc/shmem.rs:364` returned `Response length exceeds output buffer limit` (`src/ipc/shmem.rs:368`) *before* touching `OUTPUT_LEN`, so after a rejected oversize response the length field still described the previous request's document. Three arms of the shared-memory loop call `write_output`, log the failure and set `STATE_ERROR` anyway (`src/protocol/nmcp_binary.rs:448` then `:452`, `:713` then `:717`, `:743` then `:747`), and the client's error arm reads that length straight back: `client/src/transport/shmem.rs:227` enters the `STATE_ERROR` branch, `:229` calls `read_output_len`, and `:234` renders whatever bytes it names as `Error::SharedMemory("Server error: {body}")`. So a host on the published 3.2.0 crate was shown **another request's response body as its own error** — strictly worse than the empty error it should have received, because it looks like an answer. The fix is one statement at each rejection site (`src/ipc/shmem.rs:336`, `src/ipc/shmem.rs:367`) that zeroes the length before returning `Err`, which sends the client past the `out_len > 0` guard to the generic `Server returned STATE_ERROR` at `client/src/transport/shmem.rs:237`. The invariant this closes, and the one to check on any future write path here: **a write that returns `Err` must leave the length field describing nothing**, because every reader on the far side of a state byte treats a non-zero length as payload. No client release is needed to repair this — the server-side fix covers the already-published crate, which is the rarer direction for these bugs to go in. Sending a short "response too large" error document instead of an empty `ERROR` state was considered and deliberately not done here: that changes what the wire carries and belongs in the #501/#508 bless-or-revert batch, not in a defect repair. `test_output_length_overflow_rejected` at `src/ipc/shmem.rs:496` now pins both arms (`:513`-`:521`), and it was red-checked **clause by clause**, because an `assert!` run aborts at its first failure and one combined red run would have attested only one of the two sites: restoring the `write_output` line alone failed at `:514`, restoring `write_output_raw` alone failed at `:520`, each at `REAL_EXIT=101`, and both went green again with the lines put back. `src/ipc/shmem.rs` went 669 to 688 lines, which moved exactly one published pointer — the `write_output` error literal in the bullet above, from `:366` to `src/ipc/shmem.rs:368`. That literal was the only pre-existing pointer into the file at or below the first shifted line: the corpus's other four, `src/ipc/shmem.rs:12`, `src/ipc/shmem.rs:13`, `src/ipc/shmem.rs:35` and `src/ipc/shmem.rs:115`, all sit above 334 and never moved. The input side is the mirror image and is recorded as a non-defect rather than fixed for symmetry: `write_input` at `src/ipc/shmem.rs:357` still leaves a stale `INPUT_LEN` on rejection, but the only reader of that field is the server loop after `STATE_REQ_READY`, and a host whose own write was rejected never sets that state, so no path renders those bytes.
- **`cargo test --workspace` was red on CI's own literal command, and this round's fix was not what broke it.** `both_dispatchers_page_the_same_tools_list` passed inside `--lib` (756/0/38) and failed inside the workspace run, panicking at `src/protocol/nmcp_binary.rs:2243` with `divergence for cursor "1"`, the two twenty-item pages differing by exactly one entry: `e2e_test_tool`. The mechanism is visible in the test itself — it asks the JSON-RPC dispatcher and the shared-memory arm the same question at `src/protocol/nmcp_binary.rs:2239` and `:2179`, and both read the *process-global* tool registry a moment apart — while `test_convert_and_execute_e2e` at `src/registry.rs:6027` registers a converted tool into that same global, and `register_tool_lazy` bumps `REGISTRY_GENERATION` at `src/registry.rs:144`, which invalidates `get_tools()`' cache. So the second dispatcher was answering a genuinely larger registry than the first: a test-isolation defect, not a transport divergence, and one that predates #508 — #508's parity test is simply the second full-registry reader that made it visible. The repo already names the right convention: `lock_registries` at `src/registry.rs:2740` is documented as serialising tests that "read or mutate the process-global" registries, but it lived inside a private `mod tests`, so no other module *could* follow it and the new reader did not. Two in-place widenings on existing lines — `pub(crate) mod tests` at `src/registry.rs:2732` and `pub(crate) fn lock_registries` at `src/registry.rs:2740` — make the single static `REGISTRY_TEST_LOCK` at `src/registry.rs:2738` reachable, and the reader now holds it at `src/protocol/nmcp_binary.rs:2208`. The proof is structural rather than sampled: one mutex, both sides take it, so the two bodies cannot interleave; four clean runs corroborate it. The cost was one re-anchor — the inserted line moved the parity `assert_eq` from `:2180` to `src/protocol/nmcp_binary.rs:2243`, the only pointer in the corpus into that file below 2,145, and the bullet above now reads `:2181`. **The class is now closed, and closing it needed seven tests rather than the six this bullet priced.** The seventh came from reading what the mutation actually does rather than counting calls: `load_plugins` assigns `*registry = plugins` at `src/registry.rs:100`, so a reload *replaces* the whole plugin registry instead of merely bumping the generation, which means `tools_list_plugin_reload_bumps_generation` at `src/protocol/nda_native.rs:1843` can both delete another test's freshly loaded tools and have its own `registry_generation() > gen_before` assert satisfied by a different test's bump. Each enrolment is one statement placed as the first line of the body, so every `fn` line keeps its number — `src/registry.rs:4295`, `:4353`, `:5701`, `src/protocol/json_rpc.rs:1533`, `src/protocol/nda_native.rs:1806` (`tools_list_cache_hit_and_invalidation`), `:1842`, `:1855` — and the shipped line counts moved only by the inserted lines: registry.rs 6,098 -> 6,101, json_rpc.rs 1,663 -> 1,664, nda_native.rs 3,059 -> 3,062. **The priced bill was wrong in both directions and the corrected form is measured:** "eight doc citations" undercounted the work — the true bill was 12 shifted cited lines carrying 17 occurrences in this file plus one in project memory, 18 rewrites — because the four it missed (`src/registry.rs:4302`, `:6027`, `src/protocol/json_rpc.rs:1546`, `src/protocol/nda_native.rs:1821`) are citations *inside that very sentence*, i.e. a count that excluded its own self-references. The zero-shift alternative is now measured and rejected rather than merely doubted: `rustfmt --check --edition 2021` was run on a temp file carrying both candidate shapes, merged `#[test]` + `#[serial_test::serial(name)]` attributes on one line and two merged statements on one line, and it exits **1 splitting both**, so neither survives CI's `cargo fmt --all -- --check` and the doc bill had to be paid. Two global mutators deliberately stay unenrolled, each for a stated reason: `src/transport/http.rs:1796` bumps only the generation inside a loop that retries until the watcher notices, so a concurrent bump can only help it and it registers no tool, and `tests/binary_protocol_test.rs:247` calls `load_plugins` from an integration-test crate, which cannot reach `#[cfg(test)] pub(crate) mod tests` at `src/registry.rs:2732` at any visibility — enrolling that one means making the lock a real public item, which is an API decision and not a test fix. The hammer test `tools_list_cache_survives_concurrent_registration` at `src/protocol/nda_native.rs:1856` keeps the concurrency it exists to exercise: the guard is held by the test body, its four reader threads never take it, so what the mutex excludes is other *tests*, not that test's own threads. Everything here is `#[cfg(test)]` code behind `src/registry.rs:2731`, so nothing in this repair can reach a released binary or the wire. Gates on the settled tree: `cargo fmt --all -- --check` exit **0**; `cargo test --lib` **756 passed / 0 failed / 38 ignored** exit **0**, then **ten** further repeat runs of the whole parallel lib suite in two five-run loops, all exit 0 at the identical 756/0/38 — the repeats corroborate, the proof is structural (one mutex, both sides take it, so the bodies cannot interleave), and no per-test red-check is claimed because the defect is a scheduling race that a revert would not reliably reproduce; `cargo test --workspace` **1,150 / 0 / 55 over 30 test binaries** exit **0**, and `cargo test --workspace --features http,database,oauth2` **1,221 / 0 / 55 over 30** exit **0**. The default total is unmoved at 1,150 while the featured total moves **1,219 -> 1,221**, and that +2 is the two tests that landed after the 2026-10-11 featured measurement (default went 1,148 -> 1,150 across the same window), with the two profiles still exactly **71** apart — the check that neither profile silently lost a binary. **Citation census after this repair: 506 checked, 375 resolving to a populated line, 0 hard failures, 123 advisory, 8 allowed, exit 0, with the three buckets reconciling at 375 + 123 + 8 = 506.** The run taken immediately after the guards and re-anchors printed 506 / 373 / 0 / 125 / 8, and the two extra advisories were manufactured by this bullet: it pointed at two nda_native fn signature lines whose test names the prose did not quote, and because the gate clears a pointer as soon as any identifier from any backticked span on the doc line occurs on the cited line, an unquoted function name reads as an advisory rather than a wrong pointer. Quoting those two test names in the prose cleared both, 125 down to 123 with no line of code touched, and the tally published here is the re-run taken after this sentence existed — the instrument has to be run twice, because the sentence that explains an advisory usually adds the citation that moves the count. Against the 496 / 364 / 0 / 124 / 8 that the interp.c bullet below published before this repair, checked is up 10 and populated up 11, and this bullet alone now carries 23 of the 506 pointers — that is what enrolling seven tests and re-anchoring their citations costs.

True of that JSON arm, false of the transport: the NDA-native binary arm still emits the whole registry (`src/protocol/nmcp_binary.rs:199`), which is the still-open #515. Rewritten inside the same four lines to say which arm pages and which does not, so the two pointers into the match block at `:490` and `:496` did not move.

Five of those thirty-one sit on this round's bullets and are named here rather than left for a human to find: four are the first bullet's re-quotations of the corpus's four pre-existing pointer targets into that file — the two output-buffer size constants, the `#[link]` attribute line and the event-name `format!` line — correct anchors that merely share no token with the sentence quoting them, and the fifth is the second bullet's `src/registry.rs:144` anchor, which points at the bare `bump_registry_generation();` call site and is advisory only because that bullet's prose carries no token the call line does. **The headline tally is the fixed point, and getting to it took two measured runs:** an earlier draft of this sentence published 453 checked / 326 populated / 0 hard / 119 advisory / 8 allowed, which the first bullet made stale the moment it quoted four more pointers, and the rewrite that moved it to 457 checked first shifted one populated item into the advisory column (457 checked / 325 populated / 0 hard / 124 advisory / 8 allowed) because it had dropped the call-site identifier from this line.
