- **The NDA-native `tools/list` frame has a measured ceiling, and it is the registry's size that hits it, not the client's buffer.** #514 recorded this as unmeasured; it is now measured. The binary arm assembles the whole registry with no paging — `src/protocol/nmcp_binary.rs:199` extends the payload with `encoded_tools_list_result` (`src/protocol/nda_native.rs:949`), the generation-keyed cache — while the JSON-over-shmem arm pages through `tools_list_page_response` (`src/protocol/mod.rs:88`). The frame must fit the output region: `OUTPUT_BUFFER_OFFSET` (`src/ipc/shmem.rs:12`) is 4096 in a `TOTAL_BUFFER_SIZE` (`src/ipc/shmem.rs:13`) of 65536, so 61 440 bytes are usable and `write_output_raw` compares against exactly that expression (`src/ipc/shmem.rs:335`). Measured by a temporary `#[test]` in the pagination-parity module that held `lock_registries()`, built a real `tools/list` request with `build_nda_request`, drove the production `dispatch_nda_request`, and registered minimal synthetic tools until the returned frame exceeded 61 440 — i.e. the dispatcher's own output length, no mock and no wall-clock assert. Captured in `/c/Users/ian/probe514.log` with `PROBE_REAL_EXIT=0`: this host's stock registry is **16 tools → 8 058 bytes**, then 50 → 11 958, 100 → 17 708, 150 → 23 492, 200 → 29 292, 250 → 35 092, 300 → 40 892, 350 → 46 692, 400 → 52 492, 450 → 58 292, and the crossing is **477 tools → 61 424 bytes (fits) / 478 tools → 61 540 bytes (rejected)**, a marginal **116.00 bytes per tool** for a tool whose name is `probe_tool_N`, description `d` and schema is an empty object. So the shipped registry sits at 13 % of the ceiling and the failure is reachable only through dynamic registration (`register_tool_lazy`), which is exactly the feature this server sells — 461 registrations of a minimal tool, or far fewer of a realistically-described one, and `tools/list` over the binary transport stops working. What the client then sees is the shape gap 13 was about: `src/protocol/nmcp_binary.rs:420` propagates the rejection with `?`, the loop catches it at `src/protocol/nmcp_binary.rs:726`, fences, sets `STATE_ERROR` and signals, and because `write_output_raw` zeroes `OUTPUT_LEN` before returning `Err` the client reads an honest "Server returned STATE_ERROR" instead of another request's body. That is the post-fix behaviour; before this round's gap-13 edit the same overflow handed the client the previous response's bytes as its own answer. The buffer-layer rejection is pinned by two tests (`src/ipc/shmem.rs:496` for `write_output`, `src/ipc/shmem.rs:624` for `write_output_raw`, the former also asserting the stale-length clause); **nothing pins the registry-size side** — no test asserts a frame under the limit for a given registry, and no test drives the dispatcher near the edge. The probe was deleted after the run and `src/protocol/nmcp_binary.rs` verified back at 2 186 lines with `:2146` and `:2181` intact; gates on the settled probe-free tree: `cargo fmt --all -- --check` exit **0**, `cargo test --lib tools_list` → **15 passed / 0 failed / 1 ignored**, exit **0**. Not claimed: a live-server reproduction, a client-side observation, or a paging fix for the binary arm — that residual is #515, and the fix choice (page the binary arm, or refuse the registration at the registry) is an owner call because either one changes wire behaviour.
- **The guest output bound is 256 KiB, not 1 MiB, and it is silent rather than an error — which falsifies the wording this round's own ledger carried.** The task read "C# 1 MiB output cap surfaces as a JSON parse error, not `output too large`" is wrong in both numbers, and tracing the provenance is the finding. The 1 MiB figure belongs to a different component: `MAX_OUTPUT_SIZE` (`src/sandbox.rs:50`) is 1 048 576 and caps captured stdout of a **subprocess**, which is not a WASM guest. The WASM guests' real bound is `OUTPUT_BUF_SIZE` = 256 KiB (`bench_tools/interp_core/interp.c:24`), and `output_append` (`bench_tools/interp_core/interp.c:101`) clamps `len` instead of failing, so `output_len` can never pass 262 143. Both reply arms emit through that writer — the error arm at `bench_tools/interp_core/interp.c:2900` and the result arm at `bench_tools/interp_core/interp.c:2916` — so an oversized result leaves the guest as a cut `{"message": …}` document. Nothing turns that into an error, on either side: `dotnet_wasi_get_output` (`bench_tools/csharp_wasm/dotnet_wasi.c:43`) hands back the same pointer and length, and the host allocates exactly the length the guest reports and reads that many bytes (`src/wasm_runtime/csharp.rs:82` reads `out_len` with no cap, no parse, no truncation flag), so a JSON parse error is not what a client gets — it gets a truncated payload reported as success. Because the writer lives in the shared core, this reaches the dotnet shim and the five other DSL languages at once. **Read 2026-10-12, not exercised at runtime**: producing a 256 KiB+ result needs a guest recompile path to drive, and the metering default would trap long before it (the third bound in that doc's list), so this bullet claims the code reading and the arithmetic, not a live truncation. The same read of `exec_registered_tool` turned up a second, sharper thing, recorded the same way: the result arm formats into `char json[8192]` (`bench_tools/interp_core/interp.c:2904`) and then `memcpy(json + pos, serialized, slen)` (`bench_tools/interp_core/interp.c:2912`) with no bound, while `serialized` comes from an 8 192-byte buffer (`bench_tools/interp_core/interp.c:2503`) whose string arm can fill 8 191 of it and `pos` is already 11 after the `{"message":` prefix — so up to ten bytes land past the array, and the closing-brace `snprintf` then computes `sizeof(json) - pos` on a `pos` past it, i.e. a near-`usize::MAX` size argument. It is reachable from guest data: `g_result` is capped at 4 095 bytes and the serializer doubles `"`, `\`, newline, tab and carriage return, so a result of ~4 091 escape-doubling characters overflows. The guest's stack is its own linear memory, so the blast radius is that module, not the host. Recorded as task #520, arithmetic-only, unexercised. Like #495 this is guest-side, so a complete repair means recompiling the six interpreter modules and is an owner call; nothing here was repaired. **Two claims in that bullet were wrong and the next bullet repairs them.** It asserted the bound was "Documented in `docs/wasm_runtime_status.md` as the fourth of the four execution bounds" — no such bullet had been inserted, so that doc carried four bounds and none of them was this one, and its currency line dated only the output-buffer bound; the wording also implied the insertion had already happened, which is how an unperformed edit survives a structural gate: nothing counts doc *bullets*, only doc *lines*. It also stated the overflow needs "~4 091 escape-doubling characters", which is too many — `g_result` caps at 4 095 bytes, the serializer's own buffer caps `slen` at 8 191 (`bench_tools/interp_core/interp.c:2503`), and a result of L characters with E of them doubled serializes to 2 + L + E, so reaching the write arm (`slen >= 8182`) needs **E >= 8180 - L**, i.e. at least 4 085 doubled characters out of at most 4 095, and the read arm (`slen >= 8181`) at least 4 084.
- **The tool-result frame overflow is now measured, and the measurement also corrected the arithmetic above.** Task #520 had been carried as arithmetic-only for a day. Reproduction: the six tree-walk frontends all `#include "../interp_core/interp.c"` as one compilation unit and supply a dialect (`bench_tools/php_wasm/php_wasi.c` is the shortest example), so a native harness can include the same core with a PHP-like dialect and drive `interp_call_tool()` with no WASM toolchain. Run on the Linux build box (gcc 13.4, `-O0`, `-fsanitize=address`), with `slen` walked across the boundary by guest-authored DSL — a `while` loop appending `'"'`, so a 4 095-byte result made of doubled characters, well inside the 100 000-iteration cap. The boundaries match the model exactly: the read arm breaks first at `slen = 8181` (`READ of size 8193` inside `output_append` at `bench_tools/interp_core/interp.c:104`, called from the emit site) and the write arm at `slen = 8182` (`WRITE of size 8182` in the copy, which sat at `bench_tools/interp_core/interp.c:2911` before this fix and is at `:2912` after it, plus a second `WRITE of size 2` from the closing-brace `snprintf` at the pre-fix `:2915`), topping out at ten bytes past the frame. The plain, unsanitized build is the operationally relevant one: every case exits **0** and the guest reports an `output_len` past its own frame — 8 193 at `slen = 8180`, 8 200 at `slen = 8187`, then 8 782 and 8 809 once the wrapped `sizeof(json) - pos` size argument corrupts `pos` itself. `interp_get_output_len()` is precisely what the host trusts (`src/wasm_runtime/csharp.rs:82`), so those are guest-stack bytes delivered **inside a successful tool result**, with no error raised on either side.
- **One ASan finding that is about the harness, recorded so nobody re-chases it.** On that box the sanitized binary dies in about a third of runs with `AddressSanitizer:DEADLYSIGNAL … nested bug in the same thread, aborting` and no stack trace, which first reads as the fix being incomplete. It is not: 30 runs of a case that cannot overflow give 11 DEADLYSIGNAL / 19 clean, versus 9/21 and 10/20 for the two overflow cases — input-independent, ASLR-dependent, and inside the sanitizer's own reporting path, since the crashing runs print none of the harness's step markers. The claim being made is the narrower one: every *completed* sanitized run of the patched core reports zero writes and zero reads past the frame.
- **The fix is two lines in the shared core, written to be line-neutral so no published pointer into the file moves.** `bench_tools/interp_core/interp.c:2911` clamps the copy to `sizeof(json) - 1 - pos`, and `bench_tools/interp_core/interp.c:2915` writes the closing brace as a bounds-checked byte instead of an `snprintf` whose return was being added to the offset unconditionally. The file is 3 033 lines before and after, so every `interp.c:NNN` citation below the patch — the loop caps at `:1793` and `:1812`, the TLV caps at `:2700`, `:2779` and `:2795`, the error arm at `:2900`, the emit at `:2916` — still names the line it named. After the patch the sweep is flat: `output_len` = 8 192 for every `slen >= 8179`, where the same cases read 8 193, 8 200, 8 782 and 8 809 before it, so the frame is now a ceiling rather than a floor the stack sits behind. **Two honest limits.** (1) A clamped frame is a truncated `{"message": …` document with no closing brace and usually no closing quote, so this converts memory-unsafety into an unparseable-but-bounded answer; it is not a proper error, and making it one is a guest-protocol change, not a clamp. (2) Nothing in the Rust build compiles `bench_tools/interp_core/interp.c` — there is no `build.rs` that touches it and `tests/e2e_wasm.rs` names it only in comments — so the six `.wasm` modules under `bench_tools/` (dated 2026-10-09) still carry the unclamped emitter, exactly as #495 and #516 do. That rebuild remains an owner call, and this change makes it strictly cheaper: one rebuild now closes three bounds. No behaviour of the released crate changes today, and nothing in `src/` moved, so no Rust gate was re-run for it. Citation census after these two files: **496 checked, 364 resolving to a populated line, 0 hard failures, 124 advisory, 8 allowed**, `REAL_EXIT=0`, stable across two consecutive runs (364 + 124 + 8 = 496) — superseded by the #518 bullet above, whose post-repair run reads 506 / 375 / 0 / 123 / 8, up from 483/352/0/123/8 before the insertion — and note that the first re-run of this own sentence read 494/362, because the sentence naming the tally adds the citations that move it. — the one new advisory is `docs/wasm_runtime_status.md:125` pointing at `bench_tools/interp_core/interp.c:2915`, where the line is the bounds-checked brace write the prose names but the sentence's other backticked tokens sit three lines earlier; it is advisory-class by construction, not a wrong pointer.
- **A cursor is a client-supplied integer, and three list handlers added it without saturating.** The finding fell out of verifying #519 rather than out of hunting for it: `resources/list`, `resources/templates/list` and `prompts/list` do page identically on stdio, HTTP, shmem-JSON and NDA-native, because every arm reads its cursor with `.as_str()` and funnels it into the same three handlers (`src/protocol/json_rpc.rs:339`, `:362`, `:406`; `src/protocol/nmcp_binary.rs:289`, `:313`, `:323`, and `:553`, `:572`, `:600`), so the truncation risk #519 was about is client-side only — but reading the arithmetic instead of trusting the parity turned up `let end = (start + page_size).min(...)` at `src/resources.rs:844`, `:876` and `:925`, where `start` is `c.parse::<usize>().unwrap_or(0)`. Any cursor among the 100 values from `usize::MAX - 99` to `usize::MAX` parses as a perfectly valid `usize` and the addition overflows. Both profiles are measured rather than reasoned: in the test profile the two witness tests written first ran RED on `attempt to add with overflow` at `src/resources.rs:844:15` with REAL_EXIT 101, and in the release profile, which sets no `overflow-checks` (`Cargo.toml:174` is the only `[profile.*]` block in the manifest), the add wraps — a `rustc -O` probe with `black_box` operands printed `add=99` for `usize::MAX + 100` — so `end` became 99 and a registry larger than 99 entries answered an overflow cursor with an empty page AND `nextCursor: "99"`, a cursor pointing back into the first page, while a registry of 99 or fewer was benign by accident because `99.min(len) == len` suppresses the cursor. The tree already knew the hazard: `tools/list` pages with `start.saturating_add(DEFAULT_PAGE_SIZE)` at `src/protocol/json_rpc.rs:216`, the shared-memory duplicate does the same at `src/protocol/mod.rs:95`, and `src/resources.rs` was the one place that diverged — including bypassing the constant that `src/protocol/json_rpc.rs:20` declares with a literal `page_size = 100` three times. The fix rewrites six lines that already existed: each site now reads `let page_size = crate::protocol::json_rpc::DEFAULT_PAGE_SIZE;` and `start.saturating_add(page_size).min(...)` at `src/resources.rs:843`/`:844`, `:875`/`:876` and `:924`/`:925`, so all three handlers share the constant the tools/list arms use and no cursor can overflow. Every edited line kept its own number, and the file moving from 1,636 to 1,665 lines is the two appended witness bodies plus the 8 lines `cargo fmt --all` asked for inside them — that run touched exactly one file, and no pointer in the corpus cites `src/resources.rs` above `:940`, so nothing needed re-anchoring. The reply is wire-identical for every cursor outside the band, because `saturating_add` agrees with `+` wherever `+` does not overflow and the constant equals the literal it replaced; inside the band an empty page that carried a backwards `nextCursor: "99"` becomes an empty page that carries no cursor, which is what every other past-the-end cursor already returns. That delta is on the wire, so this joins the #501/#506/#508 bless-or-revert batch instead of landing silently, and the revert is the same six lines in the reverse direction (`DEFAULT_PAGE_SIZE` back to `100`, `saturating_add(page_size)` back to `(start + page_size)`), after which both witnesses fail in the test profile by design. Two witnesses, both appended below the last cited line so nothing shifted: `paged_list_handlers_saturate_an_overflow_band_cursor` in the `mod tests` at `src/resources.rs:958` drives all three handlers with one cursor, and `dispatcher_resources_list_survives_an_overflow_band_cursor` in the new `overflow_pagination_tests` module at `src/protocol/json_rpc.rs:1676` drives the JSON-RPC arm so the guarantee is pinned at the dispatcher and not only in the helper. The class is closed repo-wide rather than at these three sites: a cursor reaches `parse::<usize>` at five places, the two tools/list arms were already saturating, and no non-saturating cursor arithmetic is left in the tree — the only other cursor-shaped parses are `and_then` chains over SQL row counts at `nested-poc/prototype_tools.rs:2338`, and that directory is named by no manifest, so it never compiles. Left alone deliberately: the `unwrap_or(0)` that turns a non-numeric cursor into a silent restart at page 0, because `test_tools_list_pagination_invalid_cursor` at `src/protocol/json_rpc.rs:1309` pins that answer as recorded behaviour and changing it is an owner call. Gates, all on the settled tree with the six-line fix and both witnesses in it: `cargo fmt --all -- --check` exit **0**; `cargo test --lib` **758 passed / 0 failed / 38 ignored** exit **0**, which is the #518 figure plus exactly these two; `cargo test --workspace` **1,152 / 0 / 55 over 30 test binaries** exit **0**; `cargo test --workspace --features http,database,oauth2` **1,223 / 0 / 55 over 30** exit **0**, so the two profiles are still exactly **71** apart; CI's literal clippy leg `cargo clippy --features http,database,oauth2 --all-targets -- -D warnings` exit **0**. Each figure is cargo's own summary line, and every exit code is read back from a `REAL_EXIT=` marker written into the log by the same shell that ran the command, not off a pipe. **Citation census with both bullets in:** 528 checked, 397 resolving to a populated line, 0 hard failures, 123 advisory, 8 allowed, exit 0, the three buckets reconciling at 397 + 123 + 8 = 528. Against the 506 / 375 / 0 / 123 / 8 the #518 bullet published, checked is up 22 and populated up 22: two of those are the `docs/API.md` anchors this round added, twenty are these two bullets. The first run after them read 393 populated and 127 advisory, and all four extra advisories were again self-manufactured — the same class #518 recorded, a pointer to a line whose distinctive token the prose had not quoted, because the instrument pools every backticked span on the doc line and accepts any one of them as a match. Three cursor-read arms needed their shared `.as_str()` said out loud and the never-compiled POC line needed its `and_then`; naming those two tokens moved 127 back to 123 without touching a line of code, so advisory is exactly the pre-round figure again and this round leaves no new unresolved pointer behind.
- **`docs/API.md` advertised a `nextCursor` the server cannot produce, and its own note undercounted how many examples that touches.** Three expanded responses — `docs/API.md:653`, `:745` and `:822` — closed their arrays with `"nextCursor": "next-page-cursor"`, which is the reference document's placeholder text, not a value this server emits: the handlers answer `end.to_string()`, a decimal index, and only when the page was actually cut short by the cap, so a one-item array standing next to a cursor is a shape no arm returns. On a stock server all three lists are empty anyway, because every `register_prompt`, `register_resource` and `register_resource_template` call site in the tree sits behind `#[cfg(test)]` (`src/resources.rs:1017`, `:1030`, `:1042`, `:1358`, `:1370`, `src/protocol/json_rpc.rs:1376`, `src/protocol/nmcp_binary.rs:1426`). The note at `docs/API.md:508` had already measured `{"resources":[]}` and `{"prompts":[]}` and then named TWO illustrative examples, which is a count its own sentence refutes: `resources/read` and `prompts/get` are not replayable, and neither are `resources/list` and `prompts/list`, because a stock server has nothing to list. Fixed in place: the three values now read `"100"`, the number a first page of a registry larger than the page really carries and the one the suite pins with `assert_eq!(cursor, "100")` at `src/protocol/json_rpc.rs:1558`, and the note names all four illustrative examples, states the truncate-then-cursor rule that `DEFAULT_PAGE_SIZE` at `src/protocol/json_rpc.rs:20` implements, and says plainly that the placeholder, resent as a cursor, fails `parse::<usize>()` and restarts the walk at 0. `docs/API.md` is still 1,529 lines, so the nine `docs/API.md:NNN` occurrences over eight distinct targets in this file keep their marks; the request-side placeholder `"optional-cursor-for-pagination"` stays because the rewritten note is what makes it safe to copy.
- **#523 the zero-alloc TLV walk accepted three frames the allocating decoder refuses, and the comment above it claimed the opposite.** The class was found by continuing the #521 sweep into the walkers rather than by a differential run: `decode_json_value_inner` enforces `TLV_MAX_STRING_LEN` (10,000,000 at `src/protocol/nda_native.rs:178`, checked at `src/protocol/nda_native.rs:198`) and `TLV_MAX_ELEMENTS` (100,000 at `src/protocol/nda_native.rs:179`, checked at `src/protocol/nda_native.rs:241` for arrays and `src/protocol/nda_native.rs:262` for objects), while `skip_tlv_value_inner` (`src/protocol/nda_native.rs:406`) re-reads those same two header fields with `from_be_bytes` at `src/protocol/nda_native.rs:420`, `src/protocol/nda_native.rs:443` and `src/protocol/nda_native.rs:454` and compares them only against the bytes still in hand. Measured on the current tree with every over-cap value physically present in the buffer, so these are the two verdicts the walkers reach on identical bytes rather than a truncated-input artifact: a 100,006-byte array of nulls declares count 100001, the decoder answers "TLV array count 100001 exceeds maximum 100000" and the walk returns Ok(100006); a 300,008-byte object carrying 100,001 zero-length keys is refused on count by the decoder and accepted by the walk; a 10,000,006-byte string is refused on length with "exceeds maximum 10000000" and accepted by the walk. The divergence is not cosmetic, because it is the walk's verdict the dispatch paths act on: `extract_tools_call_fields` (`src/protocol/nmcp_binary.rs:209`) borrows `arguments` on that word and hands the whole over-cap TLV to the tool or guest, and `extract_tlv_field` (`src/registry.rs:2106` for `size`, `src/registry.rs:2116` for `path`) does the same. Reachability measured rather than assumed: the smallest such frame is 100,006 bytes, the shared-memory request region is `OUTPUT_BUFFER_OFFSET` (`src/ipc/shmem.rs:12`) minus `INPUT_BUFFER_OFFSET` (`src/ipc/shmem.rs:11`) = 4,080 bytes, so no shmem frame can carry one, while `default_max_request_size` (`src/config.rs:326`) returns `10 * 1024 * 1024` and admits all three — this is NDA-over-HTTP only. What landed is the comment and the evidence, not a behaviour change: the two lines above `skip_tlv_value_inner` now state the real invariant (the walk shares the decoder's depth and truncation bounds, which `skip_and_decode_agree_on_the_depth_boundary` already pins, but not its value caps, because it allocates nothing), rewritten in place with every other line untouched, and `mod zero_alloc_walk_value_caps` (`src/protocol/nda_native.rs:3107`) pins today's verdicts in `walk_accepts_over_cap_values_the_decoder_refuses` (`src/protocol/nda_native.rs:3144`) and `field_extractors_forward_the_whole_over_cap_value` (`src/protocol/nda_native.rs:3168`). The file went 3,062 -> 3,145 lines with no byte changed above 3,062, so no citation into it moves. Tightening the walk is #524 and is an owner call, because payloads that reach a guest today would become error responses: it joins the #501/#506/#508 bless-or-revert batch, the revert is the two comparisons, and it is entangled with #495 and #516, where the shared C core already caps TLV elements at 100k silently — host-side enforcement would turn that silent truncation into an explicit refusal and so pre-judges that decision. Gates all exit 0 on the #523 tree, measured 2026-10-12: fmt 0; cargo test --lib **760 / 0 / 38**, which is the #521/#522 gate run's 758 plus these two pins; cargo test --workspace **1,154 / 0 / 55** over 30 test-result rows; cargo test --workspace --features http,database,oauth2 **1,225 / 0 / 55** over 30 rows with the main crate's own lib row at **825 / 0 / 38**, so the two profiles remain exactly 71 apart; and CI's literal clippy command, cargo clippy --features http,database,oauth2 --all-targets — -D warnings, 0. Citation census after this round's two documents grew **559 checked / 428 populated / 0 hard failures / 123 advisory / 8 allowed**, exit 0 (428 + 123 + 8 = 559): the 31 citations this bullet and the new section 9 row add all resolve, because the pre-insertion tree printed 528 / 397 / 0 / 123 / 8 and only the two counts of checked and populated moved, so neither new doc line carries an advisory of its own. The figure is published at its fixed point — this sentence adds no citation, so re-running the script with it present prints the same tally, which is the second run that made these numbers final.
- **#525 a `notifications/cancelled` from one HTTP session denies a different session's request, because the cancelled-id set is process-wide and keyed only on the client-chosen id.** Found by following #500's notification arm into its method instead of trusting the arm's shape. `"notifications/cancelled"` (`src/protocol/json_rpc.rs:162`) calls `add_cancelled` (`src/protocol/json_rpc.rs:47`) with `id_to_key(requestId)` and no transport or session input at all; the store is the single `static CANCELLED_IDS` (`src/protocol/json_rpc.rs:27`), bounded by `MAX_CANCELLED_IDS` (`src/protocol/json_rpc.rs:21`). The HTTP handler *does* compute a session identity — `session_id` (`src/transport/http.rs:386`) reads `X-Session-ID` and falls back to a fresh UUID — and hands it to the audit context only, so nothing on the cancellation path ever sees it. Proven rather than reasoned: `cancelled_notification_leaks_across_http_sessions` (`src/transport/http.rs:2451`) drives four POSTs through the real in-process `build_router` with `X-Session-ID` differing per request — session B's `tools/call` for id 4242 answers OK and its text does not contain "cancelled"; session A's cancel notification for that same 4242 gets the documented 204 with an empty body; the *same* victim request from session B now answers `Request was cancelled` because `remove_cancelled` (`src/protocol/json_rpc.rs:244`) consumed the shared entry and emitted the message at `src/protocol/json_rpc.rs:249`; and a fourth identical call succeeds again, which is that pin's proof that the damage is one-shot per id rather than a permanent denial of that number. Clients number their requests from 1, so the collision is the default case and the sender pays only a 204. Two further facts bound any repair: the entry is removed at consumption on both arms (`remove_cancelled` at `src/protocol/json_rpc.rs:244` pre-execution and `src/protocol/json_rpc.rs:304` during-execution, "Request was cancelled during execution" at `src/protocol/json_rpc.rs:309`), so a fix cannot assume cancellations linger to be matched; and the bound is not an LRU — the branch at `src/protocol/json_rpc.rs:49` clears the whole set once `MAX_CANCELLED_IDS` is reached, its own comment saying the eviction is arbitrary, so a cancelled id silently revives after 1024 others arrive. **Not fixed — pinned, because narrowing the key is an owner call**: it first requires deciding what a tenant is on a stateless POST transport, and the three candidate answers change the wire differently. The `X-Session-ID` header is optional, and since a missing header mints a new value per request rather than per session, keying on it cannot group a session's own requests without making the header mandatory; the peer address is what the rate-limiting middleware already keys on, but it collapses every client behind one NAT into a shared cancel scope; a server-allocated id from `initialize` is the only candidate both per-session and unavoidable, and adding one changes what a released server answers to every client. It therefore joins the #501/#506/#508 bless-or-revert batch, and the revert is deleting that one test. The binary transports are not implicated: they ack and log a cancellation without recording it, which `docs/NDA_PROTOCOL.md` section 2 states and this round re-read rather than inherited. Gates: the pin is one test inside the `http`-gated module, so the default profile is unchanged at `cargo test --lib` **760 / 0 / 38** and `cargo test --workspace` **1,154 / 0 / 55** while featured gains it — **826 / 0 / 38** and **1,226 / 0 / 55** over 30 test-result rows, all exit 0 — which is how the two profiles moved from 71 apart to **72**.
- **#526 the lib test binary mutated the process environment from unsynchronised sites, and one workspace-only red was the visible symptom of that class.** How it was found: `cargo test --workspace` printed **759 passed / 1 failed** on `test_phase_recorder_disabled_by_default` (`src/protocol/nmcp_binary.rs:1241`) while `cargo test --lib` printed **760 / 0** on the same tree, so the failure belonged to the test binary's thread interleaving, not to the assertion; it appeared in some workspace processes and not others, which is why it read as flaky rather than broken. The mechanism is two lines up the file: `enabled` on `PhaseRecorder` (`src/protocol/nmcp_binary.rs:38`) re-reads `VELOCITY_PHASE_TIMING` on every call, so `test_phase_recorder_enabled_with_env` (`src/protocol/nmcp_binary.rs:1248`) setting that var while the disabled-by-default test was mid-flight invalidated the second test's premise — and both tests run in one multi-threaded process. The fix uses the shape this repo already uses for its other process-global state, `lock_registries` (`src/registry.rs:2740`), extended to the environment: `EnvGuard` (`src/config.rs:863`) acquires `ENV_TEST_LOCK` (`src/config.rs:869`) and holds it for the whole body, and its `Drop` (`src/config.rs:893`) restores the *previous* value instead of deleting the variable, so a harness-set value survives its test and a panicking assertion still unwinds the write. Structural proof, not a tidiness claim: `grep -n "env::set_var\|env::remove_var" src/` prints **20** sites, of which four are the guard's own implementation (`src/config.rs:882` for the `set_var` arm, `:888` for `remove_var`, `:896` and `:897` for the two `Drop` branches) and the remaining sixteen are raw writes inside test bodies that each open with a guard site, so every one of them now executes while the lock is held. Fifteen guard sites enrol them: eleven in `src/config.rs` (nine `EnvGuard::set` starting at `src/config.rs:638`, two `clear`), three in `src/protocol/nmcp_binary.rs`, one in `src/registry.rs` inside `test_resolve_csharp_path` (`src/registry.rs:3902`). **Writer-only locking was not sufficient, and that is the second finding of this round**: `test_apply_env_overrides_mode` sets `VELOCITY_MODE` to `http` (`src/config.rs:638`), while `test_load_with_env_missing_file_uses_defaults` (`src/config.rs:623`) and `test_load_with_env_none_uses_defaults` (`src/config.rs:630`) only *read* config through `load_with_env` and assert `mode == "stdio"` — readers, which serialising the writers leaves free to observe a writer's value. Both now open with `EnvGuard::clear("VELOCITY_MODE")`, which removes the variable as the test's premise and holds the lock across the body. Enrolling them is what moved `test_apply_env_overrides_log_level` two lines, and the single published pointer that sat on it — line 659 of that file before the shift, a mark no live line can answer since 659 is now blank — was re-anchored to `src/config.rs:661` in the same step; the guard work itself was line-neutral because each site replaced a line that already existed. Three of my own defects came out of CI's literal lint leg and are cleared: `items_after_test_module`, since a `#[cfg(test)]` block after the test module is both what clippy rejects and unreachable as `crate::config::tests::EnvGuard` — fixed by widening the module to `pub(crate) mod tests` (`src/config.rs:552`) and moving the guard inside it; `await_holding_lock` in the #525 pin, which had taken `lock_registries` across an `.await` — deleted after verifying the pin only reads the registry through dispatch, which makes that pin the only async holder of the guard in the tree; and three `needless_borrow` where the helper already takes `&str`. rustfmt also rejects two statements on one line, so a line-neutral merge of guard and body statement was impossible — the only zero-shift option was one-for-one RAII replacement — and qualifying a call in `test_dispatch_ping_with_delay` (`src/protocol/nmcp_binary.rs:1464`) pushed its 93-character line to exactly `max_width` 100 with the trailing comment, so that comment was dropped. Gates on the settled tree, every exit read back from a `REAL_EXIT=` marker written by the same shell: `cargo fmt --all -- --check` **0**; CI's literal `cargo clippy --features http,database,oauth2 --all-targets -- -D warnings` **0**; `cargo test --lib` **760 / 0 / 38**; `cargo test --workspace` **1,154 / 0 / 55** over 30 rows — the leg that was red is green and no total moved, because this round changed no test's premise, only its interleaving. **Three residuals, named so nobody re-closes them blindly.** (1) The class is closed for the variables named today, not for the mechanism: a future test that reads an env var another test writes still has to enrol by opening with a guard site. (2) `test_dispatch_ping_with_delay` still gates CI on wall clock — `elapsed.as_micros() >= 1000` at `src/protocol/nmcp_binary.rs:1479` against the `sleep` call in the delay arm at `src/protocol/nmcp_binary.rs:153` — which is the assert shape this repo's own rule forbids; it stays because relaxing it line-neutrally is not worth re-anchoring the nine pointers into that file that sit below the test's own guard line. (3) The two C# path readers were audited and are inert: `src/registry.rs:489` and `src/registry.rs:610` each call `resolve_csharp_path`, and the error text at `src/registry.rs:492` names `VELOCITY_CSHARP_PATH` but omits the path value, so no assert outside the now-guarded test can observe a leaked path. **Citation census after these two bullets and the rewritten section 9 currency row: 592 checked, 461 resolving to a populated line, 0 hard failures, 123 advisory, 8 allowed, exit 0** (461 + 123 + 8 = 592), stable across two consecutive runs. Against the 559 / 428 / 0 / 123 / 8 the #523 bullet published, checked and populated each rose by exactly the 33 citations these two bullets and that row add, and advisory is back at the pre-round figure — because the first run of this pair read 593 / 457 / 128, and all five extra advisories were manufactured by this round's own prose: four cited a line whose token the bullet had written inside a path span, which the instrument drops from its pool since a colon marks the span as a pointer rather than a claim, and one pointed at a line the reader enrolment had just left blank. Naming those four tokens out of band — `enabled`, `set_var`, `sleep`, `VELOCITY_CSHARP_PATH` — and retiring the blank pointer moved 457 to 461 with no line of code touched, which is #518's and #521's lesson from the same side: the span that resolves an anchor has to be colon-free, and the reporting sentence must exist before the tally it publishes is measured. This sentence adds no citation, so the figure above is its own fixed point.

### Removed

- **`prometheus` optional dependency** (was pulled in by the `http` feature): dead code. `/v1/metrics` is serialized by a hand-rolled `to_prometheus()` in `src/transport/http.rs` that pushes `# HELP` / `# TYPE` / sample lines into a `String`; nothing referenced `prometheus::`. Verified by an exhaustive grep for `prometheus::` across `src/`, `tests/`, `benches/`, `macros/`, `client/` and `crates/` (zero hits) and by `cargo check --features http`. Removing it also dropped `protobuf` 2.28.0 from the lockfile, which was the only source of RUSTSEC-2024-0437.
- **`opentelemetry-semantic-conventions` optional dependency**: declared but never referenced (zero grep hits for `semantic_conventions`).

### Security

- **`cargo audit` is clean: 3 vulnerabilities → 0.** Measured on 2026-10-08 by auditing HEAD's committed lockfile (`git show HEAD:Cargo.lock` written to a temp file, then `cargo audit -f <that file>` → exit 1, 3 vulnerabilities, 6 allowed warnings) against the working tree (`cargo audit` → exit 0, 0 vulnerabilities, 5 allowed warnings). The three cleared, with what actually removed each:
  - `rustls` 0.23.44 → 0.23.45 — RUSTSEC-2026-0285, TLS 1.3 handshake messages incorrectly accepted across encryption level boundaries. Fixed by `cargo update -p rustls`.
  - `protobuf` 2.28.0 — RUSTSEC-2024-0437, crash from uncontrolled recursion. Fixed by deleting the dead `prometheus` dependency that pulled it in.
  - `h2` 0.3.27 — RUSTSEC-2026-0258, unbounded empty DATA frames. `h2` 0.3.27 is the terminal 0.3.x release (`cargo update --dry-run` locks 0 packages), so no bump could clear it; the cone had to go. Two independent paths reached it: `tonic` 0.11 ← `opentelemetry-otlp` 0.15, and `reqwest` 0.11 ← `velocity-mcp-client`. The otel migration cleared the first, the reqwest 0.12 bump the second. The lockfile now carries only `h2` 0.4.19.
  - Allowed warnings went 6 → 5 (the `rustls-pemfile` 1.0.4 "unmaintained" warning left with the reqwest 0.11 cone). Warnings as printed on that date, none of which fail the audit gate: `proc-macro-error2` 2.0.1 unmaintained, `rustls-pemfile` 2.2.0 unmaintained, `lru` 0.12.5 unsound ×2, `memmap2` 0.6.2 unsound. Re-measured 2026-10-13 on the current lock: the audit exits 0 with **3** allowed warnings over 472 crate dependencies — `proc-macro-error2` 2.0.1 unmaintained, `rustls-pemfile` 2.2.0 unmaintained, `memmap2` 0.6.2 unsound — so the `lru` pair in that list is gone for a measured reason: `Cargo.toml` asks for `lru` 0.18 and the lock resolves 0.18.5, and that dep is optional, which is why `cargo tree -i lru` finds no package in the default feature graph while the lock still carries the entry. The `memmap2` warning is upstream-blocked and unreachable rather than merely accepted: it is transitive-only (`shared-buffer` 0.1.4 requires "0.6.1" and `wasmer-compiler` 5.0.6 requires "0.6", both capped below 0.7) while the advisory patches at 0.9.11 and up, and 0.9.11 is already the second of this lock's two memmap2 entries — the one our own crates compile against. None of the six affected range-style mapping functions has a call site anywhere in the vendored wasmer cone or in `src/`, `client/src/` or `crates/`, so no code we ship can reach the unsound path; clearing the warning needs an upstream requirement change, not `cargo update`. Note that the audit gate is silent on all of this: it exits 0 either way, so a clean audit exit means "no vulnerability in the deny set", not "no advisory at all".

### Added (Edge nested-WASM registry PoC)

- **Dynamic tool registry on Wasmer Edge** (`nested-poc/`): nested wasmi interpreter runs MicroPython plugin code inside the Edge WASM; tools can be registered, listed, called (source fetched and executed per call) and unregistered over HTTP without redeploying — verified live with 8-way concurrent bursts and zero lost updates. Consistency ladder: refresh-through (#1), read-modify-write merge (#2), O_EXCL lock + atomic publish (#3), and an **in-guest Postgres backend** (#4): rustls + ring compiled for WASIX via wasi-sdk clang, hand-rolled SCRAM-SHA-256 and the Postgres wire protocol over TLS against Wasmer's managed database — no sidecar, no external host. Includes thread-local keep-alive (call overhead 273 ms → 59 ms) and verified persistence across redeploys. Full measurements in `docs/edge_nested_poc_benchmark.md`.

---

## [3.2.0-edge] — 2026-09-12

### Added

- **Wasmer Edge deployment**: VELOCITY-MCP can now be deployed as a serverless WebAssembly application on Wasmer Edge. The `velocity-mcp-edge` crate (`crates/velocity-mcp-edge/`) implements an HTTP server using hyper that runs within the Wasmer Edge WASM runtime. Supports JSON-RPC over HTTP with 14 MCP methods (tools, resources, prompts, elicitation, roots, completion, sampling), 10 built-in pure-Rust tools (echo, json_format, text_count, text_transform, math_eval, bench_echo, timestamp, base64_encode, base64_decode, hash_text), and 6 security layers (request size limits, CORS, API key auth, rate limiting, error sanitization, request logging).
- **velocity-mcp-core crate**: Extracted pure protocol logic into a WASM-compatible library crate (`crates/velocity-mcp-core/`) with zero OS-specific dependencies. Provides `handle_mcp_request()`, `parse_request()`, and `serialize_response()` for both native and WASM targets. Compiles as both `cdylib` and `rlib`.
- **One-click deployment scripts**: `deploy-edge.sh` (Linux/macOS) and `deploy-edge.bat` (Windows) automate the full deploy pipeline: environment validation, WASM build, binary size check (<5MB), test execution, deployment via `wasmer deploy`, and post-deploy health verification. Both scripts are idempotent and support `--skip-tests`, `--skip-build`, and `--dry-run` flags.
- **GitHub Actions deploy pipeline**: `.github/workflows/deploy-edge.yml` provides CI/CD for Edge deployments. Triggers on pushes to `main` when edge-related files change, and supports manual dispatch with environment selection (production/staging) and dry-run mode. Validates binary size, runs tests, deploys via Wasmer CLI, and verifies health check with retry logic.
- **Comprehensive Edge documentation**: `docs/edge_user_guide.md` (quick start, configuration reference, API reference, security configuration, performance tuning, troubleshooting) and `docs/edge_operations.md` (monitoring setup, alerting thresholds, scaling guidelines, backup/recovery, incident response playbook, log analysis patterns, cost management).
- **Production-optimized wasmer.toml**: Configured for free tier (128MB memory, 5M instruction limit, 0-3 instance scaling). Includes documented environment variables for API key authentication, request size limits, request timeouts, and health check configuration.
- **Metering middleware integration** (native only): WASM plugin execution on the native binary uses `wasmer-middlewares` metering to enforce per-request instruction limits, preventing runaway computations. On Edge, resource limits are enforced by the Wasmer Edge runtime itself via `wasmer.toml` configuration.
- **WASM module compilation caching** (native only): Compiled WASM modules are cached on the native binary to avoid redundant compilation on subsequent requests, providing ~20x faster cold starts for plugin-heavy workloads. Edge deployments rely on Wasmer Edge's built-in module caching.

### Changed

- **Repository structure**: Added `crates/velocity-mcp-edge/` and `crates/velocity-mcp-core/` workspace members. The edge crate depends only on the core crate plus minimal HTTP dependencies (hyper, tokio, serde_json) compatible with WASM targets.
- **README updated**: Added Edge deployment section with architecture diagram, feature comparison table (native vs edge), quick deploy instructions, and links to the user guide and operations runbook. Version badge updated to 3.2.0 with new "edge-ready" status badge.

### Known Limitations

The following features from the native binary are not available on Edge:
- NDA binary protocol and shared memory IPC (require OS-level shared memory)
- Process spawning via `shell_exec` (WASM sandbox prevents `fork`/`exec`)
- Local filesystem access beyond temporary storage
- Custom TCP/UDP sockets beyond WASI HTTP
- WebSocket transport
- SQLite database resources
- Linux seccomp sandboxing (replaced by WASM sandbox isolation)

### Migration from Previous Versions

No breaking changes for native binary users. The v3.2.0-edge release adds a new deployment target alongside the existing native binary. To deploy on Edge:

```bash
rustup run wasix rustc --version && cargo install cargo-wasix --locked
./deploy/deploy-edge.sh    # from the repository root; a wasm32-wasip1 target does not provide WASIX
```

Existing native deployments continue to work without modification. The `velocity-mcp-core` crate is a new workspace member that extracts protocol logic shared between native and Edge targets.

---

## [3.2.0] — 2026-09-14 — Binary Protocol & WASM Optimization

### Added

- **Toggleable NDA frame Merkle verification**: The per-frame SHA-256 Merkle root in NDA's 36-byte header is now configurable — enabled by default for integrity verification and audit provenance, and disableable for latency-sensitive deployments that trust their transport (e.g., same-machine shared memory). Configure via TOML `[features] nda_merkle = false` or `VELOCITY_NDA_MERKLE=0`. When disabled, frame builders write 32 zero bytes instead of hashing, validators skip the hash comparison (all other structure validation still applies), and audit entries omit the `merkle_root` field entirely. The NDA document Merkle tree (`nda_document.rs`) and all JSON transports are unaffected.
- **Prebuilt WASM module loading for the Go runtime**: The Go/TinyGo runtime now accepts a path to a pre-compiled `.wasm` module in the plugin manifest (`source` field), matching the Rust runtime's behavior, in addition to inline Go source compiled via TinyGo at load time. Per-call stores share the module's compilation engine, and each call gets a fresh instance with proper WASI imports (`_start` initialization, `malloc`-based input allocation, `(ptr<<32)|len` result ABI).
- **Binary argument protocol for WASM tools**: Eliminated double JSON serialization bottleneck across all 12 WASM runtimes by implementing TLV (Type-Length-Value) binary protocol. Added `call_tool_binary()` trait method with default JSON fallback for backwards compatibility. All 6 toy interpreters (PHP, C#, Java, R, Julia, Perl) share single ~230-line C TLV decoder in `interp_core/interp.c`, benefiting all simultaneously. Real engine runtimes (Lua, MicroPython, QuickJS/TypeScript) have custom TLV decoders (~150-200 lines each). Complete dispatch chain operational: `nmcp_binary.rs` → `registry::call_tool_binary()` → `plugins::call_wasm_tool_binary()` → `runtime.call_tool_binary()` → WASM module's `*_wasi_call_tool_binary()` → shared `interp_call_tool_binary()`. Comprehensive test suite (11 tests covering encoding, decoding, nested structures, arrays, floats, limits) all passing. Benchmark results show TLV roundtrip at 1.02 µs vs JSON at 856 ns — key benefit is eliminating interpreter-side `JSON.parse()` which dominates total tool call latency.
- **Criterion benchmark suite for binary protocol**: Added comprehensive benchmark measuring TLV encoding, decoding, round-trip performance, and size comparison against JSON. Benchmarks cover small payloads (2 fields), medium payloads (10 fields), and edge cases. Results documented in project memory.
- **WASM plugin executor**: Plugin system now supports WebAssembly-based tools with 12 language runtimes: JavaScript (QuickJS), TypeScript (QuickJS), Python (MicroPython), Ruby (mruby), Lua, Go (TinyGo), Rust (wasm32-wasi), PHP, C#, Java, R, Julia, Perl. Plugins use `executor_type: "wasm"` with a `language` field and inline `source` or `source_file`. Six runtimes (JS/TS/Py/Ruby/Lua/Go/Rust) execute manifest source with full argument passing; six runtimes (PHP/C#/Java/R/Julia/Perl) are minimal toy interpreters with hardcoded demo wrappers (documented honestly in manifests). E2E test verifies all 12 runtimes through the real stdio binary.

### Changed

- **Protocol response unification**: Extracted `build_initialize_response()` helper to eliminate 4-way duplication across JSON-RPC stdio, NDA stdio, shmem NDA, and shmem JSON handlers (~40 lines removed). Function accepts `include_extra` flag to control elicitation/roots capability inclusion based on transport mode.
- **NDA dispatch consolidation**: Unified NDA request handling by having `json_rpc::handle_nda_frame` delegate to `nmcp_binary::dispatch_nda_request`, eliminating ~90 lines of duplicated method dispatch logic (PING, INITIALIZE, TOOLS_LIST, TOOLS_CALL, etc.). Error frames properly constructed for parse failures instead of propagating Err.
- **WASM factory centralization**: Moved runtime creation logic from `src/plugins/mod.rs` to `wasm_runtime::create_wasm_runtime_for_language()`, providing single point of truth for all 12 language runtime instantiations. Reduced plugins/mod.rs factory from ~40 lines to 7-line wrapper.
- **Plugin module restructuring**: Began splitting 1529-line `src/plugins/mod.rs` into focused modules. Created `src/plugins/manifest.rs` with PluginManifest, PluginTool, PluginExecutor data structures as first extraction step.
- **WASM runtime classification update**: Documentation now accurately distinguishes 7 production-ready WASM runtimes (JavaScript/QuickJS, TypeScript, Python/MicroPython, Lua, Ruby/mruby, Rust/WASI, Go/TinyGo) from 6 planned runtimes currently implemented as toy interpreters (PHP, Perl, C#, R, Java, Julia). Historical changelog entries referencing "12 runtimes" are preserved as-is for accuracy of the historical record.

### Security

- **shell_exec command injection prevention**: Expanded dangerous command blocklist from 5 patterns to 31 patterns covering both Unix (17 patterns: `rm -rf /`, fork bombs, `dd if=`, `mkfs.`, pipe-to-shell variants) and Windows (14 patterns: `format`, `del /f /s /q`, `rd /s /q`, `diskpart`, `bcdedit`, `reg delete`, encoded PowerShell). All patterns checked cross-platform to prevent OS-detection bypass. Added shell metacharacter detection (`;`, `|`, `&`, `` ` ``, `$`, `\n`) with audit logging. All shell_exec invocations now emit `tracing::info!` audit trail entries.
- **SSRF blocklist expansion**: Extended `http_request` SSRF prevention from basic `127.0.0.1`/`localhost`/`10.`/`192.168.` blocking to comprehensive coverage: full RFC 1918 private ranges (all 16 `172.16.`–`172.31.` subnets, not just `172.16.`), link-local `169.254.`, full `127.0.0.0/8` loopback range, and IPv6 private ranges (`::1`, `[::1]`, `[::]`, `fe80:`, `fc00:`, `fd00:`).
- **HTTP authentication warning**: Server now emits `tracing::warn!` at startup when HTTP transport is enabled without API key authentication, alerting operators to the open-access configuration.
- **Silent error discard elimination**: Replaced `let _ = child.wait()` in sandbox timeout handler with proper error logging. Replaced `let _ = writeln!(..)` / `let _ = f.flush()` in NDA phase logger with `if let Err(e) = ...` + `tracing::debug!` logging. Replaced `let _ = tx.send(..)` in C# reader thread with debug logging on receiver disconnect. All error paths now produce diagnostic output.
- **shell_exec whitespace normalization**: Dangerous pattern matching now collapses all whitespace runs to single spaces before checking, preventing bypass via double spaces, tabs, or mixed whitespace (e.g., `rm  -rf /` or `rm\t-rf /`).
- **SSRF host-scoped checking**: SSRF blocklist now extracts the host portion of the URL before checking patterns, eliminating false positives where private IP substrings appear in URL paths or query strings (e.g., `https://example.com/page?version=10.5` no longer blocked by `10.` pattern).
- **edit_file resource bounds**: Maximum 1000 edits per request, maximum 1MB per oldText/newText field. Prevents resource exhaustion from maliciously large edit payloads.

### Fixed

- **Compiler warnings eliminated**: Zero-warning build achieved. Cfg-gated `Arc` import, `tls_cert`/`tls_key` declarations and `--tls-cert`/`--tls-key` argument parsing behind `#[cfg(feature = "http")]`. Added `#[allow(unused)]` for oauth2-cfg variables (`method`, `body`, `timeout_secs` in `http_request`). Removed unused `error` import from `src/plugins/marketplace.rs`. Added `#[allow(unused)]` for `addr` variable only consumed by http match arm.
- **Test expectations after NDA consolidation**: Updated `test_nda_frame_tools_call_error_path` and `test_nda_frame_parse_error` to match new behavior where `dispatch_nda_request` returns Ok(error_frame) instead of Err for parse failures.

### Performance (benchmarked 2026-09-02, release build, 500 iter × 3 rounds median)

All hardening changes have negligible performance impact — string-matching blocklist checks complete in nanoseconds, dominated by I/O costs. Unification refactorings maintain identical performance characteristics (zero overhead, pure code organization).

**NDA/shmem transport (primary path):**

| Method | Latency (avg) | Throughput | vs JSON/stdio |
|--------|--------------|------------|----------|
| ping | 0.002 ms (2µs) | 445,279 r/s | 7.8x faster |
| tools/list (17 tools) | 0.007 ms | 136,983 r/s | 27.7x faster |
| tools/call (64B) | 0.003 ms | 313,582 r/s | 7.3x faster |
| health/check | 0.002 ms | 471,904 r/s | 9.6x faster |

**Payload scaling (bench_echo):**

| Payload | NDA/shmem | JSON/stdio | Speedup |
|---------|-----------|------------|---------|
| 256 B | 0.001 ms | 0.026 ms | 18.1x |
| 1 KB | 0.002 ms | 0.027 ms | 12.1x |
| 4 KB | 0.004 ms | 0.029 ms | 7.2x |

**tools/list registry scaling:**

| Tools | NDA/shmem | JSON/shmem | Speedup |
|-------|-----------|------------|---------|
| 17 | 0.006 ms | 0.077 ms | 13.2x |
| 49 | 0.013 ms | 0.123 ms | 9.6x |
| 81 | 0.035 ms | 0.235 ms | 6.7x |
| 145 | 0.075 ms | 0.464 ms | 6.2x |

**Overall: 9.5x–27.7x faster across methods** (NDA/shmem vs JSON/stdio).

**Node.js vs Rust (JSON/stdio, fair comparison — same 16 tools, 500 iterations, median of 3 rounds):**

| Method | Node.js avg | Rust avg | Speedup |
|--------|------------|----------|---------|
| ping | 0.029 ms | 0.017 ms | 1.7x |
| tools/list | 0.080 ms | 0.202 ms | 0.4x* |
| tools/call | 0.029 ms | 0.023 ms | 1.3x |
| health/check | 0.030 ms | 0.020 ms | 1.5x |

*tools/list: Node.js returns a static array (pre-built constant), Rust dynamically assembles with cache checks + hashset dedup + pagination.

**4-Pipeline Comparison (benchmarked 2026-09-02, release build):**

Isolates service (Node.js vs Rust), tool format (JSON vs NDA), and transport (stdio vs shmem) differences.

| Pipeline | Ping avg | tools/list avg | tools/call avg |
|----------|----------|----------------|----------------|
| Node.js JSON/stdio | 0.029 ms | 0.080 ms | 0.029 ms |
| Rust JSON/stdio | 0.017 ms | 0.202 ms | 0.023 ms |
| Rust NDA/stdio | 0.025 ms | 0.164 ms | 0.032 ms |
| Rust NDA/shmem | 0.002 ms | 0.007 ms | 0.003 ms |

**Key findings:**

| Comparison | Ping | tools/list | tools/call | Conclusion |
|------------|------|------------|------------|------------|
| Service (Node.js vs Rust, same JSON/stdio) | 1.7x | 0.4x* | 1.3x | Marginal — Rust faster on ping/call, Node.js faster on tools/list (static array) |
| Transport (JSON/stdio vs NDA/shmem) | 8.5x | 28.9x | 7.7x | **Dominant factor** — shmem transport is order-of-magnitude faster |
| Full stack (Node.js JSON/stdio vs Rust NDA/shmem) | 14.5x | 11.4x | 9.7x | Combined effect of service + transport |

*tools/list: Node.js returns pre-built `const TOOLS` array; Rust dynamically assembles from 5 sources with cache validation.

Transport primitive costs on test machine:
- Win32 Event RTT (same process): 5.39µs (2.69µs one-way)
- Win32 Event RTT (cross-process): 5.28µs (2.64µs one-way)
- SHA-256 of 8KB (Merkle): 3.60µs (2.28 GB/s, SHA-NI accelerated)
- `flush_async` (FlushViewOfFile): 24.28µs

## [3.0.0] — 2026-08-28

### Added

- **NDA-native binary protocol**: Shared memory transport now supports NDA binary frames (`NMCP` magic + SHA-256 Merkle root + TLV-encoded payloads) as the native wire format. Zero JSON parsing on the hot path. Auto-detects NDA vs JSON frames for backwards compatibility.
- **MCP spec compliance**: `ping`, `logging/setLevel`, `notifications/cancelled`, cursor pagination on `tools/list`, `tools.listChanged` capability advertisement. Server now fully compliant with MCP protocol version 2024-11-05.
- **Win32 Event IPC**: `CreateEventW`/`WaitForSingleObject`/`SetEvent` for zero-poll blocking waits on Windows. Replaces 100μs polling sleep with instant event signaling. `Drop` impl for handle cleanup. Non-Windows platforms use 100μs sleep fallback.
- **NDA-native fuzz tests**: 7 new property-based tests (1,700+ cases): random payload resilience, Merkle tampering, TLV round-trip for arbitrary JSON, truncation safety, frame detection correctness, request/response round-trips.
- **Cancellation support**: Pre- and post-execution cancellation checks via `notifications/cancelled` tracking.

### Changed

- **Shared memory protocol**: Auto-detects NDA-native vs JSON-RPC frames by checking for `NMCP` magic bytes. Both formats supported simultaneously.
- **Initialize response**: Now advertises `tools.listChanged: true` and `logging` capability.
- **Test count**: 146 → 172 tests (128 unit + 27 integration + 17 fuzz).
- **Version**: 2.0.0 → 3.0.0.

### Security

- NDA-native frames verified with SHA-256 Merkle roots — any payload tampering detected.
- TLV decoder enforces depth limit (32), max string length (10 MB), max element count (100K).
- Cancellation tracker uses poisoning-tolerant mutex.

---

## [2.0.0] — 2026-08-18

### Added

- **Ed25519 NDA signatures**: Sign and verify NDA documents for authenticity and tamper detection. Backward-compatible with unsigned documents.
- **Capability-based sandbox**: Adapted from Velocity-IDE's TabSandbox. Restricted profile blocks network, isolates filesystem, controls interpreters. Violation tracking with categories.
- **Windows Job Object memory limits**: OS-level 256 MB memory cap for sandboxed processes via `extern "system"` FFI.
- **Merkle tree integrity verification**: SHA-256 root in NDA header, pair-wise hash verification of all triples.
- **Token bucket rate limiter**: 20 req/sec, burst 100. Prevents abuse across all tool calls.
- **Audit logging**: 10K entry ring buffer, poisoning-tolerant mutex, global instance. Records every tool execution.
- **Error sanitization**: Strips internal paths (Windows/Unix), truncates at 500 chars. Prevents information leakage.
- **Property-based fuzz testing (proptest)**: 10 properties, 2,250+ random cases per run. Covers NDA round-trips, random bytes, Merkle integrity, Ed25519 signatures, Unicode strings, sandbox cleanup.
- **Adversarial integration tests**: 15 tests covering XML parsing attacks, sandbox escape attempts, signature tampering, rate limiter burst, audit overflow, error sanitization, parser robustness.
- **GitHub Actions CI**: Automated build + test + cargo audit on every push/PR. Three jobs: build-and-test, security-audit, fuzz-tests.
- **Spec-compliant XML parsing**: Replaced regex-based XML extraction with quick-xml for XLSX/DOCX parsing. Handles namespaces, entities, malformed XML safely.

### Changed

- **Architecture**: Migrated from C# delegation to fully native Rust NDA operations. All compile/read/execute runs in-process.
- **Test count**: 46 → 146 tests (109 unit + 27 integration + 10 fuzz).
- **Security model**: 12 defense layers, all active and tested.
- **Dependencies**: Added quick-xml 0.41, ed25519-dalek 2, rand 0.8, proptest 1 (dev).
- **Documentation**: README.md rewritten with security layers, test breakdown, CI docs. USER_GUIDE.md expanded with comprehensive security model section.

### Security

- NDA parser: bounds checking on all header fields, string pool offsets, triple/command counts.
- Execution: 30s timeout, 1 MB stdout cap, 256 KB stderr cap, 256 MB memory limit.
- Sandbox: capability-based access control, violation recording, temp dir isolation with cleanup.
- Signatures: Ed25519 sign/verify, backward-compatible signature section.
- Dependencies: 0 vulnerabilities across 95 crates (cargo audit clean).

---

## [1.0.0] — 2026-08-17

### Added

- Initial release: high-performance MCP server in Rust.
- **Dual-protocol support**: Stdio JSON-RPC v2.0 and Shared Memory IPC.
- **Four built-in tools**: convert_to_nda_document, convert_to_nda_tool, read_nda, execute_nda.
- **NDA binary format**: 52-byte header, semantic triples, display commands, string pool, Merkle tree.
- **File format support**: CSV, XLSX, DOCX, PDF, Images (PNG/JPG/WebP), 20+ source code languages.
- **Dynamic tool hosting**: Auto-discovery of C# backend tools, merged with built-in NDA tools.
- **Graceful shutdown**: Ctrl+C handler with atomic shutdown flag.
- **Health checks**: `health/check` JSON-RPC method.
- **Structured logging**: tracing + tracing-subscriber with env-filter.
- **Path validation**: Rejects empty, relative, and traversal paths.
- **46 tests**: 34 unit + 12 integration.
